<meta>This is the title page of the 1614 edition of the Heidelberg Catechism, featuring both Greek and Latin versions of the title. Friedrich Sylburg was a notable scholar of the Renaissance, and this edition reflects the humanist interest in translating key theological texts into classical Greek.</meta>

# THE CATECHISM
# OF THE CHRISTIAN
# FAITH,
<note>original Greek: ΚΑΤΗΧΗΣΙΣ ΤΗΣ ΧΡΙΣΤΙΑΝΙΚΗΣ ΠΙΣΤΕΩΣ. A catechism is a summary of religious principles used for instruction.</note>

->JUST AS IT<-

->is delivered in the churches<-
->and schools of the Electoral Palatinate.<-
<note>The "Electoral Palatinate" was a significant territory of the Holy Roman Empire, and its capital, Heidelberg, was a major center for the Protestant Reformation.</note>

->BY<-

->FRIEDRICH SYL-<-
->BURGIUS of Wetterau, translated into the<-
->language of the Greeks.<-
<note>Friedrich Sylburg (1536–1596) was one of the most distinguished classical scholars of his time. He translated the Heidelberg Catechism into Greek to demonstrate the compatibility of Reformed theology with classical learning.</note>

# CATECHISM
# OF THE CHRISTIAN
# RELIGION,
<note>original Latin: CATECHESIS RELIGIONIS CHRISTIANÆ</note>

->Which is taught in the Churches & Schools of the<-
->Electoral Palatinate;<-

->**BY FRIEDRICH SYLBURGIUS OF WETTE-**<-
->**RAU, TRANSLATED INTO GREEK.**<-

->Decorative floral design<-

->**HANAU** Printed by the Heirs of Guillaume Antony, 1614.<-
->*With the Privilege of the Electoral Palatinate.*<-
<note>In the 17th century, a "Privilege" acted as an early form of copyright, granting the printer exclusive rights to publish the work within a specific territory.</note>

<summary>The title page of a 1614 edition of the Heidelberg Catechism, translated into Greek by the scholar Friedrich Sylburg and published in Hanau. It presents the title in both Greek and Latin, emphasizing its use in the churches and schools of the Palatinate region.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the formal preface addressed to Frederick IV, Elector Palatine. It discusses the scholarly process behind Friedrich Sylburg's Greek translation of the Heidelberg Catechism, specifically mentioning the esteemed theologians who reviewed and approved the work before its publication.</meta>

# PREFACE

*which is publicly taught in the Churches and Schools of Your Most Illustrious Highness, [Sylburg] wished to illuminate with a Greek Metaphrase <note>original: "Metaphrasi" – a translation or stylistic transformation of a text into another language or form, in this case, Greek.</note>. He did this following the example of others who spent pious and useful labor in the same field, and by the authority of men most distinguished for their piety and erudition. To their censorship and judgment, he wished to subject this work—which he had carefully crafted and completed—before it was released to the public. These men also performed no small service in this regard, as can be seen from their own notes which I found in the author’s library. Among others, these men were:* **Dr. CASPAR OLEVIANUS** <note>Caspar Olevianus (1536–1587) was a significant German Reformed theologian and one of the primary contributors to the Heidelberg Catechism.</note>, **Dr. FRANCISCUS JUNIUS**, **Mr. JOHANNES PISCATOR**, and **Mr. SIMON STENIUS**; *all of whom were Theologians and distinguished scholars of Greek literature. They have partially earned the highest merit, and are partially still diligently serving, especially within the Palatine Churches and Schools. Nor was this pious undertaking lacking the support of the Reverend* **Dr. DAVID PAREUS** <note>David Pareus (1548–1622) was a prominent Reformed Protestant theologian and professor at Heidelberg.</note>, *the Overseer <term>Antistes: a high-ranking cleric or superintendent</term> of the Churches and of Your Highness’s College, which they call the* **COLLEGE OF WISDOM** <note>original: "SAPIENTIÆ" – The Sapienz-Collegium in Heidelberg was a famous seminary for training Reformed ministers.</note>; *who*

# PREFACE

*—intendent <note>original: "Antistes" – this continues the word from the previous page, referring to David Pareus, the superintendent of the College of Wisdom.</note>; who, by completing the task cut short by the author’s premature death and by comparing the notes of the aforementioned scholars more precisely, applied the finishing touches <note>original: "extremam manum" – literally 'the final hand,' a Latin idiom for the final stage of polishing a work</note> to this work. How much everyone publicly, and each individual privately, owes to these men, no one—unless they are extremely ungrateful—fails to acknowledge. My own role in this labor was nothing more than this: I was unwilling to any longer deny the public a work intended for the press by the author himself and long desired by many most excellent men; and I labored to ensure that it was published accurately, faithful to his own manuscript <note>original: "autographi"</note>, and in a suitable typeface <note>original: "typoformaque"</note>.*

*Indeed, to You, Most Illustrious and Most Clement Prince, I decided this pious work should be offered—a work owed to Your Highness <note>original: "Cels. Tuæ" (Celsitudini Tuae)</note> for many reasons. I discovered among the author’s other papers that he himself intended this, no doubt mindful of the greatest favors which he received previously under the Prince of most pleasant memory, your most praised Grandfather,* **FREDERICK III** <note>Frederick III (1515–1576), Elector Palatine, who commissioned the original Heidelberg Catechism.</note>, *while he served as a teacher <note>original: "præceptorem"</note> of the illustrious School of Neuhausen <note>original: "Neohusianæ" – a reformed Latin school near Worms where Sylburg taught</note>; and even—*

*...which is publicly taught in the churches and schools of Your Most Illustrious Highness, [the author] wished to clarify with a Greek Paraphrase <note>original: "Metaphrasi" – a translation or restatement in another language, here referring to the Greek version of the Catechism</note>. He did this following the example of others who have devoted pious and useful labor to this same field, and by the authority of men most distinguished for their piety and learning. He wished to submit this work—which he had refined and completed himself—to their review and judgment before it was published. These men performed no small service <note>original: "non pœnitendam... operam" – literally 'not-to-be-regretted work,' meaning commendable effort</note> in this regard, as can be seen from their own notes, which I found in the author’s library. Among others, these were:* **Dr. CASPAR OLEVIANUS** <note>1536–1587; a central figure in the German Reformation and one of the primary authors of the original Heidelberg Catechism.</note>: **Dr. FRANCISCUS JUNIUS** <note>1545–1602; a French-born Reformed theologian and influential professor at Heidelberg.</note>: **Master JOHANNES PISCATOR** <note>1546–1625; a prolific German theologian known for his translation of the Bible and his logical commentaries.</note>: **Master SIMON STENIUS** <note>1540–1619; a humanist scholar, poet, and rector of the Paedagogium in Heidelberg.</note>, *Theologians and distinguished scholars of Greek literature, who have partly served the Palatine <note>Referring to the Electoral Palatinate, the region of Germany centered at Heidelberg.</note> churches and schools especially well, and are partly still diligently deserving of praise. Nor was the Reverend* **Dr. DAVID PAREUS** <note>1548–1622; a key Reformed theologian who eventually oversaw the publication of Sylburg's works.</note>, *of the churches and of Your Highness’s college—which they call the College of* **WISDOM** <note>The "Collegium Sapientiae" in Heidelberg, a seminary established to train Reformed clergy.</note>, *opposed to this pious undertaking, serving as super-* <note>The text breaks here; the original Latin "Anti-" completes as "Antistes" on the next page, meaning a leader or superintendent.</note>

*...likewise afterwards, under Your Highness <note>original: "sub Celsit. Tua" — shorthand for Celsitudo, a formal title for a Prince.</note>, while performing outstanding labors in the Electoral Printing Office <note>The official press of the Elector Palatine in Heidelberg.</note> with the highest testimony of good men, he received [benefits]; to him the care of the Academic Library had already been committed in that same place, and he would have been employed for even greater things, had he not prematurely departed from the living.*

*Although I know this was not the only reason the author had, Your Highness may rightfully claim this* **CATECHISM** *for yourself, and whatever may be added to it, by a sort of hereditary right. For Your Highness wishes it—having been drawn from the clearest fountain of Holy Scripture by that most prudent Prince of most blessed memory, your grandfather* **FREDERICK III** <note>Frederick III "the Pious" (1515–1576), who commissioned the Heidelberg Catechism in 1563.</note>—*to be publicly set forth and diligently studied in your churches and schools with the most pious zeal, following that most praised ancestral example, and indeed by the mandate of God himself.*

## Mark, Chapter 8. <note>original Greek: "Μάρκου τῷ ηʹ κεφαλαίῳ"</note>

For whoever is ashamed of me and my words in 
this adulterous and sinful generation, 
the Son of Man will also be ashamed of him 
when he comes in the glory of his Father with 
the holy angels.

### Mark 8. <note>original Latin: "Marci 8."</note>

*Whoever is ashamed of me and my words, in this*
*adulterous and sinful generation: of this one shall*
*the Son of Man also be ashamed, when he*
*comes in the glory of his Father, with the holy Angels.*

# FREDERICK,

->by the grace of God, Count Palatine on the Rhine,<-
->Duke of Bavaria, Elector of the Holy Roman Empire,<- <note>original Greek: "ἀρχαιρεσιαστὴς" (archairesiastēs), a term for the Prince-Electors who chose the Emperor.</note>
->to all those entrusted with the churches or schools in the<-
->Palatinate, greetings and<-
->prosperity.<- <note>original Greek: "εὐπράττειν" (euprattein), a standard greeting in Greek letters wishing the recipient both good health and right action.</note>

---

<image-desc>Decorative floral drop cap letter E inside a square frame with vine patterns.</image-desc>

Since we have learned those things which are fitting for us, both from the divine oracles <note>original: "θείων λογίων" (theiōn logiōn), meaning the Holy Scriptures.</note> and from nature itself; and since it is necessary for us to obey God—who commanded these things to us—above all else; we have decided to pursue nothing in this life so much as the fulfillment of our conscience and the provision, as far as is attainable for us, for the salvation of those under our rule. For we do not consider it sufficient to administer justice <note>original: "θέμισας" (themisas), referring to divine law or the administration of right.</note> in such a way that the people entrusted to us might live quietly and without disturbance, practicing works of justice and virtue <note>original: "καλοκἀγαθίας" (kalokagathias), the classical Greek ideal of "gentlemanly" excellence or moral nobility.</note>, unless they are also led into the true [knowledge] of both our Creator and Savior... <note>The sentence is interrupted by the page break; the catchword suggests "knowledge" is the next word.</note>

<summary>This page contains the opening of a formal decree by Frederick III, Elector Palatine, addressing the religious and educational leaders of his territory. He expresses his divine duty to ensure not just the civil order and justice of his subjects, but their spiritual salvation and knowledge of God.</summary>

<keywords>Frederick III, Elector Palatine, Holy Roman Empire, Reformation, Church governance, salvation, divine law, Palatinate, preface</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the opening scriptural citations into the formal preface written by Frederick III (1515–1576), Elector Palatine. Frederick was the primary patron of the Heidelberg Catechism, and here he establishes his legal and moral authority to reform the churches and schools within his territory. The catchword "verbo" from the previous page leads directly into his explanation of his duties as defined by the "Word of God."</meta>

<sig>A 2</sig>

<image-desc>A horizontal woodcut ornament featuring a symmetrical design of scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

# FREDERICK, BY THE GRACE OF GOD, COUNT PALATINE OF THE RHINE, AND ELECTOR OF THE HOLY ROMAN EMPIRE,

## Duke of Bavaria, etc., to all those to whom the care of the Churches and Schools in the Palatinate has been entrusted,

->Sends Greetings.<- <note>original Latin: "S. D.", abbreviation for "Salutem Dicit," a standard formal greeting meaning "He says health/greetings."</note>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter "P" set within a square frame featuring foliate scrolls and a light background.</image-desc>

Since we have understood what our duty is, both from the word of **GOD** and from nature itself, and that in this matter we must especially obey **God the Best and Greatest** <note>original: "DEO Opt. Max.", short for "Deo Optimo Maximo," a title adopted from Roman antiquity to signify the supreme nature of the Christian God.</note> who imposed this duty upon us: we have determined that there ought to be nothing for which we labor so much in our whole life as to satisfy our own conscience and to provide, as much as lies within us, for the salvation of our people.

However, we did not consider it enough for us to administer justice with such counsel and reason that the people committed to our trust might live peacefully and quietly, cultivating right and honor, unless they were also led to the point that they might [know] **GOD** their creator and redeemer from... <note>The sentence continues onto the next page, shifting from civil governance to the necessity of religious instruction.</note>

<summary>In this preface, Frederick III, Elector Palatine, asserts that his responsibility as a ruler extends beyond maintaining civil peace and justice to ensuring the spiritual well-being and salvation of his subjects.</summary>
<keywords>Frederick III, Elector Palatine, Palatinate, divine right, governance, religious reform, Heidelberg Catechism, salvation, Holy Roman Empire</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Frederick III, Elector Palatine. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the need for the people to know God. This page begins with the completion of that thought, emphasizing that knowledge of God is the foundation of all virtue and explaining Frederick's motivation for reforming the schools and churches.</meta>

<page-num>1</page-num>

...be led by the hand toward knowledge <note>original Greek: γνῶσιν χειραγωγῶνται (gnōsin cheiragōgōntai). The Greek implies a "pedagogical" leading, like a teacher guiding a child by the hand.</note>. (For this knowledge is the foundation of the other virtues, and especially of obedience and genuine piety toward **God**.) We have therefore endeavored with all diligence to deliberate upon these matters, so as to leave out nothing that contributes to the acquisition and preservation of this well-being. 

Much, indeed, has already been piously and profitably ordained by those who held this leadership before us—our most dear kinsmen <note>A reference to Frederick's predecessors in the Palatinate, such as his cousin Otto Henry (Ottheinrich), who began the Reformation in the region.</note> (whose memory we hold in reverence and honor)—partly to magnify the glory of **God**, and partly to instruct the people. Nevertheless, as we have learned through experience at the beginning of our administration, the explanation and practice of these things was not pursued with as much zeal as was appropriate to the greatness of the matters. Therefore, it is no wonder if the fruits were less than expected. 

From these circumstances, then, we were moved to recall and restore what was well and properly ordained by them; but as for those things that were weak or had simply become corrupt and inconsistent, we aimed to strengthen the former and correct the latter. The schools were lying in neglect, the youth were being disregarded, and not even an accurate [knowledge] of the Christian religion... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note>

<meta>catchword: καὶ (and)</meta>

<summary>Frederick III explains that while his predecessors attempted religious reforms, the results were insufficient due to a lack of diligent practice and education. He announces his intent to reform the schools and restore proper Christian instruction to the youth of the Palatinate.</summary>

<keywords>Frederick III, Elector Palatine, religious reform, education, piety, Palatinate, predecessors, Christian instruction</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Frederick III, Elector Palatine. It picks up from the previous page's discussion on the necessity of knowing God, shifting focus to Frederick's personal commitment to reform and his critique of how previous decrees were handled by administrators.</meta>

# PREFACE

A 3

...might rightly know Him through His word; (for this is the sole and firm foundation of both the other virtues and, most especially, of all obedience and true piety toward **God**.) We have therefore begun to meditate upon this matter with our whole heart, desiring to omit nothing that might serve to both achieve and maintain such blessedness. 

And although those who preceded us—our kinsmen, the **Palatine Electors** <note>The Electors of the Palatinate were high-ranking princes of the Holy Roman Empire who held the prestigious right to elect the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> (whose memory we recall with love and reverence)—had piously and usefully instituted various measures to illuminate the glory of **God** and to keep the people in their duty; nevertheless, as we ourselves have experienced from the very start of our administration, the diligence required to carry out these measures and adapt them for the public good was not applied as much as such a great matter deserved. Therefore, it is hardly surprising if the fruits that were hoped for could not be harvested. 

Moved by these circumstances, we sought not only to recall and restore what they had rightly established, but also to strengthen what was weak and to improve and correct what had become corrupt or distorted. The **Schools** were lying in neglect, the tender youth were being ignored, and there was no certain or fixed instruction in the **Christian religion**...

<meta>catchword: and con-</meta>

<summary>Frederick III explains that while his predecessors attempted religious reforms, a lack of administrative diligence hindered their success. He expresses his intent to restore and correct the educational and religious institutions of the Palatinate, noting that schools and youth were being neglected.</summary>

<keywords>Frederick III, Elector Palatine, religious reform, education, piety, Palatinate, Christian religion, school reform</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Frederick III, Elector Palatine. While the previous page established Frederick's desire to improve upon the work of his predecessors, this page identifies the specific failure of current education: the lack of a standardized, clear system for teaching the foundations of faith to the youth.</meta>

# PREFACE

...and consistent <term>elementary instruction</term> <note>original: στοιχείωσις (stoicheiōsis), referring here to the systematic teaching of the basic principles of faith.</note>. Because of this, the youth were educated either poorly or according to rules that were not precise, but rather according to whatever seemed best to each individual; at times they were not instructed at all, but were left ignorant and untrained. From this, countless other evils arose, and especially the fact that those who had never tasted <term>pure religion</term> <note>The Elector uses "pure" or "unadulterated" (ἀκίβδηλος) to distinguish his Reformed faith from what he considered the "corruptions" of Roman Catholicism or the inconsistencies of other sects.</note> grew to adulthood in the deepest ignorance—either having wandered into various errors or having been burdened with useless and curious inquiries.

Looking upon these misfortunes, we recognized the very heart of the matter: namely, that no church, nor state, nor even a single household can be established, nor can <term>moral excellence</term> <note>original: καλοκἀγαθία (kalokagathia), a classical Greek term for the ideal of being both "beautiful and good," implying a noble and virtuous character.</note> and good order be introduced and maintained, unless the ignorance of the young is removed <term>from their earliest years</term> <note>original: ἀφ' ἁπαλῶν ὀνύχων (aph’ hapalōn onychōn), a Greek idiom literally meaning "from tender nails," signifying the very beginning of childhood.</note> through a uniform tradition of the elements of the true and stainless religion, and unless they are continually exercised in the study of these things. 

Therefore, so that we might fulfill our duties in this area above all others, and bring the necessary remedy to these evils as quickly as possible with all the power we possess, we have commanded our theologians and those entrusted with the high oversight of the churches in our domain to prepare a **Catechism** <note>This refers to the commissioning of the Heidelberg Catechism (1563), a foundational document for the Reformed tradition.</note> of the **Christian religion**, drawn from the divine oracles, in both the **German**...

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>Frederick III argues that a lack of standardized religious education leads to social and spiritual decay, necessitating a uniform Catechism to train the youth from childhood. He announces that he has commissioned his theologians to produce this work in the German language.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the preface by Frederick III, Elector Palatine, detailing the specific consequences of the educational neglect mentioned on the previous page and announcing his official commission of a new, standardized catechism.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...and consistent <term>instruction</term> <note>Latin: *institutio*. This refers to the systematic training or "institution" of the young in the foundations of faith.</note>. Consequently, the youth were being educated either poorly or according to no fixed standard, but rather according to the whim of each teacher; or they were not being taught at all, but were left entirely unformed and unrefined. From this, while countless other evils arose, this one stood out: that having never been taught true religion, or being entangled in various errors, or burdened with unnecessary questions, they grew up unhappily in the deepest ignorance. 

When we observed such great calamities and considered the reality—namely, that neither the Church, nor the <term>Commonwealth</term> <note>Latin: *Respublica*. In this context, it refers to the civil state or the "common good" of the Palatinate.</note>, nor even individual families can be established, nor can there be any place for integrity or discipline among citizens, unless the unformed youth are instructed from their earliest years with one and the same voice in true and sincere religion, and are constantly practiced in it: the necessity of the situation compelled us to remedy this evil as quickly as possible by whatever means we could. We did this so that, in this very important part of our office, we would not be found wanting in our required duty. 

Therefore, we have commissioned our <term>Theologians</term> <note>These included Zacharias Ursinus and Caspar Olevianus, who are traditionally credited as the primary authors of the resulting Heidelberg Catechism.</note> and those to whom the primary care of the Churches in our domain is entrusted, to prepare a Catechism of the Christian religion, based on the Word of GOD, in Germa-

<meta>This page continues the preface by Frederick III, Elector Palatine. The previous page ended with his decision to commission theologians to prepare a standardized manual of faith. This page explains the purpose of the dual-language (Greek and Latin) edition and issues a formal charge to the teachers and ministers of the Palatinate.</meta>

# PREFACE.

...and to compose it in the Latin tongue; so that in the future, when those who teach in the churches and the schools carry out their duties, they might have a certain fixed and defined <term>Form</term> <note>Greek: *diatyposis*. This refers to a systematic outline or pattern of doctrine that ensures consistency across different locations.</note> according to which they may educate the youth. This is intended to ensure that they do not frequently introduce novelties based on their own private opinions, or pass down teachings that do not agree with the word of God.

The task remains yours hereafter: looking on the one hand toward the glory of God, and on the other toward the salvation of both yourselves and those souls entrusted to each of us, you must gratefully receive this <term>Catechism</term> <note>Greek: *Katechesis*. A method of oral instruction, often in Q&A format, used to ground new believers and children in the faith.</note>. You must continually instill it with all diligence into the ears and souls of the young, in the churches as well as the schools, so that they may become accustomed to providing a clear demonstration of its truths—not only in their thoughts, but also in their manner of life and their works. 

If you do this, as both faithfulness and the stewardship given to you by God require, there is good hope that the all-good God—having had mercy on us through Christ—will relax the punishments due to us. We trust that by His fatherly kindness, He will prosper your labors and bring benefit to the whole country. Just as we continue to plead for this with all our soul, so we also ask you to further confirm and increase the hope we have regarding your service.

# CATECHISM OF THE
->Christian religion.<-

<margin>1.</margin> WHAT is your only comfort <note>original: *παραμυθία* (paramythia). In this context, it implies a deep, theological solace or strength that sustains a person through suffering and the fear of death.</note>, both in life and in death?

That I, with body and soul, both in life and in death, am not my own, but belong to my most faithful Savior, the Lord Jesus Christ; who, having paid for all my sins with his precious blood as a sufficient <term>ransom</term> <note>original: *λύτρον* (lytron). A price paid to buy back a slave or captive; here it refers to Christ's sacrifice freeing humanity from the debt of sin.</note>, has delivered me from all the power of the devil; and so preserves me that without the will of my Father in heaven, not a hair can fall from my head, but rather all things must work together for my <term>salvation</term> <note>original: *σωτηρίαν* (sōtērian). The state of being saved from harm or divine judgment, leading to eternal life.</note>. Therefore, by his Holy Spirit, he also assures me of eternal life, and makes me heartily willing and ready, from now on, to live for him.

<margin>2.</margin> How many things are necessary for you to know, so that in this comfort you may be able to live and die blessedly?

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the "First Part: Concerning the Misery of Man" from the previous page. It transitions from Question 5, regarding the human inability to keep the Law, into Questions 6 through 9, which explore the origin of human sinfulness and the nature of God's justice.</meta>

By no means; <note>original: "Οὐδαμῶς." This corresponds to the Latin "Minimè" from the previous page's catchword.</note> for I am by nature prone to hate both God and my neighbor.

<margin>6.</margin> Did God then create man so wicked and perverse?

By no means; but God created man good, and in His own image—that is, in true righteousness and holiness—so that he might rightly know God his Creator, love Him from the heart, and live with Him in eternal blessedness, to praise and glorify Him. <note>The "Image of God" refers to a spiritual resemblance in character, specifically in the qualities of justice and purity, rather than a physical likeness.</note>

<margin>7.</margin> From where then does this depravity of human nature come?

From the fall and disobedience of our first parents, Adam and Eve, which happened in Paradise long ago; by which our nature became so corrupted that we are all conceived and born in sin. <note>This refers to the doctrine of "Original Sin," the idea that the inclination toward wrongdoing is an inherited part of the human condition from birth.</note>

<margin>8.</margin> But are we so corrupted that we are totally unable to do any good, and are inclined toward all evil?

Yes; unless we are born again by the Spirit of God. <note>To be "born again" describes a spiritual renewal or transformation that the text suggests is necessary to overcome a person's natural state.</note>

<margin>9.</margin> Does God then do man an injustice, when He demands of him in His law what he is unable to perform?

<summary>This page explains that human sinfulness is not God's original design but resulted from the Fall of Adam and Eve, leaving humanity spiritually incapacitated without divine renewal. It establishes the tension between God's perfect Law and human inability to fulfill it.</summary>
<keywords>Image of God, Fall of Man, Adam and Eve, Original Sin, Depravity, New Birth, Divine Justice, Law of God</keywords>

->apostasy unpunished?<-

God is indeed merciful; but He is also just; therefore His justice requires that sin, which is committed against the supreme majesty of God, be punished with the extreme—that 
->is, the eternal punishment of both<-
->body and soul<-
->be the penalty.<-

# THE SECOND PART.

->Concerning the redemption of<-
->man.<-

**12. Since, according to the righteous judgment of God, we are subject to temporal and eternal punishments, how might we escape these punishments and be restored to grace?**
God demands that satisfaction <term>satisfaction</term> <note>original: τὸ ἱκανὸν ποιεῖσθαι; a legal term meaning to fulfill an obligation or pay a debt in full to satisfy a claim of justice.</note> be made to His justice; therefore, it is absolutely necessary that this be done either by ourselves or by another.

**13. Are we then able to make satisfaction by ourselves?**
Not at all; rather, we even increase our debts <note>original: τὰ ὀφειλήματα; refers to our moral and spiritual failures viewed as unpaid debts to God.</note> every day.

**14. But can any mere creature <note>original: ψιλή τις κτίσις; "ψιλή" means "bare" or "mere," emphasizing that a created being like an animal or angel lacks the necessary nature to pay a human debt.</note> among those in heaven or on earth pay what is owed on our behalf?**
None. For first, God does not deem it right to punish in any other creature the sins committed by man. Furthermore, no creature can sustain the weight of the wrath of God against sin and unburden <note>original: ἀποφορτίσαι; literally to "offload" or discharge a heavy cargo.</note> others of this load.

**15. What kind of mediator and redeemer must we then seek?**

<vocab>redemption <note>original: ἀπολύτρωσις</note>, justice <note>original: δικαιοσύνη</note>, punishment <note>original: τιμωρία</note>, creature <note>original: κτίσις</note>, mediator <note>original: μεσίτης</note>, redeemer <note>original: λυτρωτής</note>, wrath of God <note>original: ὀργὴ Θεοῦ</note></vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the necessity of a redeemer, explaining that neither humans nor mere creatures can satisfy God's justice or endure the weight of His wrath. It sets the stage for identifying Jesus Christ as the required mediator.</summary>
<keywords>redemption, satisfaction, justice, wrath of God, mediator, debt of sin, creature vs creator</keywords>

# THE SECOND PART

<margin>5.</margin>
## CONCERNING THE DELIVERANCE OF MAN.

**12. Since, therefore, by the righteous judgment of God, we are liable to temporal and eternal punishments: is there any remaining way or means by which we may be delivered from these punishments and be reconciled to God?**

<margin>
a Gen. 3:17;
Exod. 23:7;
Ezek. 18:4;
Matt. 5:26;
2 Thess. 1:6;
Luke 16:2.
b Rom. 8:3.
</margin>
*God wills that His justice be satisfied*<sup>a</sup> <term>satisfied</term> <note>original: "satisfieri"; in a legal sense, this refers to paying a debt or penalty in full so that justice no longer has a claim against the debtor.</note>: *wherefore it is necessary that we make satisfaction either through ourselves or through another*<sup>b</sup>.

**13. Are we able to make satisfaction ourselves?**

<margin>
c Job 9:2 & 15:15-16 & 4:18;
Ps. 130:3;
Matt. 6:12 & 18:25 & 16:26.
</margin>
*Not in any part: on the contrary, we even increase our debt every single day*<sup>c</sup>.

**14. Can any creature in heaven or on earth, which is merely a creature, make satisfaction for us?**

<margin>
d Ezek. 18:4;
Gen. 3:17.
e Nah. 1:6;
Ps. 130:3.
</margin>
*None: For, in the first place, God does not will that the sin which man committed should be punished in any other creature*<sup>d</sup>. *Furthermore, no mere creature can sustain the wrath of God against sin and deliver others from it*<sup>e</sup>.

**15. What kind of mediator and deliverer must we then seek?**

<vocab>human deliverance <note>Latin: "liberatio hominis"</note>, satisfaction <note>Latin: "satisfactio"; a legal payment of a debt</note>, mediator <note>Latin: "mediator"; a go-between</note>, punishment <note>Latin: "pœna"</note>, justice of God <note>Latin: "justitia Dei"</note>, sin <note>Latin: "peccatum"</note></vocab>

<summary>This page marks the beginning of the Catechism's second section, explaining that since humans cannot pay their own spiritual debt to God's justice, a mediator is required. It establishes that neither humans nor "mere creatures" (like animals or angels) are capable of bearing God's judgment on behalf of humanity.</summary>
<keywords>redemption, satisfaction, divine justice, mediator, original sin, debt, punishment</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the Greek translation of the Heidelberg Catechism. It picks up from the answer to Question 15, which defines the necessary qualities of a Mediator, and continues through Question 19. The previous page (page 10) provided the Latin equivalent for the preceding questions. The catchword "Εκ" (Ek) at the bottom indicates the beginning of the first word on the following page.</meta>

One who is at once a true and righteous man, and yet stronger than all creatures; that is, one who is at the same time also true God.

16. Why must he be a true and 
->righteous man?<-

Because the justice <term>justice</term> <note>original: δικαιοσύνη (dikaiosynē); in this theological context, it refers to God's requirement that the law be satisfied and sin be punished.</note> of God demands that the human nature which committed the sin should also pay the penalty for that sin. However, a person who is himself guilty of sins would not be able to pay the penalty on behalf of others.

17. Why is it also necessary for him to be 
->true God?<-

So that, by the power of his divinity, he might be able to sustain <note>original: ὑποστῆναι (hypostēnai); to stand under or endure the weight of something.</note> the weight of God's wrath in his humanity, and might be able to recover and restore to us the righteousness and life that we had lost.

18. Who is this mediator <term>mediator</term> <note>original: μεσίτης (mesitēs); an intermediary or "go-between" who reconciles two parties—in this case, God and humanity.</note>, who is at once true God and a true and righteous
->man?<-

Our Lord Jesus Christ, who became for us wisdom from God, as well as righteousness, sanctification <term>sanctification</term> <note>original: ἁγιασμὸς (hagiasmos); the process of being made holy or set apart.</note>, and redemption.

19. From where is this known to you?

<vocab>righteousness, sin, God, Jesus Christ, mediator, redemption, sanctification</vocab>

From the holy gospel <term>gospel</term> <note>original: εὐαγγελίου (euangeliou); literally "good news."</note>, which God himself first revealed in paradise; then he advanced it through the holy patriarchs and prophets, and foreshadowed it through the sacrifices and other ceremonies of the law; and finally, he fulfilled it through his only-begotten Son.

20. Are all people then, just as they fell into destruction through Adam, likewise
-> obtain salvation through Christ? <-
-> <-

Not all, but only those who through a true faith are engrafted <term>engrafted</term> <note>original: ἐμφυτευόμενοι (emphyteuomenoi); a botanical metaphor, common in the New Testament, describing a believer being joined to Christ like a branch to a vine.</note> into him, and receive all his benefits.

-> 21. What is true faith? <-

It is not only a firm conviction <term>conviction</term> <note>original: ὑπόληψις (hypolēpsis); an intellectual acceptance or assent to the truth of God's Word.</note>, by which I firmly assent to all things decreed by God in his word as completely true; but also a firm confidence <term>confidence</term> <note>original: πεποίθησις (pepoithēsis); a deep, personal trust or "heart-knowledge" that goes beyond mere intellectual agreement.</note> in my heart, kindled by the Holy Spirit through the voice of the gospel; by which I am fully assured that not to others only, but also to me, forgiveness of sins, eternal righteousness, and salvation have been granted by God, and these by his grace alone, solely for the sake of Christ's ransom <term>ransom</term> <note>original: λύτρον (lytron); the price paid to provide freedom or redemption.</note>.

21 What is true faith?

*It is not only a certain knowledge <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "notitia"; an intellectual understanding or acquaintance with facts.</note> by which I firmly assent to all things that God has revealed to us in his word^h, but also a certain trust <term>trust</term> <note>original: "fiducia"; a heart-level reliance or confidence.</note>^i, kindled in my heart by the Holy Spirit^k through the Gospel^l, by which I rest in God, being surely convinced that not only to others, but to me also, forgiveness of sins, eternal righteousness, and life have been granted^m—and that freely, out of God's mercy, solely for the sake of the merit of Christ^n.*

15.

### 22. What is it then that a Christian must
### believe?

Everything that is promised to us in the Gospel, which our universal and undoubted Christian <term>Creed</term> <note>original: σύμβολον (*symbolon*); a "symbol" or "token" used in the early church as a summary of core beliefs to identify true believers.</note> expresses for us in brief headings.

### 23. What then are the words of this
### Creed?

I believe in God the Father Almighty <term>Almighty</term> <note>original: παντοκράτορα (*pantokratora*); literally "the one who holds all things" or "ruler of all."</note>, maker of heaven and earth. And in Jesus Christ, his only begotten Son, our Lord; who was conceived by the Holy Spirit, born of the Virgin Mary; suffered under Pontius Pilate, was crucified, died, and was buried; he descended into <term>hell</term> <note>original: ᾅδȣ (*hadou*); referring to Hades, the realm of the dead.</note>; on the third day he rose again from the dead; he ascended into heaven, and sits at the right hand of God the Father Almighty; from there he shall come to judge the living and the dead. I believe in the Holy Spirit; I believe in the holy universal <term>universal</term> <note>original: καθολικὴν (*katholiken*); the root of the word "Catholic," meaning the church as a whole, existing throughout all times and places.</note> church; the communion of saints; the forgiveness of sins; the resurrection of the flesh; and life everlasting. Amen.

### 24. Into how many parts is this Creed
### divided?

<meta>catchword: Three.</meta>

### 22 What are those things which it is necessary for a Christian to believe?

*All things which are promised to us in the Gospel <note>original: "Euangelio"; from the Greek meaning "Good News."</note>, the summary of which is briefly contained in the Apostles' Creed <note>original: "Symbolo Apostolico"; a "symbolum" was a sign or token, used here as a standard summary of faith to distinguish true believers from others.</note>, or in the articles of the universal and undisputed faith of all Christians.*

### 23 What is that Creed?
> I believe in God the Father Almighty, creator of heaven and earth: and in Jesus Christ his only begotten Son, our Lord: who was conceived by the Holy Spirit, born of the virgin Mary: suffered under Pontius Pilate, was crucified, dead, and buried, he descended into <term>hell</term> <note>original: "inferna"; referring to the place of the dead.</note>: on the third day he rose again from the dead: he ascended into heaven, and sits at the right hand of God the Father Almighty: from there he shall come to judge the living and the dead. I believe in the Holy Spirit: I believe in the holy <term>universal</term> <note>original: "catholicam"; meaning the whole or "according to the whole," referring to the church across all times and places.</note> church, the communion of saints: the forgiveness of sins: the resurrection of the <term>flesh</term> <note>original: "carnis"; the physical body.</note>: and life everlasting. Amen.

24 ->In how many parts is this Creed divided?<-

<vocab>Apostles' Creed, I believe, Gospel, faith, Church, resurrection</vocab>

Three. The first, concerning God the Father and our creation. The second, concerning God the Son and our redemption. The third, concerning God the Holy Spirit and our sanctification.

### 25 Why then do you name three—the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit—when there is only one
->essence <note>original: "ousia"; referring to the single, undivided nature of God.</note> of the Godhead?<-

Because God has so revealed himself in his Word <note>original: "rhēmati"; the spoken or written revelation</note>, that these three distinct persons <term>hypostases</term> <note>original: "hypostaseis"; a technical term for the individual "subsistences" or persons within the one God.</note> are the one, true, and eternal God.

### CONCERNING GOD THE FATHER.

### 26 What do you believe when you say: "I believe in God the Father, Almighty, Creator of heaven and earth"?

I believe that the eternal Father of our Lord Jesus Christ—who created heaven and earth and all that is in them out of nothing, and still sustains and governs them by his eternal counsel and providence—is, for the sake of his Son Jesus Christ, my God and my Father. Therefore I have such confidence in him and so trust him, that I do not doubt he will provide me with everything necessary for my soul and <insert>body</insert> <note>The text breaks here at "soma-", completed by the catchword "tikas" to form "somatikas" (physical/bodily).</note>

...physical needs as are necessary, he will fatherly provide; and furthermore, all the evils that meet me in this troublesome life by his providence, he will cause to result in my salvation; since he is able to do these things at once as the almighty God, and also wills to do them as a kind father. <note>The text emphasizes that God's power (almighty) and His character (kind father) work together to ensure the believer's welfare.</note>

27. What do you understand by the providence of God?

The almighty and everywhere present power of God, through which he preserves heaven and earth, with all creatures, as if by his hand, and so governs them, that herbs and grass, rain and drought, fruitful and unfruitful years, food and drink, health and sickness, riches and poverty, and in a word, all things come to us not by chance <note>original: "αὐτομάτως" (automatos); literally "of its own accord" or "by accident."</note>, but from his fatherly counsel and will.

28. What benefit does the knowledge of creation and providence provide us?

That we may be patient in adversity <term>duspraxia</term> <note>From the Greek "δυσπραγία," meaning ill-fortune or difficult circumstances.</note>, and thankful in prosperity <term>eupraxia</term> <note>From the Greek "εὐπραγία," meaning good-fortune or success.</note>, and for the future have a firm confidence in our most faithful God and Father, knowing well that nothing at all can separate us from the love of God; since he so manages all creatures by his hand, that without his will not even the least thing can be moved.

<gloss>God, providence, creation, prosperity, adversity, almighty</gloss>

# ON GOD THE SON

### 29. Why is the Son of God called Jesus, which means Savior? <note>original: "Σωτήρ" (Sōtēr). The name Jesus is derived from the Hebrew "Yeshua," meaning "Yahweh saves."</note>

Because he saves us from our sins; and salvation is not in any other, nor should it be sought or found elsewhere.

### 30. Do those, then, who seek their salvation and happiness in "saints," or in themselves, or anywhere else, truly believe in Jesus as our only Savior?

By no means; rather, though they boast of Jesus as the only Savior in words, in their actions they deny him. For one of two things must be true: either Jesus is not a perfect Savior, or those who receive this Savior in true faith must find in him everything necessary for their salvation. <note>This question reflects a classic Reformation-era argument, asserting that looking to other sources for spiritual security diminishes the "completeness" of Christ's work.</note>

### 31. Why is he called Christ? <note>original: "χριστὸς" (Christos). This is the Greek translation of the Hebrew "Messiah," meaning "Anointed One."</note>

Because he was appointed by God the Father and anointed with the Holy Spirit to be our chief prophet and teacher—who has fully explained to us the secret counsel of the Father concerning our redemption—and to be our only high priest, who by the <term>sacrifice of his own body</term> <note>Referring to the crucifixion as a singular, definitive ritual act.</note> once...

<meta>catchword: ...offered (pro-)</meta>

<vocab>Jesus, Savior, salvation, Christ, God the Father, Holy Spirit, prophet, teacher, redemption, high priest</vocab>

<summary>This page explains the theological significance of the names 'Jesus' and 'Christ,' emphasizing Jesus as the sole and perfect source of salvation and his roles as Prophet and High Priest.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, Savior, Salvation, Atonement, Prophet, High Priest, Holy Spirit, Redemption</keywords>

**32. Why are you called a Christian?**

Because I am a member of Christ through faith, and therefore share in his anointing <note>original: "χρίσματος" (chrismatos). **Son, seeing that we also**

We, however, have received the grace of adoption <note>original: "υἱοθεσίαν" (huiothesian). This refers to the legal and spiritual act of being brought into God's family through Christ.</note> from God because of him.

<page-num>27.</page-num>
<meta>catchword: The dea-</meta>

...might deliver us from folly <note>original: μωρείας (mōreias). In this context, it refers to the spiritual corruption or "madness" of sin.</note>, and might acquire for us the grace of God, **righteousness** <term>Righteousness (dikaiosynē): A legal and moral standing of being "right" or justified before God.</term>, and eternal life.

**38. Why did he suffer under Pontius Pilate?**

That he, though innocent, might be condemned before a worldly judge, so that he might deliver us from the inescapable condemnation of God that was destined to be brought upon us all.

**39. Is there anything more significant in his being crucified than if he had suffered some other kind of death?**

Certainly. For by this death <note>original: πτώματι (ptōmati), literally "fall" or "downfall," used here to denote his sacrificial death.</note> it is evident that he took the **curse** <term>Curse: The divine penalty for breaking God's law; according to ancient scripture, dying on a "tree" or cross was a sign of being cursed.</term> that lay upon me onto himself; for death upon the cross was cursed.

**40. Why was it necessary for him to die?**

Because, according to the justice and truth of God, satisfaction for our **sin** <term>Sin (hamartia): Missing the mark or failing to meet God's standard.</term> could not be achieved in any other way than by the death of his Son.

**41. Why was he buried?**

So that by this evidence it might be made manifest that he had truly died.

**42. Since Christ died for us, why must we also die?**

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the exposition of the Apostles' Creed, transitioning from the legal necessity of Christ’s death to the personal and spiritual benefits his sacrifice provides to the believer. It specifically addresses the "mortification" of sin and the theological meaning behind the "descent into hell" and the Resurrection.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<meta>catchword: How</meta>

Our death is not a **ransom** <term>Ransom: Translated from the Greek 'antilutron' (ἀντίλυτρον), referring to a price paid to redeem someone from debt or captivity.</term> for our sin, but only a putting to death of sin, and a transition into eternal life.

**43. What further benefit does the offering and death of Christ upon the cross bring to us?**

That by his power our **old man** <term>Old Man: A biblical concept (from Romans 6:6) referring to the sinful nature or the person one was before being spiritually renewed.</term> is crucified, killed, and buried with him; so that fleshly desires may no longer reign in us, but that we may present ourselves as a living and pleasing sacrifice to him.

**44. Why is it added: "He descended into hell"?**

So that in my greatest temptations and pains, I may be assured that my Lord Jesus, through the inexpressible **agony** <term>Agony: Referring here to the intense spiritual and mental suffering (the 'pains of hell') Christ endured in the Garden of Gethsemane and on the Cross.</term>, pains, and terrors, which he endured in his soul both previously and especially on the cross, has delivered me from the agony and punishment of hell. <note>In this tradition, Christ’s 'descent into hell' is often interpreted as the spiritual suffering he felt while on the cross, rather than a physical journey to a subterranean realm.</note>

**45. How does the resurrection of Christ benefit us?**

First, by his resurrection he has overcome death, so that he might share with us the **righteousness** <term>Righteousness: The legal standing of being innocent and 'right' before God, which the text argues Christ earned for believers.</term> obtained through his own death. Second, we also are now raised up by his power to a new life. Third, the resurrection of Christ is a sure **pledge** <term>Pledge: Translated from 'enechyron' (ἐνέχυρον), a legal or financial term for a security deposit that guarantees a future transaction.</term> to us of our own resurrection to a blessed life.

<vocab>death, sin, life, Christ, cross, hell/Hades, agony, resurrection, righteousness, pledge</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that while human death cannot pay for sin, Christ's death and resurrection empower believers to overcome their sinful nature and provide a guarantee of their own future eternal life. It also offers a psychological interpretation of Christ’s "descent into hell" as his extreme suffering on the cross.</summary>
<keywords>Apostles' Creed, Mortification of Sin, Descent into Hell, Resurrection, Righteousness, Assurance, Atonement</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catechism's examination of the Apostles' Creed, moving from the Resurrection to the Ascension and the dual nature of Christ (the "Hypostatic Union"). It follows the discussion of Christ's victory over death and hell.</meta>

<page-num>31.</page-num>

46. How do you understand his **ascension** <term>Ascension: The departure of Christ from Earth into the heavenly realm, forty days after his Resurrection.</term> into the heavens?

*That Christ, while the disciples were watching, was lifted up from the earth and was taken up into heaven; and for our benefit he remains there until he shall return to judge the living and the dead.*

47. Is Christ then not with us until the **end of the age** <note>original: "συντελείας τοῦ αἰῶνος" (synteleias tou aionos)</note>, just as he promised?

*Christ is both true man and true God. According to his human nature, he is not now on earth; but according to his divinity, majesty, grace, and Spirit, he is never absent from us.*

48. Are the two natures in Christ not pulled apart in this way, if the humanity is not wherever the divinity is?

*By no means; for since the divinity is beyond all limit <note>original: "ἀπερίληπτός" (aperileptos); meaning infinite or unable to be contained in one space.</note> and is present everywhere, it follows of necessity that it exists beyond the humanity it assumed, but nonetheless is also within it and remains **personally** <term>Personally: Greek "ὑποστατικῶς" (hypostatically), referring to the theological concept of the "Hypostatic Union," where the divine and human natures are joined in one individual person without mixing.</term> united to it.*

49. What benefits does the ascension of Christ bring to us?

49. <note>The question corresponding to this answer is: "What benefit do we receive from Christ’s ascension into heaven?"</note> First, that he intercedes for us in heaven before the Father. Second, that we have our own flesh in heaven as a reliable pledge that he, as our Head, will in time take us, his members, up to himself. Third, that he sends the Holy Spirit to us as a down payment <note>original: "arrhabōn" (ἀρραβῶνα); a legal or commercial term for a deposit that guarantees the full amount will be paid later.</note>; by whose power we seek not earthly things, but the things above, where Christ is seated at the right hand of God.

50. Why is it added that **he sits at the right hand of the Father**?

Because Christ ascended to heaven for this purpose: to reveal himself from there as the Head of the Christian church, through whom the Father governs all things.

51. What benefit does this majesty of our Head, Jesus Christ, provide for us?

First, that through the Holy Spirit he pours out heavenly gifts upon us, his members. Second, that by his power he guards and preserves us against all our enemies.

52. What comfort does Christ's return **to judge the living and the dead** bring to you?

That in all my afflictions and persecutions, I may lift up my head <note>An idiom meaning to look up with confidence and hope rather than shame or fear.</note> and expect this very same person as my Judge from heaven—the one who previously stood before God's judgment in my place and removed all the curse <note>Referring to the "curse of the law" mentioned in Galatians 3:13, which believers hold was taken by Christ during the crucifixion.</note> from me.

<keywords>Ascension, Holy Spirit, Intercession, Head of the Church, Right Hand of God, Final Judgment, Assurance</keywords>

## CONCERNING GOD THE HOLY
### Spirit.

<margin>53.</margin> What do you believe concerning the Holy Spirit?

First, that he is true and co-eternal <note>original: "συναΐδιος" (synaidios); existing together for all eternity</note> God with the Father and the Son. Second, that he has also been given to me, so that he makes me a partaker of Christ and all his benefits through true faith, and remains with me forever.

<margin>54.</margin> What do you believe concerning the holy catholic <note>The term "catholic" is used here in its original sense of "universal" or "all-encompassing," rather than referring to a specific denomination.</note> Church of Christ?

That the Son of God, out of the entire human race, gathers and protects for himself a church chosen for eternal life, through his Spirit and Word, in the unity of the true faith, from the foundation of the world until the end of the ages; and that I am a living member of this church, and will remain so forever.

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the exposition of the Apostles' Creed, specifically the articles concerning the Church and the benefits of salvation: the communion of saints, the forgiveness of sins, and the resurrection of the body.</meta>

37

55. ->What is meant by "the communion of saints"?<-

First, that all believers, collectively and individually, as members, share in <note>original: "κοινωνοῦσιν"; to participate in or have a common share in something</note> the Lord Jesus and all his treasures and gifts. Second, that it is the duty of each person to contribute the gifts given to them for the benefit and salvation of the other members, doing so eagerly and gladly.

56. ->What do you believe regarding the forgiveness of sins?<-

That God, because of the most sufficient satisfaction <note>original: "ἀπότισιν"; a full payment or compensation that settles a debt</note> made by Christ, will never remember any of my sins, nor even the innate wickedness <note>A reference to original sin, the inherent tendency toward wrongdoing believed to exist in humans from birth.</note> against which I must struggle throughout my whole life; instead, he graciously grants me the righteousness of Christ as a gift, so that I may not come into judgment.

57. ->What comfort does the "resurrection of the flesh" bring to you?<-

That not only my soul, immediately after it is released from this mortal body, shall be taken up to Christ its head, but also this very flesh of mine, raised up by the power of Christ and joined once again to my soul, shall be made like <note>original: "σύμμορφος"; having the same form or nature</note> the glorious body of Christ.

58. ->What comfort do you enjoy from believing in "eternal life"?<-

That, because I now feel the beginning of eternal life in my heart, after this life, I shall receive the complete possession of blessedness; which eye has not seen, nor ear heard, nor has it entered into the heart of man <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 2:9, describing the inconceivable nature of heaven</note>; so that in this church I may continue glorifying God with my fellow-heirs forever.

### 59. What do you gain further from
->believing<-
all these things?

That in Christ I am righteous before God, and have become an heir of eternal life.

### 60. How are you righteous before God?

Only by true faith in Jesus Christ; so that even if my conscience accuses me, claiming that I have grievously sinned against all God's commandments, and not only that I have never kept any of them, but also that I have always been inclined toward all evil; God—without any merit of my own works, but by his grace alone—grants and credits to me the perfect satisfaction <note>original: "ἀπότισιν" (apotisin); in a legal sense, this refers to the full payment of a debt or penalty</note>, righteousness, and holiness of Christ. It is as if I had never committed or had any sin, but rather as if I myself had perfectly fulfilled the obedience which Christ performed on my behalf—if only I accept these divine favors with a steadfast faith.

<note>Vocabulary from the original text: righteousness (δικαιοσύνη), faith (πίστις), Christ (Χριστός), grace (χάρις), satisfaction/payment (ἀπότισις), eternal life (αἰώνιος ζωή)</note>

<summary>This page concludes the explanation of "life everlasting" and introduces the core Protestant doctrine of justification, explaining that believers are considered righteous before God solely through faith in Christ's work rather than their own merit.</summary>

61. Why do you say that you are righteous by faith alone?

Not that I please God by the worthiness <note>original: "ἀξίᾳ" (axia) - meaning merit or value</note> of my faith, but because only the satisfaction <term>ἀπότισις - a Greek rendering of the Latin "satisfactio," referring to Christ paying the debt of sin in full</term> of Christ, and his righteousness and holiness, is my righteousness before God; and I can receive this and make it my own in no other way than through faith alone.

<margin>
Gal. 3. 10.
Deut. 27. 26.
Esai. 64. 6.
Phil. 3. 9.
Jac. 2. 10.
</margin>
62. Why can our good works not be considered either righteousness, or even a part of righteousness, before God?

Because the righteousness which is to stand before the judgment of God must be perfect through and through, and must harmonize with the divine law in all things; but even the best of our works in this life are imperfect and defiled by sins.

<margin>
Luc. 17. 10.
Matt. 5. 12.
2. Tim. 4. 8.
Hebr. 11. 6.
</margin>
63. How is it that no reward is owed to our good works, when God promises to give them a reward both in the present and in the future life?

This reward is not given as a debt <note>original: "ὀφείλημα" - something legally owed</note>, but according to grace.

<margin>
Matt. 7. 18.
Luc. 6. 43.
Joh. 15. 5.
Rom. 6. 1. 2.
</margin>
64. Does this teaching not make people indifferent <note>original: "ὀλιγώρους" - literally "caring little" or negligent</note> and ungodly?

By no means; for it is impossible for those who are grafted into Christ through true faith not to bring forth fruits of gratitude <note>original: "εὐχαριστίας" (eucharistias) - thankfulness or thanksgiving</note>.

66. What then are the sacraments?
They are visible and holy symbols and seals <note>original: "sphragismata" — referring to an official seal used to authenticate a document</note>, ordained by God for this purpose: that by their use, the promises of the Gospel may be more clearly shown to our minds, and as it were, sealed; namely, that God grants the forgiveness of sins and eternal life as a free gift, not only to the church in general but also to each individual believer, because of the <term>offering of Christ</term> <note>original: "prosphoran" — the sacrificial death of Jesus</note> which was offered once for all upon the cross.

Gal. 3:27.
</margin>

Yes; for the Holy Spirit teaches in the Gospel and reassures <note>original: "plerophorei" — to bring full assurance or conviction</note> through the holy sacraments...

## CONCERNING HOLY BAPTISM

69. How does baptism remind and assure you that you share in the offering of Christ, which was performed once for all on the cross?

Because Christ, having ordained this external washing with water <note>original: ὑδατολουσίαν (hydatolousian)</note>, promised that just as I am washed on the outside by water—which by its nature washes away the impurities of the body—in the same way, by His blood and Spirit, every stain of my soul (that is, all my sins) has been cleansed.

70. What does it mean to be washed by the blood and Spirit of Christ?

It signifies both receiving the forgiveness of sins as a gift from God because of the blood of Christ shed for us in the offering on the cross; and being renewed by the Holy Spirit and sanctified to be a precious member of Christ—

*It is not* ᶠ: *For only the blood of Jesus Christ and the Holy Spirit cleanse us from all sin* ᵍ.

73. Why then does the Holy Spirit call Baptism the "washing of regeneration" and the "washing away of sins"?

<margin>
ʰ Revelation 1:5 & 7:14. 1 Corinthians 6:11.
</margin>
*God speaks this way not without a significant cause; namely, not only to teach us that just as the filth of the body is cleansed by water, so our sins are expiated* <note>original: expiari; to be purified or atoned for</note> *by the blood and Spirit of Christ* ʰ: *But much more, so that He may make us certain by this divine symbol and pledge* <note>original: pignore; a guarantee or token of a promise</note> *that we are no less...*

<summary>This page explains that while the water of baptism is a physical sign, the true cleansing of sins comes only from the blood of Christ and the Holy Spirit. It cites the Great Commission and other New Testament passages to establish baptism as a divine pledge of spiritual renewal.</summary>
<keywords>Baptism, blood of Christ, Holy Spirit, washing of regeneration, purification, sacraments, Heidelberg Catechism, Great Commission</keywords>

74. But should infants also be baptized?
Yes, indeed; for since they belong to the <term>covenant</term> <note>A formal, spiritual agreement between God and His people</note> of God and to His church just as much as adults do, and since the forgiveness of sins through the blood of Christ and the Holy Spirit—who works faith in the heart—is promised to them no less than to adults, they must also be incorporated into the Christian church through baptism, which is the symbol of the covenant. They are to be distinguished from the children of unbelievers, just as happened in the Old Testament through circumcision, in whose place baptism was established in the New Testament.

### 76. What does it mean to eat the crucified body of Christ and to drink his shed blood?

It is not only to embrace with a firm faith the entire death and sufferings of Christ, and thereby to share in the forgiveness of sins and eternal life; but also, in addition to these, through the **Holy Spirit**—who lives at once in Christ and in us—to be so united to his blessed body every day that, even though he is in heaven above and we are on the earth below, we are nevertheless flesh of his flesh and bone of his bones <note>A biblical reference to the intimate union described in Genesis 2:23 and Ephesians 5:30</note>; and just as the members of the body are governed by one and the same soul, so we also are brought to life and governed by one and the same Spirit.

<summary>This page explains the spiritual significance of the Lord's Supper, asserting that the bread and wine are reliable signs of Christ's sacrifice. It defines the "eating" of Christ's body as a spiritual union through faith and the Holy Spirit, despite Christ being in heaven.</summary>
<keywords>Lord's Supper, Eucharist, Holy Spirit, Communion, remembrance, deacon, spiritual union, faith, Heidelberg Catechism</keywords>

77. How does Christ promise that he will so truly feed and give drink to believers with his own body and blood, as they eat of the broken bread and drink of the cup?

In the institution of the Holy Supper, with these very words a:

> The Lord Jesus, in the night in which he was betrayed, took bread; and after giving thanks, he broke it and said: "Take, eat; this is my body, which is broken for you; do this in my remembrance." This directly answers the previous page's question: "Do the bread and wine then become the very body and blood of Christ?"</note> But just as the water in baptism is not changed into the blood of Christ, nor does it become the forgiveness of sins itself—of which it is only a divine symbol and seal—so also the holy bread in the Lord’s Supper does not become the actual body of Christ; although, according to the nature of the sacraments <note>original: μυστηρίων (mysterion), literally "mysteries," the term used in the Greek tradition for sacraments</note> and the way the Holy Spirit usually speaks about these things, the bread is called the body of Christ.

79. Why then does Christ call the bread his body, and the cup his blood, or the New Covenant in his blood; and Saint Paul, the communion <note>original: κοινωνίαν (koinonian), meaning a shared participation or fellowship</note> of the body and blood of Christ?

Christ does not speak this way without an important reason. For he wants to teach us by this phrasing not only that just as bread and wine nourish this temporary life of the body, so also his crucified body and poured-out blood are the true food of our soul for eternal life; but even more, he wants to assure us by this visible symbol and pledge <note>original: ἐνεχύρῳ (enechyro), a legal or financial term for a "guarantee" or "security"</note> that we partake of his true body and blood through the working of the Holy Spirit as truly as we receive these holy symbols with the mouth of our body in his remembrance; furthermore the

**80. How does the Papal ritual, called the Mass, differ from the Lord’s Supper?**
<note>original: "Μίσα" (Misa). The Greek transliteration of the Latin "Missa," referring to the Roman Catholic Mass.</note>

The Lord’s Supper <note>original: "κυριακὸν δεῖπνον" (kyriakon deipnon). A common term for the Eucharist or Holy Communion in Reformed theology.</note> testifies to us that we have a complete forgiveness of all our sins because of the unique sacrifice of Christ, which he himself completed once for all upon the cross; and that through the Holy Spirit we are incorporated into Christ, who now, according to his human nature, resides only in heaven at the right hand of the Father, and wishes to be worshiped there. But in the Mass it is denied that the living and the dead receive the forgiveness of sins through the sufferings of Christ unless Christ is also offered daily by the priests; furthermore, it is asserted that Christ remains bodily under the form <note>original: "σχήματι" (schemati), referring to the outward appearance or "accidents" of the bread and wine.</note> of bread and wine, and for this reason ought to be worshiped in them. Therefore, this service is nothing other than a denial of the sacrifice and sufferings of Jesus Christ, who suffered once for all on the cross, and because of this, it is an abominable idolatry. <note>This strong condemnation of the Mass as "idolatry" was a later addition to the Heidelberg Catechism (added in the second and third editions) to reflect the intensifying theological conflicts of the 16th century.</note>

**81. Who is permitted to partake of the Lord’s Supper?**

<meta>catchword: As many as</meta>

<summary>This page concludes Question 79 and provides Question 80 of the Heidelberg Catechism, defining the Lord's Supper as a spiritual remembrance of Christ’s one-time sacrifice while denouncing the Roman Catholic Mass as idolatrous for its claim of a daily bodily sacrifice.</summary>
<keywords>Heidelberg Catechism, Lord's Supper, Mass, Idolatry, Sacrifice of Christ, Forgiveness of Sins, Holy Spirit, Right hand of the Father</keywords>

### 80. What is the difference between the Lord’s Supper and the Papal Mass?

<margin>
a Hebrews 10:10, 12; 7:26; 9:12, 25. John 19:30. Matthew 26:28. Luke 22:19.
b 1 Corinthians 10:13, 16; 6:17.
c Colossians 3:1. Hebrews 1:3; 8:1.
d Matthew 6:20, 21. John 4:21; 20:17. Luke 24:52. Acts 7:55. Colossians 3:1. Philippians 3:20. 1 Thessalonians 1:9.
</margin>
The Lord’s Supper <note>original: "Cœna Domini"</note> testifies to us that we have a perfect remission of all our sins because of that unique sacrifice of Christ, which he himself performed once for all on the cross<sup>a</sup>: and also that we, through the Holy Spirit, are grafted into Christ<sup>b</sup>, who now, according to his human nature, is only in heaven at the right hand of the Father<sup>c</sup>, and wishes to be worshiped by us there<sup>d</sup>.

<margin>
e In the canon of the Mass and regarding consecration, distinction 2. Council of Trent, session 13, canon 5.
f Hebrews 9:26; 10:12, 14.
</margin>
In the Mass, however, it is denied that the living and the dead have remission of sins because of the unique passion of Christ, unless Christ is still daily offered for them by the Mass-priests <note>original: "Sacrificulis" – a derogatory Latin term used by Reformers for priests, implying they performed unnecessary sacrifices.</note>: and it is also taught that Christ is bodily present under the forms <note>original: "sub speciebus" – a technical term referring to the outward appearance of bread and wine.</note> of bread and wine, and is therefore to be worshiped in them<sup>e</sup>. And thus the very foundation of the Mass is nothing other than a denial of that unique sacrifice and passion of Jesus Christ, and an accursed idolatry<sup>f</sup>. <note>The condemnation of the Mass as "accursed idolatry" was added to the Catechism in its second and third editions by order of Elector Frederick III, responding to the decrees of the Council of Trent.</note>

59.
<meta>catchword: as often as</meta>

Those who are displeased with themselves because of their sins, yet trust that these are forgiven them for Christ’s sake, and that the remaining weakness of their nature is covered by the suffering and death of Christ; and who also desire more and more to strengthen their faith and to lead a better life. But the unrepentant and hypocrites eat and drink judgment <note>original: "κρίμα" - This refers to the biblical warning in 1 Corinthians 11:29 about taking the sacrament in an unworthy manner.</note> to themselves.

**82.** But should those also be admitted who, by their confession and their life, show themselves to be unbelieving and ungodly?
->being so shown<-

No. For in this way the covenant <term>covenant: a formal, sacred agreement between God and His people</term> of God is profaned, and His wrath is provoked against the whole church. Therefore the church, according to the command of Christ and the Apostles, using the keys of the heavens, ought to exclude such people from the Lord’s table until they repent and amend their ways.

**83.** What then are the keys of the heavens?
The preaching of the holy gospel, and church discipline <note>original: "παιδαγωγία" - literally "pedagogy" or training, referring here to the corrective discipline of the church community.</note>. For by both of these, the kingdom of heaven is opened to believers and closed to unbelievers.

**84.** How then is the kingdom of the heavens opened
->and closed<-
through the preaching of the gospel?

When, according to the command of Christ, it is proclaimed to all believers and to each one individually,

It defines the "Keys of the Kingdom" as the preaching of the Gospel and church discipline, which together open or close heaven to individuals.</summary>

83. What are the keys of the kingdom of heaven? XXXI <note>The Roman numeral XXXI marks the 31st "Lord's Day," a weekly division used for teaching the catechism throughout the year.</note>.

ᶜ Matthew 18:18; & 16:18; John 20:21.

*The preaching of the Gospel and ecclesiastical discipline: by which heaven is opened to believers but closed to unbelievers ᶜ.*

84. How is the kingdom of heaven opened and closed by the preaching of the Gospel?

*When, by the command of Christ, it is publicly announced to all and each of the believers, th-* <note>The text cuts off here at the start of the word "omnia" (everything/all), which serves as the catchword for the next page.</note>

<meta>This page continues the Greek translation of the Heidelberg Catechism, finishing the answer to Question 84 regarding the preaching of the Gospel and beginning Question 85 regarding church discipline. It maintains the theological focus on the "Keys of the Kingdom."</meta>

61.

...whenever they receive the promises of the Gospel with true faith, all their sins are completely forgiven by God on account of the merit of Christ; but to the unbelieving and hypocrites, the opposite is proclaimed—that the wrath of God and eternal punishments rest upon them as long as they do not repent. For according to this testimony of the Gospel, God will execute judgment both in this life and in the life to come.

## 85. How is the kingdom of heaven opened and closed by church discipline? <note>original: "παιδαγωγίας" (paedagogia), literally meaning "child-leading" or "instructional guidance"</note>

When, according to the command of Christ, those who claim to be Christians in name but show themselves by their teaching or their life to be strangers to the faith, and having been warned several times with brotherly admonitions, neglect to turn away from their error or wickedness, they are reported to the church, or to those appointed by the church for this purpose; and if they do not respect even their admonition, they are excluded by them from the Christian fellowship by being forbidden from the holy mysteries <note>original: "μυστηρίων" (mysteriōn), referring to the sacraments, specifically the Lord's Supper</note>, and are excluded by God Himself from the church of Christ; and conversely, if they promise repentance in words and demonstrate it in deeds, they are received again as members of Christ and the church.

<meta>catchword: Τ Ο</meta>

<keywords>Gospel promises, forgiveness of sins, church discipline, excommunication, repentance, holy mysteries, sacraments, Heidelberg Catechism</keywords>

### THE THIRD PART.

86. concerning gratitude <note>original: "εὐχαριστίας" (eucharistias). In this theological context, it refers to the life of thankfulness and service that follows salvation, rather than the ritual of the Eucharist.</note>.

Since we have been delivered from all sin and misery, without any merit of our own, by God’s grace through Christ, why must we also do good works?

Because Christ, after having redeemed us with his own blood, also renews us by the Holy Spirit into his own image, so that through our whole life, we may show ourselves thankful to God for these benefits, and he may be glorified through us. Furthermore, so that we may each receive assurance within ourselves concerning our own faith from its fruits. In addition to these, so that by a godly <term>conduct</term> <note>original: "ἀναστροφῆς" (anastrophēs), referring to one's entire manner of life or behavior</note> we may win our neighbors for Christ.

87. Can those then be saved who do not turn to God from an ungrateful and unrepentant life?

By no means. For, as the Scripture testifies, neither the unchaste, nor idolaters, nor adulterers, nor thieves, nor the greedy, nor drunkards, nor slanderers, nor robbers, shall inherit the kingdom of God.

<vocab>gratitude, Christ, sin, faith, godly conduct, kingdom of God</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the "Third Part" of the catechism, explaining that good works are the necessary expression of a believer's gratitude for salvation and a sign of the Holy Spirit's renewal. It concludes with a warning that a life of persistent, unrepentant immorality is incompatible with inheriting the kingdom of God.</summary>

<keywords>gratitude, good works, sanctification, Holy Spirit, assurance of faith, morality, repentance, Kingdom of God</keywords>

86. Since we have been delivered from all our sins and miseries, without any merit of our own, by God’s mercy alone through Christ, why must we then do good works?

Further, so that we ourselves may be certain of our own faith by its fruits ^d^. Finally, so that by the integrity of our life we may win over others to Christ ^e^.*
