## THE THIRD PART.

### 86. ON GRATITUDE.

Since we have been delivered from all sin and misery, without any merit of our own, by the grace of God through Christ, why then must we do good works?

Because Christ, after having redeemed us with his own blood, also renews us by the Holy Spirit into his own image, so that throughout our whole life, we may show ourselves thankful to God for these benefits <note>The Greek term *eucharistia* here refers to the broader concept of "thankfulness" or "gratitude" as a way of life, rather than the specific ritual of the Lord's Supper.</note>, and he himself may be glorified through us. Furthermore, so that each of us may receive assurance of our own faith from its fruits. Besides this, so that through a godly way of life, we may also win our neighbors for Christ.

### 87. Can those then be saved who, from an ungrateful
->and unrepentant life toward God<-
->or do not return?<-

By no means. For, as the Scripture testifies, neither the impure, nor idolaters, nor adulterers, nor thieves, nor the greedy, nor drunkards, nor <term>slanderers</term> <note>original: "loidoroi," meaning those who use abusive or insulting language to damage others' reputations.</note>, nor robbers, shall inherit the kingdom of God.

<meta>catchword: How many</meta>

88. How many parts are there to true repentance, or
->conversion?<-
<note>original: ἐπιστροφῆς (epistrophēs), literally a "turning back" or a return to a original state.</note>

Two: the <term>dying <note>original: νέκρωσις (nekrōsis), often rendered in older English as "mortification."</note></term> of the old self, and the <term>coming to life <note>original: ζωοποίησις (zōopoiēsis), often rendered as "vivification."</note></term> of the new self.

89. What is the dying of the old
->self?<-

To be truly and from the soul distressed that our sins have provoked God to anger, and to hate and flee from them more and more.

90. What is the coming to life of the new
->self?<-

Heartfelt joy in God through Christ, and a diligent and eager desire to live according to the will of God in all good works.

91. What then are good works?

Those which are done from true faith, according to the law of God, and for His glory; and not those based on our own opinion or the traditions of other men.

92. What then are the commandments of the divine
->law?<-

And the Lord spoke all these
words.

->The First.<-

I am the Lord your God,

...commandments. In the first <note>original: ἐν ταῖς μὲν. Referring to the first tablet of the Law.</note> it commands those things pertaining to holiness toward God; the other tablet, in six commands, guides us in what is owed to our neighbor.

### 94. What does the Lord require in the first commandment?

It commands that, as much as I care for my salvation, I must avoid and completely renounce all idolatry, magic, and sorcery <note>original: ἐπωδὰς. This refers to incantations or spells used in folk magic.</note>. I must turn away from and flee the worship of saints or any other created things. Instead, I must rightly acknowledge Him, the only and true God; trust in Him alone with all humility and patience; look to Him alone for all good things; and love, honor, and fear Him with all my soul. I must be willing to renounce all created things rather than commit even the smallest offense against His will.

### 95. What is idolatry?

It is the act of imagining or inventing something else in which to place one's trust, instead of or alongside the one and true God who has revealed Himself in His Word <note>original: ῥήματι. Here referring to the Holy Scriptures as God’s spoken and written revelation.</note>.

### 96. What does the second commandment command?

->Neither to depict any form of God, nor again to serve Him with any other forms of worship than those commanded by Him in His word.<-

**97. But is it then not permitted to make any image of God at all?**

It is neither possible nor pious <note>original: ὅσιον (hosion); refers to that which is sanctioned by divine law</note> to depict God. While it is permitted to portray created things, we are forbidden by divine law to make or keep images of them for the sake of worship, or to worship God through them.

**98. May we not then have images <note>original: εἴδωλα (eidōla); the author uses this term to refer to religious icons or statues in a polemical sense</note> in the churches to serve as "books" for the unlearned masses?** <note>This refers to a common medieval defense of religious art—that images served as "the books of the illiterate" (libri pauperum).</note>

No. For it does not belong to us to be wiser than God, who wills that His people be taught not by means of silent images, but by the living preaching of the divine word.

**99. What does the third commandment command?**

Not only that we must not blaspheme God's name or abuse it for cursing or perjury <note>original: ἐπιορκίας (epiorkias); the act of swearing a false oath</note>, but also that we must not misuse it for empty swearing or sorcery, nor participate in such wicked deeds by either consenting to them or remaining silent; to put it briefly, that we should not use the name of God in any other way, except with

<vocab>God, image/representation, idols/images, commandment, to blaspheme, perjury/false swearing, worship/religion, form/likeness</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that God cannot and should not be represented by images, rejecting the idea that icons serve as educational tools for the illiterate. It also introduces the Third Commandment, forbidding the misuse of God's name in oaths, magic, or through complicit silence.</summary>
<keywords>idolatry, icons, religious education, preaching, Third Commandment, blasphemy, perjury, divine word</keywords>

...with reverence and awe, so that he may be rightly confessed and called upon, and be glorified in all our words and deeds.

### 100. Is the profaning of God’s name by cursing or perjury so serious a sin that God is also angry with those who do not prevent or forbid it as much as they can?

*Yes. For no sin is greater, nor provokes God’s wrath more, than the blasphemy of his name. For this reason, God commanded that it be punished by death.* <note>The text refers to the capital punishment for blasphemy established in the Mosaic Law, specifically Leviticus 24:16.</note>

### 101. But is it not also lawful to take an oath by the name of God?

*Yes, if the magistrates* <note>original: "ἄρχοντες", referring to civil authorities or rulers</note> *require it of their subjects, or if necessity demands it; so that in this way faithfulness and truth may be maintained, to the glory of God and the safety of one's neighbor. For such swearing is consistent with God's word; and therefore the saints in both the Old and New Testaments rightly made use of it.*

### 102. But is it also lawful to swear by the saints or any other created things?

*No. For a lawful oath is an invoca-* <note>The text cuts off mid-word (ἐπικλήσις / epiklesis), which is also noted by the catchword "κλησις" at the top of the page.</note>

***

...[an oath is] an invocation, <note>original: "ἐπίκλησις" (epiklēsis), a calling upon God</note> so that He, as the only one who is the knower of hearts, <note>original: "καρδιογνώστης" (kardiognōstēs). No creature is worthy to receive this honor.

### 103. What does God command in the fourth commandment?

First, that the ministry of the Gospel and the schools be preserved, and that I—on other days and especially on festive days <note>This refers to the Sabbath or Lord's Day.</note>—diligently attend the holy assemblies to listen to the divine words with attention, to participate in the holy mysteries, <term>mysteries: This is the Greek term for the Sacraments, specifically Baptism and the Lord's Supper.</term> to call upon the divine name, and to collect fitting alms for poor Christians. Second, that on all the days of my life I rest from wicked works, yielding my soul to be exercised by the divine Spirit; and thus to make a beginning of the eternal Sabbath in this life.

### 104. What are we commanded in the fifth commandment?

To render fitting honor, love, and faithfulness to my father, my mother, and all those set over us; to submit to their sound instructions and corrections with proper obedience; and to bear patiently with their difficulties and other perverse failings, <note>original: "σκαιοτρόπων ἐλαττωμάτων" (skaiotropōn elattōmatōn), implying flaws in character or awkward behavior.</note> since God wills to govern and direct us by their hand.

<page-num>79.</page-num>

# 105. What is commanded of us in the sixth commandment?

That we should not hate, insult, harm, or kill our neighbor, whether in our thoughts, our words, or by any gesture of the body, and much less by our actual deeds—whether we do this ourselves or through others. Instead, we must lay aside every impulse for revenge <note>original: "τοῦ τιμωρητικοῦ ὁρμὴν"</note>. Furthermore, we are not to harm ourselves or recklessly throw ourselves into danger. For this reason, to prevent murders, God willed that the <term>civil authorities: Governors or magistrates</term> should be armed with the sword.

# 106. But does this commandment seem to contain only a prohibition of murder?

Yes; but by the prohibition of murder, God also condemns the roots of murder, such as envy, hatred, anger, and the desire for revenge; hinting that all these things are "hidden murder" <note>original: "λαθρίδιον... φόνον"</note> in His sight.

# 107. Is it enough, then, not to kill our neighbor, as has been said?

No; for God, by the condemnation of envy, hatred, and anger, requires us to love our neighbor as ourselves; to behave towards them with gratitude and peace, and furthermore with gentleness, justice, and kindness. We are to ward off, as much as it lies within our power, anything harmful to them; and to speak briefly, the soul should be so disposed that we are even willing to do good to our enemies <note>original: "τοὺς ἐχθροὺς εὐποιεῖν"</note>.

<summary>This page explains the Sixth Commandment, broadening the definition of "murder" to include internal attitudes like hatred and envy, while enjoining believers to actively seek the well-being of others.</summary>
<keywords>Sixth Commandment, murder, revenge, civil authority, love of neighbor, hatred, anger, pacifism, ethics</keywords>

108. What does the seventh commandment intend to say?

*That God detests all <note>original: ἀκαθαρσίαν (akatharsian) — impurity or uncleanness, referring here to moral and sexual conduct</note> impurity; and for this reason, we must hate and turn away from it with our whole soul; and on the contrary, live chastely and modestly, whether we lead a married or a single life.*

109. Does this commandment then forbid nothing else but adultery and similar
->shameful acts?<-

*Since both our body and our soul are a <term>temple of the Holy Spirit: The Christian belief that God's Spirit dwells within the believer, making their physical body sacred</term>, God requires both to be kept pure and holy by us; and for this reason He forbids all impure actions and words, as well as shameful gestures, thoughts, desires, and whatever is naturally inclined to incite a person toward such things.*

110. What does the eighth commandment forbid?

*It forbids not only thefts and robberies, which are punished by the <term>Magistrates: Civil government officials or judges</term>; but God calls "theft" all those deceptive schemes and tricks by which we seek to appropriate our neighbor's goods for ourselves, whether by force or under the pretext of justice—such as deceptive weights and measures, unequal scales, fraudulent merchandise, counterfeit money, <term>usury: The practice of lending money at unfairly high interest rates</term>, and other means of profit...*

<meta>Catchword: the (τοὺς)</meta>

<summary>This page explains the Seventh Commandment's requirement for sexual purity in thought and deed, and the Eighth Commandment's broad definition of theft, which includes fraud, counterfeit money, and exploitative lending.</summary>

# XLI.

## 108. What is the meaning of the seventh commandment?

<margin>

d Heb. 13:4.
1 Cor. 7:7.
</margin>
*That God detests all <note>original: "turpitudinem" — refers to moral foulness or shameful behavior</note> impurity<sup>a</sup>: and therefore we must utterly hate and loathe it<sup>b</sup>: and on the contrary, we ought to live temperately, modestly, and chastely<sup>c</sup>, whether in sacred marriage or in a celibate life<sup>d</sup>.*

## 109. Does God forbid nothing more in this commandment than adultery and similar shameful acts?

<margin>
e Eph. 5:3, 4.
1 Cor. 6:18, 19.
f Matt. 5:27.
g Eph. 5:18.
1 Cor. 15:33.
</margin>
*Since our body and soul are <term>temples of the Holy Spirit: The theological concept that God dwells within a person, making their physical and mental state a sacred space</term>: God wills that we possess both purely and holily. And for that reason, He forbids all deeds, gestures, speech<sup>e</sup>, foul thoughts, desires<sup>f</sup>, and whatever entices a person toward such things<sup>g</sup>.*

<page-num>83.</page-num>

...those who oppose the divine word. Furthermore, in addition to these, [He forbids] all greed <term>pleonexia: a grasping desire to have more than what is due, often at the expense of others</term>, and the wasteful squandering or misuse of God’s gifts.

### 111. What does this commandment require?

To strive as much as we are able for the benefit of our neighbor, and to deal with them in such a way as we would want others to deal with us; furthermore, to be diligent in our own labors so that we may have enough for our own needs and be able to help those in want during their times of necessity.

### 112. What is the intent of the ninth commandment?

To bear false witness against no one; not to twist anyone's words through slander, backbiting, or insults; nor to join with those who judge others thoughtlessly and rashly. Instead, we must avoid all lies and deceits as the specific works of the devil and as things most hateful to God. In courts of law and all other dealings, we must love the truth and speak it plainly without excuses. Furthermore, we must protect and promote the good name and reputation of our neighbor as much as we are able.

### 113. What does the tenth commandment require?

That not even the slightest desire <note>original: "epithymia" — often translated as lust or covetousness</note> or thought against any of God’s commandments should ever be admitted into our hearts;

<gloss>ordinance, commandment, greed, to bear false witness, truth, desire, neighbor</gloss>

<page-num>85.</page-num>

...but continually and with the whole soul, turning away from every sin, we should pursue every form of righteousness with diligence. <note>This text concludes the answer to Question 113 regarding the Tenth Commandment, which addresses the "root" of sin in human desire.</note>

114. But can those who have turned toward God fulfill these commandments perfectly?

No. On the contrary, even the holiest of people, for as long as they remain in this life, have only a small beginning of such obedience <note>original: "πειθαρχίας" — a term for submissive obedience or discipline</note>. Nevertheless, they do so in such a way that they make every effort to live according to not just some, but all of God’s commandments.

115. Why then does God want the commandments to be preached so strictly and rigorously, since no one is able to keep them in this life?

First, so that throughout our whole life we may increasingly recognize the sinful tendency of our nature <note>original: "τὸ ἁμαρτηκὸν τῆς ἡμετέρας φύσεως" — referring to the inherent inclination toward wrongdoing</note>, and thus more eagerly seek the forgiveness of sins and the righteousness found in Christ. Second, so that we may ask God with constant perseverance for the grace of the Holy Spirit, so that we may be more and more renewed every day according to the image of God <note>original: "εἰκόνα" — the 'imago Dei' or the original state of humanity</note>, until we finally reach the goal of perfection set before us after this life.

115. Why then does God want His law to be preached so exactly and severely, since there is no one in this life who can keep it?

<margin>
d Rom. 3:20;

<term>Law</term> (lex): The moral standards set by God.
<term>Sin</term> (peccatum): Any failure to conform to God's law.
<term>Obedience</term> (obedientia): The act of following God's commands.
<term>God's Commandments</term> (Dei praecepta): The specific rules, such as the Ten Commandments.
<term>Holy Spirit</term> (Spiritus sancti): The third person of the Trinity, seen here as the source of grace for personal change.
<term>Forgiveness of sins</term> (remissio peccatorum): The pardoning of wrongdoing through Christ.
<term>Image of God</term> (imago Dei): The likeness of God in humans.
<term>Perfection</term> (perfectio): The ultimate goal of complete holiness.

# ->ON PRAYER<-

<image-desc>A rectangular decorative woodcut headpiece featuring a symmetrical design of scrolling foliage, acanthus leaves, and floral motifs.</image-desc>

**116.** Why is prayer necessary for Christians?

Because it is the chief part of <term>gratitude</term> <note>original: εὐχαριστίας (eucharistias) — used here in the sense of "thankfulness" or "thanksgiving" for God's blessings</note> which is owed by us to God. Furthermore, because God deigns to give His <term>grace</term> <note>original: χάριν (charin) — God's unmerited favor</note> and the Holy Spirit only to those who ask for these things from Him with earnest sighs and unceasing petitions, and who give Him thanks for them.

**117.** What is required for a prayer to please God and be heard by Him?

First, that we call from the soul upon the only true God—who has revealed Himself in His word—for all things He has commanded us to ask. Second, that we humble ourselves before the divine majesty, having recognized our own misery and <term>poverty</term> <note>original: ἔνδειαν (endeian) — a state of spiritual lack or deficiency</note> with a <term>contrite mind</term> <note>original: συντριβῇ διανοίας (syntribē dianoias) — a heart broken or softened by the realization of one's own sin and need</note>. Third, that we be firmly convinced that our petitions—even though we happen to be unworthy—will surely be heard by God for the sake of the Lord Jesus, just as He has promised us in His word.

**118.** What then has God commanded us to ask of Him?

All things necessary for both the soul and the body.

<note>The vocab list from the original OCR: προσευχή (prayer), εὐχαριστία (gratitude), Θεός (God), ἅγιον πνεῦμα (Holy Spirit), χάρις (grace), συντριβῇ (contrition), Ἰησοῦς (Jesus), ἔνδεια (need).</note>

<summary>This page introduces the doctrine of prayer, defining it as the essential expression of Christian gratitude and the means for receiving the Holy Spirit. It outlines three requirements for prayer: sincerity toward the true God, humble self-awareness, and faith in Christ's promises.</summary>
<keywords>prayer, gratitude, grace, Holy Spirit, contrition, Jesus Christ, petition, spiritual discipline</keywords>

116. Why is prayer necessary for Christians?

*Because it is the chief part of the <term>gratitude</term> <note>original: "gratitudinis" — in this catechism's structure, "Gratitude" refers to the entire Christian life of service and obedience performed in response to being saved.</note> which God requires of us. Furthermore, because God bestows His grace and the Holy Spirit only upon those who, with true <term>sighs</term> <note>original: "veris gemitibus" — suggesting deep, heartfelt longing rather than just spoken words.</note>, continually ask these things from Him and give Him thanks for them.*

117. What is required for that prayer which pleases God, and which is heard by Him?

*First, that we ask of the only true God—who has revealed Himself in His word—for all things that He has commanded us to ask of Him, with a sincere affection of the heart. Second, that out of an intimate sense of our own <term>need and misery</term> <note>original: "indigentiæ ac miseriæ" — a foundational concept in the Reformation, emphasizing that one must recognize their spiritual poverty before they can truly seek God.</note>, we cast ourselves down as suppliants in the presence of the Divine Majesty. Third, that we rely upon this firm foundation: that we, although unworthy, are certainly heard by God for the sake of Christ, just as He has promised us in His word.*

## ON PRAYER.

116. Why is prayer necessary for Christians?
*Because it is the most important part of the gratitude <note>original: εὐχαριστίας (eucharistias)</note> which we owe to God; and further, because God chooses to give His grace and Holy Spirit only to those who, with heartfelt sighs <note>original: ἐμπαθέσι στεναγμοῖς (empathesi stenagmois) — suggests deep, inward longing or groaning</note> and unceasing petitions, ask these things from Him and give Him thanks for them.*

117. What is required for a prayer to please God and be heard by Him?
*First, that we call upon the only true God—who has revealed Himself in His Word <note>original: ῥήματι (rhemati)</note>—from the soul, for all things that He has commanded us to ask. Second, that we acknowledge our own wretchedness <term>misery</term> <note>original: ταλαιπωρίαν (talaiporian) — a technical term in this catechism referring to man's state under sin</note> and need with a deep sense of mind, humbling ourselves before the Divine Majesty. Third, that we be firmly convinced that our prayers—even though we are unworthy—will surely be heard by God for the sake of the Lord Jesus, just as He has promised us in His Word.*

118. What then has God commanded us to ask of Him?
*All our needs, both spiritual and physical,*

119. And what is this prayer?

> Our Father, who art in heaven; Hallowed be thy name; Thy kingdom come; Thy will be done, as in heaven, so also on earth; Give us today our daily bread. And forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors; And lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from the evil one; For thine is the kingdom, and the power, and the glory, forever. Amen.

120. Why did Christ command us to address God in this way,
->Our Father?<-

So that right at the beginning of the prayer, he might instill in our hearts the reverence <term>pious awe and respect</term> and confidence toward God which is appropriate for God's children. This ought to be the foundation <note>original: κρηπῖδά, meaning a base or pedestal</note> of our prayer: I mean that through Christ, God has become our Father, and he is far less likely to refuse those things we ask of him in faith than our own earthly parents are to deny us when we beg them for something.

<meta>This page continues the exposition of the Lord's Prayer, moving from the invocation "Our Father" to the specific meaning of "who art in heaven" and the first two petitions. It maintains the question-and-answer format characteristic of sixteenth-century catechisms.</meta>

### 121. Why is the phrase "who art in heaven" added?

So that we might not conceive of anything earthly or limited regarding God’s heavenly majesty <note>original: "μεγαλειότητος"</note>, and that we might expect from His almighty power <term>omnipotence; the ability to do all things</term> everything necessary for both the body and the <gloss>soul</gloss>.

### 122. What is the first petition?

"Hallowed be thy name." This means: grant us, first, to know You rightly and to honor and glorify Your almighty power, wisdom, goodness, mercy, justice, and truth, which shine forth in all Your works. Second, grant that we may direct our whole life—all our words, deeds, and even our very thoughts—toward this goal: that Your name never be blasphemed <term>spoken of with irreverence or disrespect</term> because of us, but rather be held in the highest honor and praise.

### 123. What is the second petition?

"Thy kingdom come." This means: govern us by Your word and Spirit in such a way that we submit ourselves to You more and more. Protect and grow Your church; destroy the works of the devil and every power that rises against You, along with all the wicked schemes devised against Your holy word. Do this until the full perfection of Your kingdom arrives, when You shall be "all in all" <note>original: "πάντα ἔση ἐν πᾶσιν" — a reference to the biblical concept in 1 Corinthians 15:28 where God's presence is fully realized throughout creation.</note>.

### 124. What is the third petition?

"Your will be done, on earth as it is in heaven." That is: Grant that we and all people, having renounced our own will <note>original: "τῷ ἰδίῳ θελήματι ἀποταξάμενοι" — the Greek implies a decisive "bidding farewell" to one's own desires</note>, may obey Your will alone without making excuses, and that each of us may carry out the duties assigned to us with such eagerness and faithfulness, just as Your angels in heaven fulfill Your commandments.

### 125. What is the fourth petition?

"Give us this day our daily bread." That is: Provide us with all things necessary for this physical life; so that we may come to recognize through this that You alone are the fountain <term>krounos; a spring or headwater</term> of every good thing, and that without Your blessing, neither our own anxiety and hard work, nor even the gifts You have granted us, can bring us any benefit. Therefore, turning away from reliance on all created things, let us place our trust in You alone.

### 126. What is the fifth petition?

"Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors." That is: Do not count our trespasses against us, miserable sinners that we are, nor the wickedness of our nature <note>original: "μοχθηρίαν" — refers to an innate moral corruption or wretchedness</note> which constantly clings to us; but for the sake of Christ’s

### 124. What is the third petition?

**Your will be done, on earth as it is in heaven.** That is: grant that we and all people, having submitted our own will <note>original: "τῷ ἰδίῳ θελήματι ὑποταξάμενοι"</note>, may obey Your will alone without making excuses <note>original: "ἀπροφασίστως"</note>, and that each of us may carry out the duties appropriate to us with such eagerness and faithfulness that we fulfill Your commands just as Your angels do in the heavens.

### 125. What is the fourth petition?

**Give us this day our daily bread.** That is: supply us with all things necessary for this life, so that from this we may recognize that You alone are the fountain <note>original: "κρουνόν"</note> of every good thing; and that without Your blessing, neither our own anxiety and hard work <term>φιλεργίαν (philergian); a love of labor or industriousness</term>, nor even the gifts themselves, can bring us any benefit. Therefore, let us renounce trust in all created things and have our confidence in You alone.

### 126. What is the fifth petition?

**Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors.** That is: do not count against us miserable sinners our many offenses, nor even the depravity <term>μοχθηρίαν (mochthērian); inherent wickedness or moral corruption</term> of our nature, which remains ever clinging <gloss>to us</gloss>; but for the sake of Christ’s—

### 124. What is the third petition?

**Your will be done, on earth as it is in heaven.** That is: act so that we and all people, having renounced our own will <note>original: "τῷ ἰδίῳ θελήματι ἀποταξάμενοι"</note>, may obey Your will alone without making excuses, and that each of us may carry out the things belonging to us with such eagerness and faith as Your angels in the heavens fulfill Your commands.

### 125. What is the fourth petition?

**Give us today our daily bread.** <note>The Greek term "epiousion" (ἐπιούσιον) used here for "daily" is a rare word in the New Testament, often interpreted as bread "sufficient for the day" or "necessary for existence."</note> That is: supply us with all things necessary for this life; so that from this we may recognize that You alone are the fountain <note>original: "κρουνὸν" (krounon), a spring or source</note> of every good thing, and that without Your blessing, neither our worry and industry <term>industry; hard work or diligent labor</term>, nor even Your own gifts, bring us any benefit. And for this reason, having turned away from all created things <note>original: "πάσαις ταῖς κτίσεσιν ἀποταξάμενοι"</note>, may we have our confidence in You alone.

### 126. What is the fifth petition?

**Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors.** That is: do not count against us, miserable sinners, our transgressions, nor the wickedness of nature which continues to cling to us always; but for the sake of Christ... <note>The Greek text cuts off here at the end of the page; the "mu" catchword at the bottom suggests the next word will be "mous" or "mou," likely referring to the blood (haimatos) or mediation of Christ.</note>

### 127. What is the sixth petition?

**And lead us not into temptation, but deliver us from evil:** that is, *since we are so weak in ourselves that we cannot stand for even a single moment* <term>moment: the original Greek "akare" implies a split second or the blink of an eye</term>, *and our unrelenting enemies—Satan, the world, and our own flesh* <note>In this context, "flesh" refers to human's innate sinful nature or desires</note>—*never stop attacking us, please continue to preserve and strengthen us by the power of the Holy Spirit. Grant us the strength to firmly resist them and not grow weary in this spiritual struggle, until we finally die and achieve a complete victory.*

### 128. How do you conclude this prayer?

**For Yours is the kingdom, and the power, and the glory, forever:** that is, *we ask all these things from You because, as our King and the Lord of all things, You are both willing and able to give us every good thing; we pray this so that by it, not we, but Your holy name may be glorified forever.*

### 129. What does the "Amen" signify?

"Amen" means "so be it," <note>original: "γένοιτο" (genoito); the standard Greek translation of the Hebrew 'Amen', expressing a certain hope or affirmation</note> or rather, "let it be truly fulfilled." For my prayer is heard by God with far greater certainty than I can feel in my heart when I am asking these things from God.

<meta>catchword: TO THE</meta>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut headpiece consisting of a horizontal band filled with symmetrical scrolling vine patterns and stylized floral motifs.</image-desc>

# S A Y I N G S .
<note>original Greek: ΡΗΣΕΙΣ (Rheseis), meaning utterances, maxims, or spoken words.</note>

-> From the Holy Scriptures, through which, as if through a small mirror <note>original Greek: katoptridiou pinakou, literally a 'little-mirror tablet'</note>, each person is reminded of their duty according to their own rank. <-

## TO THE RULERS .

Understand, O kings; be instructed, all you who judge the earth; serve the Lord in fear, and rejoice in him with trembling; lay hold of instruction, lest the Lord be angry, and you perish from the righteous way, whenever his wrath is quickly kindled. <note>Psalm 2:10-12. The Greek version used here (the Septuagint) renders "Kiss the son" as "Lay hold of instruction."</note>

Cease from your evil deeds; learn to do good; seek out justice; rescue the wronged, give judgment for the orphan, and do justice for the widow; and come now, let us reason together. <note>Isaiah 1:16-18</note>

## TO THE JUDGES .

Look out for yourself from among all the people capable and God-fearing men, righteous men who hate pride; and you shall appoint them over the people; and they shall judge the people at all times. <note>Exodus 18:21-22</note>

See what you are doing; for you do not judge for man, but for the Lord; and the words of judgment are with you; and now let the fear of God be upon you, and take care and act; for there is no injustice with the Lord our God, nor is there partiality <note>original Greek: thaumasai prosopon, literally "honoring the face," a common idiom for favoritism</note>, nor the taking of bribes. <note>2 Chronicles 19:6-7. The Greek title for this book is "Paraleipomenon," meaning "Things Omitted."</note>

<vocab>SAYINGS, TO THE RULERS, TO THE JUDGES, Holy Scripture, Psalms, Isaiah, Exodus, Chronicles</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the collection of "Sayings" (Rheseis) introduced on page 99. While the previous page provided a Greek-influenced introduction, this page presents the Latin versions of scriptural exhortations organized by social "ranks" or duties, beginning with civil authorities and judges.</meta>

# SAYINGS OF THE HOLY SCRIPTURE, BY WHICH
## EVERYONE IN THEIR STATION OF LIFE IS
### REMINDED AND INSTRUCTED IN THEIR OWN DUTY.

#### *TO THE MAGISTRATE.*
<note>The "Magistrate" refers to civil governors, rulers, and those holding public office.</note>

Kings, act wisely; *be instructed, you who judge the earth. Serve the Lord in fear, and rejoice in him with trembling. Accept discipline <note>original Latin: "Apprehendite disciplinam," a phrase often rendered as "Kiss the Son" in other traditions, but here emphasizing the ruler's need for correction.</note>, lest the Lord be angry and you perish from the righteous path, when his wrath is quickly kindled. Psalm 2:10, 11, 12.*

*Cease from doing evil; learn to do good; seek justice; guide the oppressed, judge for the orphan, judge for the widow. Isaiah 1:16, 17.*

#### *TO THE JUDGES.*

*Look out from among all the people for strong men, who fear God, truthful men who hate greed, and set them over the people, who shall judge the people at all times. Exodus 18:21, 22.*

*See what you are doing. For you shall not judge for man, but for the LORD <note>original: "IEHOVÆ," the Latinized form of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton.</note>, and He will be with you in the matter of judgment. Now, therefore, let the fear of the LORD be upon you; be careful and act, for there is no iniquity with the LORD our God, nor partiality, nor acceptance of bribes. 2 Chronicles 19:6, 7.*

# TO THE RULERS AND THE
### Subjects.

Let every soul be subject to the governing authorities. For there is no authority except from God. And the authorities that exist have been appointed by God. Therefore, whoever resists the authority resists the ordinance of God; and those who resist will bring judgment upon themselves. For rulers are not a cause of fear for good works, but for evil. Do you wish to have no fear of the authority? Do what is good, and you will have praise from it. For he is God’s servant to you for good. But if you do evil, be afraid; for he does not bear the sword <note>The "sword" symbolizes the state's power to punish, including the legal right to use force or capital punishment.</note> in vain. For he is God’s servant, an avenger to execute wrath upon the one who practices evil. Therefore, it is necessary to be subject, not only because of wrath but also for the sake of conscience. For this reason you also pay taxes; for they are God's ministers, attending continually to this very thing. *Epistle to the Romans 13; 1st Epistle of Peter 2.*

---

## TO THE HUSBANDS.

Husbands, love your wives, just as Christ also loved the church. *Epistle to the Ephesians 5.*
Husbands, love your wives, and do not be bitter toward them. *Epistle to the Colossians 3.*
Husbands, likewise, dwell with them with understanding, giving honor to the wife as to the weaker vessel <note>In the cultural context of the time, "weaker vessel" was a common legal and social idiom referring to the physical and social protections afforded to women, rather than a commentary on their character.</note>, as being heirs together of the grace of life, so that your prayers may not be hindered. *1st Epistle of Peter 3.*

## To Children.

Children, obey your parents in the Lord; for this is right. Honor your father and your mother; which is the first commandment with a promise, that it may be well with you, and you may live long on the earth. Epistle to the Ephesians, chapter 6. Epistle to the Colossians, chapter 3.

## To Male and Female Slaves, Hired Servants, and Laborers.

Slaves, obey your masters according to the flesh, with fear and trembling, in the sincerity of your heart, as to Christ; not by way of eye-service <note>original: "ὀφθαλμοδουλείαν" — work done only when the master is watching to gain favor</note>, as people-pleasers, but as slaves of Christ, doing the will of God from the soul; serving with goodwill to the Lord, and not to men; knowing that whatever good thing each one does, this he will receive back from the Lord, whether he is a slave or free. Epistle to the Ephesians, chapter 6. Epistle to the Colossians, chapter 3.

Household servants, be subject in all fear to your masters, not only to the good and gentle, but also to the crooked <note>original: "σκολιοῖς" — meaning harsh, unfair, or morally warped</note>. First Epistle of Peter, chapter 2.

## To Heads of Households.

Masters, do the same things toward them; giving up your threats, and providing what is just and fair to your slaves; knowing that your own Master is in heaven, and there is no partiality <note>original: "προσωποληψία" — literally "taking of the face," meaning favoritism based on status</note> with him. Epistle to the Ephesians, chapter 6. Epistle to the Colossians, chapter 3.

# CHILDREN.

*Children, obey your parents in the Lord, for that is right. Honor your father and your mother: which is the first commandment with a promise, that it may be well with you, and you may be long-lived upon the earth. Epistle to the Ephesians 6. & Epistle to the Colossians 3.*

## TO SERVANTS, HANDMAIDS, HIRELINGS, and laborers.

*Servants, obey those who are your masters according to the flesh, with fear and trembling, with the simplicity of your heart, as to Christ: not serving only when watched <note>original: "ad oculum seruientes" — literally "eye-service," meaning work performed only to impress a supervisor while they are looking</note>, as those who strive to please men, but as servants of Christ; doing from the heart what God wills: serving with benevolence to the Lord and not to men, as those who know that whatever good each person has done, this they will receive back from the Lord, whether he be a servant or a free man. Epistle to the Ephesians 6. Epistle to the Colossians 3.*

*Household servants, be subject with all fear to your masters, not only to the good and fair, but also to the perverse <note>original: "prauis" — meaning crooked, harsh, or morally difficult masters</note>. First Epistle of Peter, third chapter <note>The text cites the third chapter, though in modern Bibles this passage is found in 1 Peter 2:18.</note>.*

## TO HEADS OF HOUSEHOLDS.

*And you, Masters, do the same things toward them: setting aside threats, exhibit fairness and equality to your servants, knowing that your own Master also is in heaven, and there is no respect of persons <note>original: "personæ respectum" — partiality or favoritism based on social status or appearance</note> with him. Epistle to the Ephesians 6. Epistle to the Colossians 3.*

# TO THE YOUNG AND TO
## all others.

Do not be deceived: neither fornicators, nor idolaters, nor adulterers, nor the effeminate <note>original: "μαλακοὶ" (malakoi) — refers to those who are soft or yielding to luxury, often used in a moral or sexual context in this period</note>, nor those who lie with men, nor thieves, nor the covetous, nor drunkards, nor revilers, nor extortioners, shall inherit the kingdom of God. First Epistle to the Corinthians, chapter 6.

You who are younger, be subject to the elders. And all of you, in being subject to one another, clothe yourselves in humility; for God resists the proud, but gives grace to the humble. Humble yourselves therefore under the mighty hand of God, that he may exalt you in due time: casting all your care upon him, for he cares for you. First Epistle of Peter, chapter 5.

## To Virgins.

There is a difference between a wife and a virgin: she who is unmarried cares for the things of the Lord, that she may be holy both in body and spirit; but she who is married cares for the things of the world, how she may please her husband. First Epistle to the Corinthians, chapter 7.

## To Widows.

She who is a widow indeed and left alone, has set her hope on God, and continues in supplications and prayers night and day; but she who lives in pleasure <note>original: "σπαταλῶσα" (spatalōsa) — implying a life of luxury, indulgence, or wantonness</note> is dead while she lives. And command these things, that they may be blameless. Not idle, and gossips, and busybodies, speaking things which they ought not. First Epistle to Timothy, chapter 5.

### Page 112
#### Sig H 5

...to obey your will <note>original Latin: "voluntati"; the previous page note suggests a Greek equivalent "βουλήματι", referring to God's purpose or decree</note> and that we may be able to please you, so that all our works might contribute to the glory of your holy name and the building up <note>original: "ædificationem" / Greek: "οἰκοδομὴν" — the process of strengthening the faith and character of others</note> of our neighbor. 

And just as you have now caused the radiance of your sun to spread over the earth to provide a wonderful light for our bodies; so also, by the brilliance of your Spirit, illuminate our hearts and souls, and present yourself to us as a guide on the straight path of righteousness <term>righteousness: the state of being morally right or justifiable before God</term>. 

Grant that in whatever we undertake, we may set this before us in all things and above all else: to walk in your fear, to worship you, to honor you, and to expect all good things and all salvation from your blessing alone; and that we may not begin anything that would displease you. 

Furthermore, grant us, most kind Father, that while we labor for the frail and fleeting life of this body, we may perpetually seek your kingdom and your righteousness above all else, never doubting that all other things will also be added to us. 

Protect our bodies and our souls: strengthen us against all the temptations of Satan, and rescue us from all dangers which threaten us at almost every moment in this life. 

Since indeed it is of no benefit to have begun rightly unless we persevere until the very end; we pray that you would deign to grant us your company and protection not only today, but also that you would increase your grace in us more and more every day throughout our whole life, until at last we are perfectly united with your beloved Son, our Lord; who is the true sun of our souls, shining both day and night without ceasing for all ages. 

In addition to these things, O Lord, prosper the course of the holy Gospel; and once all the works of the devil have been destroyed, strengthen all those who are leaders <note>original: "antistites" — referring to bishops or high-ranking church officials</term> of your Church and people: and those who are harassed by persecutions and disturbances, steady them with constant consolation. 

Furthermore, so that we may obtain these and other necessary things from you, most kind Father, forgive all our sins for the sake of your beloved Son Jesus Christ, who promised us that whatever we should ask in his name would certainly be given to us; and with that confidence, [he taught us] to pray thus:

## PRAYER BEFORE ATTENDING SCHOOL.

> From Psalm 118, "How shall a young man keep his way straight? By guarding your words. With my whole heart I have sought you; do not cast me away from your commandments." <note>This refers to Psalm 119:9-10 in most English Bibles, though the Greek Septuagint numbers it as 118.</note>

**I** give thanks to you, most gentle Father, for all your other great and innumerable blessings, but especially that in your tender mercy you have called me to the knowledge of your gospel <term>gospel: the "good news" of the Christian message</term>, and have provided teachers who instruct me in beautiful and useful lessons, and who train my soul with holy and saving instructions. Grant that I may clearly recognize the greatness of such gifts and acknowledge my gratitude for them with a thankful heart. Furthermore, having scattered the clouds of my soul, enlighten me with the rays of your Spirit, so that I may learn the things delivered by my teachers with zeal and eagerness. By retaining them firmly, may I be able to recite them with ready judgment; lest the labor of the instructors, my own toil, the most useful time, the opportunity provided by you, and the fruit expected from me be shamefully lost through my laziness and ingratitude. 

So that this may not happen, grant me your Holy Spirit, the fountain of all understanding and truth; so that by its working, I may learn to be truthful and become fit for the comprehension of education. Whatever pursuit I devote myself to, let this be my goal forever: to know you alone, the true God, and Jesus Christ whom you sent, and to serve you with a pure conscience.

**I** give thanks to you, most merciful God, both for your other supreme and infinite benefits, and most especially, because in your singular mercy you have also called me to the knowledge of your Gospel <term>Gospel: from the Greek "euangelion," meaning the good news of Christ</term>, and have entrusted me to teachers who might instruct me in good literature and the arts, and imbue my soul with holy and honorable precepts. # PRAYER BEFORE
## partaking of food.

Psalm 144 <note>original: "Ψαλμ. ρμδ." — Following the Septuagint/Greek numbering; this is Psalm 145:15-16 in most English Bibles.</note>: The eyes of all look to you with hope, O Lord; and you give them their food in due season; you open your hand and satisfy every living thing with your favor.

Lord Almighty <term>Pantokrator: literally "Ruler of All," a title emphasizing God's absolute sovereignty over the universe.</term>, who by your <gloss>your</gloss> supernatural goodness created all things from nothing in the beginning, and even now preserve and govern them; you who miraculously nourished your <gloss>your</gloss> people Israel for forty years in the wilderness: bless also us, your <gloss>your</gloss> wretched servants, and sanctify the gifts of your <gloss>your</gloss> benevolent hand; so that, enjoying them soberly and holily according to your <gloss>your</gloss> will, we may truly recognize you as our Father and the fountain of all good things. And grant that we may not only focus our dispositions on the enjoyment of bodily things, but above all else pursue the spiritual bread given in your <gloss>your</gloss> words, which, by purifying our souls, leads to eternal life—that life which you have prepared for us through the precious blood of your <gloss>your</gloss> son. Our Father.

And thus Christ admonishes us in the Gospel according to <gloss>according to</gloss> Luke:

## Take heed to yourselves, lest [your hearts] be weighed down

# PRAYER BEFORE
## food is taken.

Psalm 144 <note>original: "Psalm.144" — This follows the Vulgate numbering; in modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 145:15-16.</note>. The eyes of all hope in you, O Lord, and you give them food in due season: you open your hand and fill every living creature with blessing.

*Almighty and most merciful God, who by your immense goodness created all things from nothing, and by that same perpetual divine power of yours sustain and govern them; you who miraculously nourished your people Israel in the desert for forty years; bless us also, your wretched servants, and sanctify these your gifts, so that we may enjoy them soberly and holily, and thereby recognize that you are truly a father to us and the fountain of all good things. Grant, furthermore, that while using these bodily goods, we may always be so inclined that we chiefly seek that spiritual food of your word, by which our souls are nourished unto eternal life, which Christ prepared for us by his precious blood. Our Father, etc.*

# PRAYER BEFORE
## going to bed.

> *Lord God, heavenly Father, who according to your manifold wisdom have destined the day for labor and the night for rest: we give you thanks, because you have [protected] us through this day so mercifully—* <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at "clemen-" (mercifully), continuing on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page contains Latin devotional texts: the conclusion of a warning from the Gospel of Luke, a grace to be said after meals based on Deuteronomy, and the beginning of an evening prayer.</summary>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page completes the Greek version of the evening prayer begun on the previous page and introduces a new section of public liturgical prayers. The text follows the bilingual pattern where Greek and Latin versions of the same prayers are presented for the reader's devotion and linguistic study.</meta>

—you have protected us, and with your benefits, you have abundantly and harmlessly provided for us. Grant us, O Father, that while our bodies, resting from labors and anxieties, are revived in sleep, our minds may not sleep together with our bodies, being strengthened in love, but may always hold our calling and our redemption in remembrance. May they continue to meditate compassionately upon your mercies and all your benefits, so that our consciences may also share in their own rest along with our bodies. Do not allow us to be overcome by sleep beyond measure, yielding to sloth or necessity; but once we are restored by a moderate rest, and from there return to our usual tasks with a more eager vigor, make us more ready to serve You and our neighbor. Meanwhile, deliver us from all danger while we sleep; and furthermore, keep us undefiled in both body and soul, so that even our sleep may belong to the glory of your name. Since today has passed for us not without manifold failings, seeing that we are weak and carry about the weight of terrible sins, we beseech you: just as everything now is covered by the darkness of night, so also in your supernatural compassion may you bury all our transgressions, so that because of them we may not be cast out from your presence. Furthermore, grant a <term>respite<note>original: "παύλης" (paules) — a cessation or rest from suffering.</note></term> and comfort to all those suffering from illnesses or other misfortunes, through Jesus Christ, your Son, our Lord, who taught us to intercede in all our necessities, for ourselves and for our brothers, with these your words: Our Father.

### Prayer before the sermon on Sunday, and on other feast days.

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page provides the Latin version of the evening prayer concluded on the previous page in Greek. It also marks the beginning of a new section containing public liturgical prayers intended for use during church services, specifically before the sermon on Sundays and feast days.</meta>

—you have protected, and you continually heap so many benefits upon us. Grant also, Father, that as we rest from labor and cares, we may be so refreshed by sleep that our soul meanwhile—not as if lulled to sleep along with the body—does not slumber in its love for you, but that the memory of our creation and salvation may never at any time be erased from our hearts. Grant, moreover, that our conscience may enjoy its own rest no less than our body. In addition, may we use sleep moderately, attending not to <term>sloth<note>original: "ignavia" — refers here to spiritual laziness or over-indulgence in rest.</note></term> but to necessity: so that, returning to our interrupted works more eager and vigorous, we may more readily serve you and our neighbor. Meanwhile, while we take our sleep, protect us from every danger, and keep us undefiled in both soul and body, so that even our sleep may reach to the glory of your name. Furthermore, since this day has passed for us not without manifold failings (since we wretches continually carry sin about with us), we ask you: just as the night now wraps everything in darkness, so may you, according to your immense mercy, bury all our sins, lest because of them we be repelled from your sight. Grant also rest and consolation to all who are struggling with illnesses or other calamities, for the sake of Jesus Christ, your Son, our Lord; who taught us to pray to you in any of our needs, for ourselves and our brothers, with these words: Our Father, etc.

## commonly performed in the Church.

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>Continuity: The previous page concluded the Latin evening prayer and introduced the section for public prayers. This page provides the Greek text for the public prayer performed before a sermon, beginning with the salutation "Grace, mercy, and peace" as indicated by the catchword on page 128.</meta>

## Grace, mercy, and peace from God the Father,
## and from the Son, and from the Holy Spirit, be with 
## all of us, both now and forever.

**F**ather in heaven, the righteous and all-merciful God from eternity; we recognize and confess before your divine majesty that we are wretched, miserable, and sinners, having been conceived and born in total wickedness and corruption, prone to all malice, and <term>unfit for any good<note>original: "ἀφυεῖς εἰς πᾶν ἀγαθὸν" — suggests a natural lack of capacity or talent for doing what is morally right without divine help.</note></term>. By our sinful life, we unceasingly transgress your holy commandments, and by our offenses, we continually provoke your wrath against us, piling up eternal punishments for ourselves according to your righteous judgment. But O Lord, we are grieved by the ways we have provoked you, and having condemned our own evils, we flee to your grace; entreating you, O Father, to help us in our misery and wretchedness. Have mercy on us, O most compassionate Father, and forgive us all our sins, through the blood of your Son our Lord Jesus Christ. Furthermore, by breathing upon us with the gifts of your Holy Spirit, renew our hearts in such a way that, through Him, having accurately learned all our unrighteousness, we may be <term>displeasing to ourselves<note>original: "αὐτοὶ αὑτοῖς ἀπαρέσκοιμεν" — a common theme in Reformation-era liturgy where self-denial and the recognition of one's own faults are seen as the first steps toward spiritual growth.</note></term>. May sin be put to death within us through Him, so that we may rise to a new life, in which we may be able to bring forth fruits worthy of righteousness and holiness, pleasing you through Christ. In addition to these things, extend to us the understanding of your holy Word, according to your divine will; so that by its guidance, having renounced all created things, we may be accustomed to trust in you alone. May our **old man** <note>original: "παλαιὸς ἡμῶν ἄνθρωπος" — a biblical reference to the sinful human nature before it is transformed by faith.</note> with all its desires be increasingly crucified day by day, and may we present ourselves as a living sacrifice to you; so that your all-holy name may be glorified, and our neighbor may be built up, through our Lord Jesus
-> Christ <-

<vocab>God, Christ, Holy Spirit, sin, grace, repentance, sacrifice, righteousness</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a Greek liturgical prayer intended for public worship before a sermon. It follows a traditional structure of confessing inherent sinfulness, requesting forgiveness through Christ's blood, and asking for the Holy Spirit's guidance to live a renewed life.</summary>
<keywords>liturgy, confession of sin, Holy Spirit, sanctification, sermon preparation, Greek prayer, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page contains the Latin version of the public prayer before a sermon, which follows the Greek version presented on the previous pages (128-129). The catchword "Dominum" on the previous page led into the final section of the Greek text; this page provides the complete Latin equivalent of that liturgy.</meta>

Grace, mercy, and peace from God—Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—be with you all, both now and forever.

*Heavenly Father, from all eternity a God equally just and merciful, we recognize and confess before your divine majesty that we are truly wretched sinners, conceived and born in corruption, prone to all evil, and <term>unfit for any good<note>original: "inhabiles ad omne bonum" — translating the theological concept of total depravity, suggesting a natural lack of capacity for divine righteousness.</note></term>; and that by unceasingly violating your most holy law, and by our sins provoking your wrath against us, we accumulate eternal punishments for ourselves according to your righteous judgment. But we grieve from our hearts that we have so often offended you, such a kind father: we condemn ourselves, we detest our sins, and we flee to you, humbly asking that your mercy may come to the aid of our misery. Forgive us therefore our dismal sins for the sake of Jesus Christ your Son, our Lord. Then thereafter, cultivate our hearts with the gifts of your Spirit in such a way that, through him, having thoroughly recognized all our iniquity, we may be <term>displeased with ourselves<note>original: "nobis ipsis displiceamus" — a recurring theme in Reformation-era prayer, emphasizing that true repentance involves a rejection of one's former sinful self.</note></term>; and with sin having been put to death in us through him, we may rise again to a new life; in which we may bear fruits worthy of righteousness and holiness, accepted by you on account of the same Christ. Grant moreover that we may rightly understand the meaning of your word according to the rule of your will; and with all creatures having been bidden farewell under its guidance, let us become accustomed to placing our trust in you alone; so that the <term>old man<note>original: "vetus ille homo" — a biblical reference to the sinful human nature before it is transformed by faith.</note></term> in us with all his desires may be more and more crucified, and we may offer ourselves to you as a <term>living sacrifice<note>original: "viuam hostiam" — a reference to Romans 12:1, where the believer’s life is offered as a form of spiritual worship.</note></term>: and in that way your holy name may be celebrated, and our neighbor built up; through JESUS CHRIST.*

<note>Vocabulary: Grace, mercy, Heavenly father, sinners, corruption, Jesus Christ, spirit, justice/righteousness, sacrifice, JESUS CHRIST</note>

<keywords>confession of sin, liturgy, Holy Spirit, living sacrifice, new life, repentance, justification, Protestant prayer</keywords>

### ...of Christ who taught us to pray to you in this way: Our Father. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, concluding the introduction to the Lord's Prayer.</note>

# Prayer on the Lord's Day after the preaching. <note>original: "κήρυξιν" — referring to the sermon or proclamation of the Word.</note>

**B**eloved in the Lord, since from the law of God, as if in a mirror <note>The "mirror of the law" is a central Reformation concept, suggesting the Ten Commandments show us our flaws so we seek God's grace.</note>, we learn how many and how great our transgressions are, so as to be worthy of both temporal and eternal punishments; let us each confess them from the heart, before our most bountiful Father, addressing him thus both together and individually:

I confess to you, my God and my creator, I, the wretched sinner, that I have acted lawlessly against you in many and various ways, not only having provoked you with external sins, but much more with internal ones, by innate blindness, unbelief, doubt, <term>smallness of soul<note>original: "μικροψυχίᾳ" — often translated as faint-heartedness or lack of spiritual courage.</note></term>, self-will, pride, envy, hatred, and other evils; which you, O Lord, know about me, and which I am unable to sufficiently recognize. But for these things, O God, I am burdened from my soul and I repent, and I pray to obtain grace, through your beloved Son our Lord Jesus Christ.

After this, the <term>minister<note>original: "ἐκκλησιαστὴς" — literally "one of the assembly," usually referring to the preacher or leader of the congregation.</note></term> announces to the believers the forgiveness of sins, but to the unrepentant the judgment of God, in these words:

**H**ear then the unchanging comfort concerning the grace of God, which he promises to all who believe in the Gospel; for thus says the Lord Jesus in John:

# PRAYER AFTER THE
### Sermon on the Lord’s day.

*Beloved in the Lord; because from the commandments of God, as if from a mirror <note>The "mirror of the law" is a classic Reformation teaching: the idea that the Ten Commandments act as a mirror to show believers their spiritual "spots" or sins, driving them to seek God's mercy.</note>, we recognize how many and how great our sins are, so much so that they deserve both temporal and eternal punishments: let each of us confess them with the whole heart, before our most faithful Father, everyone together and individually addressing him: I confess to you, my God and my creator, that I have sinned against you heavily and in manifold ways; nor have I only provoked your anger with outward offenses, but much more so with internal sins, namely by innate blindness, unbelief, doubt, <term>faintheartedness<note>original: "pusillanimitate" — literally "smallness of soul," referring to a lack of spiritual courage or trust in God's promises.</note></term>, impatience, arrogance, pride, greed, envy, hatred, and other vices, which you, O Lord, know to be in me, but which I truly cannot recognize accurately enough. On which account, O Father, I grieve from my soul, and being touched by repentance, I long to obtain grace, through your beloved Son our Lord Jesus Christ.*

After this, the minister of the word announces the forgiveness of sins to the faithful, but the judgment of God to the impenitent, with these words:

*Hear now the undoubted consolation regarding the grace of God, which he promises to all believers in the Gospel. For thus says the Lord Jesus in John*

<meta>Continuity: This page provides the Greek translation of the liturgical absolution and the "Prayer after the Sermon," following the Latin text on the previous page. It begins with the famous words of the Gospel of John used as a scriptural foundation for the declaration of the forgiveness of sins.</meta>

...to the Evangelist: "For God so loved the world, that he gave his only begotten Son, that whoever believes in him should not perish, but have eternal life." <note>original: "Οὕτως ἠγάπησεν ὁ θεὸς τὸν κόσμον..." — This is the Greek text of John 3:16, a central verse in Protestant liturgy used to assure the congregation of God's love.</note> Whosoever among you are both displeased with yourselves and burdened by your own transgressions, and have believed that all your sins are entirely and completely forgiven solely for the sake of the ransom <note>original: "λύτρον" (lytron) — a term referring to the price paid to release a captive, used here for Christ's sacrifice.</note> of Christ, and have chosen to renounce your sins more and more and to serve the Lord in true holiness and righteousness: To you, since you believe in the Son of God, I declare the Word of God, that all your sins—just as the Lord promised in the Gospel—are loosed in the heavens <note>This refers to the "Power of the Keys" (Matthew 16:19), the authority granted to the church to declare the forgiveness (loosing) or retention (binding) of sins.</note>, through the perfect redemption of Jesus Christ our Lord, which was fulfilled by his most holy suffering and death for our sake, amen. 

But as many of you as dwell in your sins and shames, or persist in sins against your conscience, to you I also proclaim the command of God: that the wrath and judgment of God remain upon you, and all your sins are retained in heaven, nor is it possible for you to be delivered from eternal condemnation until you turn back <note>That is, until you repent.</note>. Since, therefore, we are unhesitatingly persuaded that we ourselves and our prayers have been sanctified through the sufferings of Christ to be pleasing to God, we call upon him from our hearts, saying thus:

> Lord Almighty, creator of both heaven and earth, we thank you from our soul, because having created us, you have continued until this present day to guard and nourish us along with all our belongings, and you intend to protect and govern us in the future. But above all, we thank you because you have made known to us your Son Jesus Christ—promised of old in Paradise <note>A reference to the "Protevangelium" or the first promise of a Savior made to Adam and Eve in the Garden of Eden.</note>—and have forgiven our sins through his terrible sufferings and death. And we beseech you, O Lord, through the preaching of your word and the power of your Spirit, renew us even more into the <term>image of your Son<note>original: "εἰκόνα" (eikona) — The restoration of the "Image of God" in humanity through Christ is a key goal of Christian life in this theology.</note></term>, so that leading an eternal life with you in both soul and body, we may glorify your majesty.

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page contains the Latin version of the liturgical absolution and the "Prayer after the Sermon." While the previous page provided these same texts in Greek, this page presents the Latin text used in the liturgy to declare the forgiveness of sins to the congregation and to offer thanks for God’s providence and redemption.</meta>

...the Evangelist: "For God so loved the world, that he gave his only begotten Son, that whoever believes in him should not perish, but have eternal life." <note>original: "Ita Deus dilexit mundum..." — This is the Latin translation of John 3:16, used here as the scriptural authority for the minister to announce the forgiveness of sins.</note> Therefore, as many of you as are displeased with yourselves and grieving for your sins, and trust that these are all and entirely forgiven you solely because of the merits of Christ, and have the purpose to bid farewell to sins more and more and to serve the Lord in true holiness and righteousness: to these, because they believe in the Son of God, I announce by the prescription of the divine word that they are loosed <note>The terms "loosed" and "retained" refer to the "Power of the Keys" (Matthew 16:19), the authority the Church believed it held to declare God's judgment or forgiveness.</note> in heaven from all their sins, as the Lord promised in the Gospel, on account of the full satisfaction <term>satisfactionem: a theological term meaning the "payment" or "reparation" made by Christ to satisfy divine justice</term> which our Lord Jesus Christ fulfilled by his most holy passion and death for our sake. 

But as many among you as still take delight in your sins and shameful deeds, or persist in sins against your conscience; to these I denounce by the mandate of God that the wrath and judgment of God hang over them, and that all their sins are retained in heaven, nor can they be freed in any way from eternal condemnation until they are converted. Since, therefore, we trust without doubt that we and our prayers are sanctified through the passion of Christ and are pleasing to God, let us invoke him from the depths of our hearts with these words:

> Almighty Lord, creator of heaven and earth: we give you thanks from our souls, because you both created us and have protected and nourished us along with our children until this day, and you deign to protect and rule us in the future as well. Above all, however, we give you thanks that you have granted us to know your Son Jesus Christ, promised of old in Paradise <note>This refers to the "Protoevangelium" or the first gospel promise in Genesis 3:15, where God promises that the offspring of the woman would crush the serpent's head.</note>, and have forgiven our sins because of his bitter passion and death. We further ask, O Lord, that by the preaching of your word and the efficacy of your Spirit, you would renew us more and more into the image of your Son: so that we may spend eternal life with you in both soul and body, and celebrate your great works— <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "bremus" at the top of the page suggests the final word is "celebremus" (let us celebrate).</note>

<meta>This page continues the formal prayer from the previous page, shifting from the personal confession of the individual to a collective plea on behalf of the whole congregation. It mirrors the structure of a liturgical confession used in the Reformed or Lutheran tradition.</meta>

...if you should indeed choose to look upon our worthiness—for our **conscience** <note>original: "συνείδησις" (syneidēsis); the internal sense of right and wrong that acts as a witness</note> accuses us and our **sins** <note>original: "ἁμαρτίαι" (hamartiai); literally "missing the mark" or falling short of God's standard</note> testify against us. Nor have you, being a just judge, remained hidden from us as the one who punishes the offenses of those who break the law against your commandments. Therefore, O Lord, when we consider our entire life, we find nothing in ourselves except only condemnation.

But O Lord, since by your unspeakable mercy you have commanded us in all our needs to call upon you alone, and have promised that you will hear our prayer—not because of our own worthiness, which is absolutely nothing, but because of the worthiness of our Lord **Jesus Christ**, whom you have appointed as our **mediator** and **advocate**—for this reason, having renounced all other help, we flee to your mercy alone.

First, then, O Lord, in addition to the countless benefits with which you continue to bless all people on earth in common, you have personally granted us such great and numerous favors that we are not even capable of expressing or contemplating them. Above all, having freed us from the most wretched slavery of Satan, you have deemed us worthy to call us to the knowledge of the holy **Gospel**. Having rescued us from the loathsome **idolatry** <note>original: "εἰδωλολατρείας" (eidōlolatreias); in the context of the Reformation, this frequently refers to the ritual practices of the Roman Catholic Church, which the author associates with the "Antichrist."</note> of the **Antichrist** in which we were wandering, you led us into the longed-for light of your truth.

Yet we, nonetheless, out of ungratefulness, have forgotten your benefits; and having turned away from you, we followed our own desires, not rendering to you the honor we owe. We have sinned, therefore, O Lord, and greatly provoked you to anger. Wherefore, if you wished to deal with us according to our merits, we would expect nothing else than the penalty of eternal death. For if we wished to defend ourselves, our conscience would come forward to accuse us, and our wickedness would testify against us.

And from the punishments into which we fall every day, we recognize that we are justly beaten by your rods; for being just, you punish no one without cause. Even now, we see your hand stretched out, ready to strike blows. And this is not unjust, O Father; for even if you punished us more bitterly than before,

...if you should indeed choose to look upon our worthiness—for our **conscience** <note>original: "conscientia"; the internal sense of guilt or awareness of one's actions before God</note> accuses us, and our **sins** <note>original: "peccata"</note> rebuke us by your own testimony. Nor is it hidden from us that you are a **just judge**, who punishes the offenses of those who break your commandments. Therefore, O Lord, if we were to consider our entire life, we would find nothing in ourselves except utter condemnation. 

But because, O Lord, in your inexpressible **mercy**, you have commanded us to call upon you alone in all our needs, and have promised that you will hear our prayers—not because of our own merits, which are absolutely nothing, but because of the merit of our Lord **Jesus Christ**, whom you have appointed as our **mediator** <note>A go-between or intercessor</note> and **advocate**—for this reason, having bid farewell to all other sources of help, we seek refuge in your mercy alone.

First, then, O Lord, in addition to the countless benefits which you bestow upon all people throughout the world, you have specifically graced us with such great and numerous favors that we can neither express them in words nor sufficiently grasp them in our thoughts. Above all, you have rescued us from the most wretched slavery of the devil, and have deigned to call us to the **knowledge of the holy Gospel**. Having also been led out from the abominable **idolatry of the Antichrist** <note>original: "Antichristi idololatria"; in the context of the Reformation, this typically refers to the rituals and authority of the Roman Catholic Church, which the authors viewed as a corruption of early Christianity</note>, in which we were wandering blindly, you have introduced us into the desirable light of your truth. 

We, however, have nonetheless forgotten your benefits through our ingratitude; having departed from you, we have followed our own desires and have not given you the honor we owed. And so we have sinned, Lord, and have gravely offended you. Wherefore, if you wished to treat us according to our merits, we could expect nothing else but the **punishment of eternal death**. For if we wished to excuse ourselves, our conscience would accuse us, and our own wickedness would bear witness against us. Furthermore, from the punishments we incur daily, we recognize that we are justly experiencing your rods <note>A biblical metaphor for divine discipline or correction</note>; for since you are just, you punish no one without cause. And even now, we see your hand extended to inflict punishments upon us—and not undeservedly, Father: for if you were to punish us even more bitterly than before...

<term>conscience</term>: the internal witness of one's moral state; <term>sins</term>: failings or transgressions against divine law; <term>just judge</term>: God's role as the arbiter of right and wrong; <term>mercy</term>: God's compassion toward the unworthy; <term>Jesus Christ</term>: the central figure of Christian salvation; <term>mediator</term>: one who acts as an intermediary; <term>advocate</term>: a legal intercessor or defender; <term>idolatry of the Antichrist</term>: a polemical term for Catholic practices; <term>knowledge of the Gospel</term>: understanding the "good news" of salvation; <term>punishment of eternal death</term>: the consequence of sin without grace.

...[you punished], and instead of one blow you had inflicted many; even if you were to visit upon us all the punishments with which you chastised your people **Israel**, you would not be treating us unjustly, nor would we object as if we were being punished without cause. Yet nevertheless, O Lord, you are our God, and we are but **dust and ashes** <note>original: χοῦς καὶ κόνις; a common biblical expression for human frailty and mortality</note>; you are our Creator, and we are the **work of your hands**; you are our Shepherd, and we are your flock; you are our Savior, and we are the people saved by you; you are our Father, and we are your **inheritance** <note>original: κληρός; often used for the portion or lot assigned to someone, here meaning God's chosen people</note>. 

Therefore, do not punish us in your anger, but gently **discipline** <note>original: σωφρόνισον; to bring someone to their senses or restore them to moderation</note> us; rather, as for the work you have begun in us, increase its progress through your **grace** <note>original: χάρις</note>, so that it may become manifest to the entire world that you are our God and Savior. 

Your people Israel often provoked you to anger with many great sins, and you justly pursued them with punishment; yet as often as they turned back to you, you received them with **mercy** <note>original: ἵλεως; gracious or propitious</note>. And no matter how great the number or magnitude of their offenses, you nevertheless turned away your wrath and the punishments that were about to fall upon them, because of the **covenant** <note>original: διαθήκη; a formal agreement or testament</note> established of old with your servants **Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob**. 

Through this covenant, you never once **rejected** <note>original: ἐξεκρούσω; literally "pushed away" or "struck out"</note> the prayers of your people. We, through your grace, possess this same covenant with you—one even more glorious and powerful than that of old—having been mediated by **Christ** our Savior, written with his own blood, and ratified by his suffering and death. 

Therefore, O Lord, having renounced ourselves and absolutely every other hope, we flee for refuge to this **covenant of your grace** alone. In this covenant, our Lord **Jesus Christ**, by offering his own body for us as a perfect sacrifice on the cross **once for all** <note>original: ἐφάπαξ; a key theological term emphasizing the finality of Christ's sacrifice</note>, has reconciled us to you for all eternity. 

Look, therefore, upon the **face of Christ**, and not upon the filth of our sins; so that, your wrath being softened through his intercessions, your face may **shine upon us** <note>original: ἐπιφαύῃ; to give light or dawn upon</note> for joy and salvation. And for what remains of our **watch** <note>original: φυλακῆς; often used for a period of time or a guard-duty, here likely referring to the remainder of earthly life or a specific liturgical hour</note>, having deemed us worthy by your **Spirit**...

## The Rites Performed Concerning
## Baptism.

Our help is from the Lord, who made heaven and earth. <note>This traditional opening is taken from Psalm 124:8 and is common in many Reformed liturgies.</note>

At the same time, he exhorts us to be humbled before God and to be displeased with ourselves; but through these things, he prepares us for a more intense longing for the grace of God, through which all the wickedness and curse of our old nature is wiped away. # THE CELEBRATION OF BAPTISM.

->Our help is from the Lord, who created<-
->heaven and earth.<-
<note>This opening sentence, known as the Votum, is based on Psalm 124:8 and has been a standard opening for Reformed worship services since the 16th century.</note>

<margin>John 3</margin>
Our Lord Jesus Christ says: "Unless one is born again, he cannot see the kingdom of God." By these words he declares that our nature is utterly perverse and cursed: and he partly exhorts us to humble ourselves before God and to be displeased with ourselves; partly he prepares us for a more vehement desire for the grace of God, through which all the wickedness and curse of our old nature is abolished; for we are not capable of divine grace until all [confidence] in our own strength, and either our own wisdom or the confidence <term>confidence</term> <note>original Latin fragment: iustitia fidu- (fiducia) — this refers to self-righteousness or trust in one's own moral standing before God.</note> in our own righteous...
