--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Form for the Administration of Baptism," specifically the theological explanation of the rite. The previous page introduced the necessity of the new birth (John 3); this page explains the spiritual significance of the Triune name used in baptism.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

...having completely cast out all fleshly confidence from our hearts, and having reached a total condemnation of all that is within us.

Christ, having set this misery before our eyes, then comforts us according to His mercy. In this, He promises to us and to our children a release from all our sins—that is, that He will not reckon our sins against us, because of the all-holy blood <note>original: πανάγιον αἷμα (panagion haima) — a term emphasizing the sacrificial purity of Christ's blood.</note> which He poured out for us. This is so that our nature might be renewed through the Holy Spirit into His own image; and so that not only the soul might be strengthened in this faith of the promise, but that the body also might receive a seal as a guarantee of this, He commanded us to be baptized in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit.

First, then, in that He wills us to be baptized into the name of the Father, it appears to us as a sworn oath <term>oath</term> <note>original: ὁρκωμοσία (horkōmosia) — a formal, solemn swearing of a promise, used here to describe God's commitment to the believer.</note> that throughout our entire life He confirms this: that God is the Father of both us and our descendants, and caring for us in all our bodily and spiritual needs, He turns all evils to our good. Since no creature, because of the covenant <term>covenant</term> <note>original: συνθήκην (synthēkēn) — the formal agreement or relationship between God and His people, central to Reformed theology.</note> which we have with God, is able to harm us, but all things are compelled to contribute to our salvation.

Next, in that He commands us to be baptized into the name of the Son, He promises that whatever the Son of God has done and suffered, all these things are our own property; and that He is the Savior for both us and our children. He bestows His saving grace upon us; by His holy conception, birth, passion, and death, He has rescued us from all filth and sin. Having removed the curse from us, nailing it to the cross, and washing it away with His own blood, He buried it with Himself. Furthermore, He has freed us from the punishment of hell, so that through His resurrection from the dead and His ascent into heaven, He might clothe us in His own righteousness <note>original: δικαιοσύνῃ (dikaiosynē) — the legal and moral standing of being "right" with God, granted through Christ.</note>. Now He stands in the heavens for us before the Father in glory, and in the final judgment, He will present us spotless and glorious before the Father.

<summary>This page explains the theological meaning of being baptized in the name of the Father and the Son, framing it as a divine promise of fatherly care and the legal imputation of Christ's merits to the believer.</summary>
<keywords>Baptism, Father, Son, Covenant, Salvation, Righteousness, Atonement, Reformed Liturgy</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Form for the Administration of Baptism," picking up mid-sentence from the previous discussion on human misery and the need for repentance. It explains the theological significance of the Trinity in the rite of baptism.</meta>

<page-num>150</page-num>
<sig>L</sig>

...having completely cast out all fleshly confidence from our hearts, we may clearly condemn all things that are within us.

With this misery set before our eyes, Christ then comforts us according to his mercy, while promising to us and to our children that he will wash us from every sin—that is, that he will not count our sins against us <term>non-imputation</term> <note>The text uses the phrase "non imputaturum esse," referring to the theological concept where God chooses not to "count" or "reckon" a person's sins against their record because of Christ's sacrifice.</note> on account of his most holy blood, which he shed for us. He also promises to renew our nature through the Holy Spirit into his own image. And so that not only the soul might be strengthened in the faith of this promise, but that the body also might receive a clear seal <term>seal</term> <note>In Reformed theology, a "seal" (sigillum) is a visible sign—like the water in baptism—that guarantees the invisible reality of God's promises to the believer.</note> of it, he commanded us to be baptized in the name of the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit.

First, therefore, while he wills us to be baptized into the name of the Father, he testifies this to us through our whole life—almost like a visible oath—that God is a father to us and to our descendants. He takes up the care of us in all needs of both body and soul, turning all evils into good for us; for no creature can harm us in any way because of the covenant <term>covenant</term> <note>original: foedus — the formal, binding relationship between God and His people.</note> which stands between us and God, but rather all things are compelled to contribute to our salvation.

Next, while he commands us to be baptized into the name of the Son, he promises that all that the Son of God both did and suffered is our very own; and that he is the Savior to us and our children. He anoints us with his saving grace, and by his holy conception, birth, passion, and death, he has freed us from all filth and sins. He has taken the curse <term>curse</term> <note>original: maledictionem — referring to the divine penalty for sin which, according to this theology, Christ took upon himself on the cross.</note> away from us and nailed it to the cross; and having soon wiped it out with his blood, he buried it with himself; so that through his resurrection from the dead and ascension into heaven, he might clothe us in his righteousness, and even now might intercede for us in heaven before the Father, and at the final judgment might present us spotless and glorious before the Father.

The third point is this: in being baptized into the name of the Holy Spirit, we receive this promise: that the Holy Spirit will be a guide and a comforter <term>comforter</term> <note>original: παράκλητος (paraklētos) — a term often used for the Holy Spirit, meaning one who is called alongside to help, advocate, or console.</note> for us and for our children forever. He makes us true members of the body of Christ, so that we may henceforth have fellowship with the church, the members of Christ, and all his good gifts. Thus, our sin is surrendered to eternal oblivion <note>original: ἀμνηστίᾳ (amnēstia) — a "forgetting" or legal pardon where the offense is no longer remembered.</note>, and as the remnants of sin and weakness are continually put to death, the Spirit becomes the beginning of a new life within us. Finally, in the resurrection of the dead—wherein our flesh shall be made like the glorious body of the Lord—its perfection will shine forth in full.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Since in every covenant <term>covenant</term> <note>original: συνθήκη (synthēkē) — a formal agreement or contract between two parties. In this context, it refers to the mutual promises made between God and the believer during baptism.</note> each party provides a guarantee, we also promise to God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit—through His grace—that we will acknowledge and confess Him alone as our one and true God. We promise to call upon Him alone in all our needs and to obey Him as obedient children, just as this rebirth <term>rebirth</term> <note>original: παλιγγενεσία (palingenesia) — often translated as regeneration; the spiritual renewal of an individual.</note> requires. This obedience consists of two parts: first, that through true repentance <term>repentance</term> <note>original: μετάνοια (metanoia) — a change of mind and heart involving grief over past sins.</note> and sorrow for our sins, we should deny all our own thoughts and desires, submit ourselves to the will of God, and turn away from and flee all sins from the bottom of our souls. Second, we shall make it our purpose and endeavor to live according to the word of God in all holiness and righteousness.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But if we should ever fall into sins through weakness, we will not remain in them, nor will we despair and seek the forgiveness of sins through any other means outside of Jesus Christ. Instead, we shall be reminded each time by our baptism to renounce our sins, firmly believing that every memory of them will be blotted out before God through the shedding of Christ’s blood. For holy baptism is an unwavering symbol for us of the eternal covenant with God, assuring us that into the living fountain of the everlasting...

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the baptismal liturgy found on page 151. While the previous page provided a Greek translation, this page contains the Latin version of the same rite, detailing the role of the Holy Spirit and the reciprocal obligations of the baptismal covenant.</meta>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<sig>L 2</sig>

Thirdly, when we are baptized into the name of the Holy Spirit, this promise is given to us: that the Holy Spirit will be a Teacher and a Comforter <term>comforter</term> <note>original: *consolatorem* — a translation of the Greek *parakletos*, referring to one who helps, counsels, and strengthens.</note> for both us and our children for all ages to come. He makes us true members of the body of Christ so that, together with the other members of the church, we may enter into the communion of Christ and all his benefits. Our sin is surrendered to eternal oblivion <note>original: *aeternae oblivioni* — meaning the sins are completely forgotten by God and no longer held against the believer.</note>, and as the remains of our sins and weaknesses are more and more put to death <term>put to death</term> <note>original: *enecatis* — this refers to the theological concept of "mortification," where the believer actively works to suppress their sinful impulses through the power of the Spirit.</note>, a certain beginning of a new life is now created within us. Finally, in the resurrection of the dead—when our flesh shall be made like the glorious body of the Lord—the Spirit's completed perfection will shine forth.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But because in every covenant <term>covenant</term> <note>original: *foedere* — a formal, binding agreement between two parties; here, the sacred contract between God and the believer.</note> each party makes a promise, we also promise to God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit that, through His grace, we will acknowledge and confess Him alone as our one and true God. We promise to call upon Him alone in all our needs and to obey Him as obedient children, just as this rebirth <term>rebirth</term> <note>original: *regeneratio* — the spiritual renewal or "being born again" that baptism symbolizes.</note> demands. This obedience consists of two parts: First, that through true repentance <term>repentance</term> <note>original: *poenitentiam* — a turning away from sin and toward God.</note> and sorrow over our sins, we deny all the impulses and desires of our own reason and subject ourselves to the will of God, turning away from and fleeing all sins from the heart. The second is that with all the effort of our purpose and zeal, we strive to live in all holiness and justice according to the word of God.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If, however, we should ever fall into sins because of our weakness, we shall not cling to them, nor shall we seek the forgiveness of sins through anyone else outside of Christ with a despairing heart. Instead, we shall be continually reminded through our baptism to stop sinning, firmly believing that every memory of those sins will be blotted out before God through the blood shed by Christ. For baptism is to us an indubitable sign of our eternal covenant with God, testifying to us that we [are grafted] into the living fountain of perennial...

<meta>This page continues the Greek translation of the baptismal liturgy. While the previous page provided the Latin text of the promises made by the Holy Spirit and the believer, this page shifts to a scriptural defense of infant baptism, grounding the practice in the covenant made with Abraham and the teachings of Jesus and Peter.</meta>

<page-num>153</page-num>

...through the compassion of the Father, and through the most holy passion and death of the Lord Jesus, and by the power of the Holy Spirit, we were baptized.

Even though our children are not yet able to understand these mysteries <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: μυστήρια (*mystēria*) — in this context, the sacred rites or sacraments that represent spiritual truths.</note> nor make a public confession of faith due to their age, we should not exclude them from holy baptism for this reason. For God has called them into His covenant <term>covenant</term> <note>original: διαθήκην (*diathēkēn*) — a formal, binding agreement between God and humanity; here, specifically the "Covenant of Grace."</note> just as He did long ago with Abraham, the father of all believers, and his offspring—and consequently, He has also made this arrangement with us and our children:

> "I will establish," says the Lord, "my covenant between me and you, and between your offspring after you, throughout their generations, as an everlasting covenant, to be your God and the God of your offspring after you." <note>original: Genesis 17:7</note>

Nor did our Lord Jesus come into the world to diminish the Father's grace; rather, He greatly extended the covenant of grace—which was previously restricted to the Israelite nation alone—throughout the entire world. In place of circumcision, He established baptism as a symbol and seal of the covenant for us and for our children. The blessed Peter clearly declares this confirmation of the covenant in the *Acts of the Apostles*, in the second chapter, where he says:

> "Repent, and let each of you be baptized in the name of Jesus Christ for the forgiveness of sins, and you will receive the gift of the Holy Spirit; for the promise is to you and to your children, and to all who are far off—as many as the Lord our God shall call." <note>original: Acts 2:38-39</note>

Furthermore, the Lord Jesus himself commands that infants <term>infants</term> <note>original: νήπια βρέφη (*nēpia brephē*) — literally "speechless babies," emphasizing their total dependency and youth.</note> be brought to Him, promising them the kingdom of heaven through His words and actions, as it is written in the tenth chapter of the *Gospel according to Mark*:

> "And they were bringing children to him so that he might touch them; but the disciples rebuked those who brought them. But when Jesus saw it, he was indignant and said to them: 'Let the children come to me and do not hinder them; for to such belongs the kingdom of God. Amen, I say to you: Whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a child shall never enter into it...'" <note>original: Mark 10:13-15</note>

...them, and embracing them, placing his hands upon them, he blessed them. <note>original: "ηὐλόγει αὐτά" — This refers to the Gospel of Mark 10:16, where Jesus welcomes the little children.</note> From these words we clearly learn that our children also share in the kingdom of God and in His **Covenant**; and for this reason, they are rightly entitled to receive **Baptism** as the seal of the Covenant <term>seal of the Covenant</term> <note>original: "Διαθήκης σφραγίσματος" — A "seal" was a legal or royal mark of ownership; here it signifies that the child belongs to God's family.</note>, even if they are not yet able to understand the mysteries of baptism because of their age. ->**The Prayer**<-

Almighty, eternal God, who according to your righteous judgment punished the unbelieving and unrepentant world through the flood, but in your great mercy saved the faithful Noah—the eighth person—bringing him through the water; and who drowned the hardened <note>original: "ἐκπεπυρωμένον" — literally "ignited" or "set on fire," here implying a heart hardened or inflamed with pride.</note> Pharaoh with all his hosts, but led your people Israel through the sea on dry feet—which was a type and foreshadowing <term>type and foreshadowing</term> <note>original: "τύπος ἦν ὑποτύπωσις" — in theology, a "type" is an Old Testament event that points toward a New Testament reality, like the Red Sea pointing to Baptism.</note> of **Baptism**: we beseech you, Lord, through your immeasurable mercy, to look kindly upon this infant. Through your Holy Spirit, incorporate <note>original: "ἐνσωμάτωσον" — literally "embody into," meaning to make the child a part of the Body of Christ.</note> this child into your Son Jesus Christ; so that, being buried with Him into His death, and rising with Him to a new life, the child may joyfully take up his cross and follow Him daily. May the child cling to Him with true faith, firm hope, and warm love, so that at the end, having courageously left this life—which differs in no way from death—for your sake, he may stand without fear before the judgment seat of your Son Jesus on the last day. Through your Son, our Lord, who with you and the Holy Spirit lives and reigns as one God, forever and ever, Amen.

Amen.

Beloved in the Lord Jesus; since you have undertaken the care of this infant, you must constantly reflect on this: that our God is most true, and he demands of us also that we live for him in truth. For this reason, it is necessary that you, the relatives and kin, and especially the parents and godparents <term>compatres|literally "co-parents," the sponsors who share the responsibility for the child's spiritual life</term>, give every effort with all zeal so that this infant may be brought up for the Lord Jesus Christ in the sincere knowledge and fear of God, according to the articles of the Christian faith and the doctrine revealed by God himself and contained in the Old and New Testaments. # PREPARATION FOR THE HOLY
## Supper of the Lord.

*Since the word of God sets these three things before our eyes. First, our sins; then, our redemption; third, the gratitude which is owed by us to God for these things: let each one consider in their mind the sum of God’s commandments:* **You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart, with all your soul, and with all your strength; and your neighbor as yourself:** *in which the will of God is explained to us. ## The practices concerning the Lord’s Supper.

Beloved in the Lord, listen to how the Lord Jesus himself ordained his holy supper word for word, just as the blessed Paul remembers in the first letter to the Corinthians, writing thus in the eleventh chapter: For I, he says, received from the Lord what I also delivered to you, that the Lord Jesus, on the night in which he was betrayed, took bread; and after giving thanks, he broke it and said: "Take, eat; this is my body which is broken for you; do this in remembrance of me." Likewise also the cup after they had supped, saying: "This cup is the new covenant in my blood; do this, as often as you drink it, in remembrance of me." For as often as you eat this bread and drink this cup, you proclaim the death of the Lord until he comes. So that whoever eats this bread or drinks the cup of the Lord unworthily shall be guilty of the body and blood of the Lord. But let a person examine himself, and so let him eat of the bread and drink of the cup; for he who eats and drinks unworthily eats and drinks judgment to himself, not discerning the body of the Lord.

In order that we may perform the Lord’s supper for our saving comfort <note>original: *paramythian* — comfort, encouragement, or consolation</note>, it is necessary above all else that we first examine ourselves accurately. Then, we must focus on the purpose set forth by Christ: his remembrance <note>original: *anamnesin* — a Greek liturgical term meaning more than just "memory"; it implies making a past event present and active again</note>.

Our accurate self-examination, therefore, consists in these three things:

First, let each person reflect within themselves on their own sins, and the cleansing from them; so that they...

## THE CE-
## LEBRATION of the Lord's Supper.

Beloved in the Lord, hear the words by which the Lord Jesus instituted his holy supper; as indeed the *blessed Paul relates in the first epistle to the Corinthians, in the eleventh chapter, writing thus:*

*For I received from the Lord that which I also delivered to you; namely, that the Lord Jesus, on that night in which he was betrayed, took bread: and when he had given thanks, he broke it and said, "Take, eat; this is my body, which is broken for you: do this in remembrance of me." Likewise also the cup after they had supped, saying, "This cup is the new testament <note>The Latin 'testamentum' can also be translated as 'covenant,' representing the new agreement between God and humanity.</note> in my blood: Do this, as often as you shall drink it, in remembrance of me." For as often as you shall eat this bread, and drink this cup, you proclaim the death of the Lord until he comes. Therefore, whoever eats this bread, or drinks this cup of the Lord unworthily, shall be held guilty of the body and blood of the Lord. But let everyone examine himself; and so let him eat of that bread, and drink of that cup. For he who eats and drinks unworthily, eats and drinks condemnation to himself, not discerning the Lord’s body.*

*Therefore, so that we may celebrate the Lord’s supper for our saving comfort, it is necessary above all else that we first examine ourselves thoroughly; then, we should strive toward that goal which Christ set before us: his remembrance <note>original: 'recordationem'—referring to the Greek concept of 'anamnesis,' an active, communal remembering of Christ's sacrifice.</note>.*

*The thorough examination of ourselves consists of these three things.*

*First, let everyone reflect in their own mind upon their sins and the curse due to them; so that they may...*

<sig>M 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...be displeased with themselves [displicens],</meta>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Exhortation" or instruction for the Lord's Supper. Having quoted St. Paul’s warning about eating and drinking unworthily on the previous page, the text now details the three specific ways a believer should examine themselves before approaching the table.</meta>

<page-num>169</page-num>

...displeased with himself, let him humble himself before God. Consider how great is the wrath of God against sin, seeing that rather than leaving it unpunished, he saw fit to punish it in his own Son, Jesus Christ, through the bitter and shameful death of the cross. <note>The author emphasizes that the "bitter" nature of Christ's death is the ultimate proof of God's justice and the seriousness of human sin.</note>

"Next, let each person examine their own heart to see whether they receive this promise with firm faith—namely, that all their sins are forgiven solely through the suffering and death of Christ, and that the perfect righteousness of Christ is granted to them and credited <note>original: *epilogizesthai* – a term from accounting or logic meaning "to reckon" or "to credit to one's account."</note> as their own, just as if they had personally paid the penalty for all their sins and fulfilled all requirements of the law.

"Third, let each person examine their own mind to see whether they are determined, for the rest of their life, to be thankful to God and to walk sincerely before him; and further, whether they sincerely renounce all enmity, hatred, and envy, and have a firm intention to live hereafter in true love and harmony with their neighbor.

"All those, therefore, who are so minded, God certainly deems worthy of his grace, and he welcomes them as worthy guests to the banquet of his Son.

"On the contrary, those who do not have the witness of this feeling in their hearts eat and drink judgment to themselves. Therefore, according to the command of the Lord Christ and the apostle Paul, we forbid the Lord's Table <note>original: *kyriakē trapeza* – a standard term for the altar or communion table.</note> to all those who are conscious of the following offenses, declaring to them that they have no part in the kingdom of Christ: I mean all idolaters; all those who pray to <note>original: *theoklytousi* – literally "calling upon as gods."</note> saints, or angels, or any other creatures; those who worship carved images or icons; those who practice magic or divination; those who cast spells <note>original: *epōdas* – incantations or charms used in folk medicine or sorcery.</note> upon cattle, people, or any sick thing, or who provide such spells; all those who despise God, his word, and the holy mysteries <term>mysteries: a traditional term for the Sacraments, specifically Baptism and the Lord's Supper</term>; all blasphemers; all those who attempt to cause factions or syncretism <note>original: *synkrētismous* – here referring to the unauthorized blending of different religious beliefs or causing schisms within the church or state</note> in the church or the state; and all perjurers.

<summary>This page outlines the requirements for receiving Communion: repentance for sin, faith in Christ’s imputed righteousness, and a commitment to lead a life of love. It concludes by "fencing the table," listing those—such as idolaters and sorcerers—who are barred from the sacrament.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific instructions for the "Exhortation" before the Lord's Supper. It concludes the three steps of self-examination and begins the "fencing of the table"—a formal warning to those living in unrepentant sin to abstain from the sacrament.</meta>

<sig>M 3</sig>

...displeasing to himself, let him humble himself before God, because the wrath of God against sin is so great that, rather than letting it go unpunished, he willed to punish it in his Son Jesus Christ by the bitter and shameful death of the cross. <note>In the 16th-century context, the "shameful" (*infamis*) nature of the cross referred to it being a death reserved for the lowest criminals, emphasizing the depth of Christ's humiliation.</note>

Next, let each person examine their heart to see whether they embrace this promise with firm faith: namely, that all their sins are forgiven solely on account of Christ’s suffering and death, and that Christ’s perfect righteousness is granted and credited to them as their own, <note>original: *imputari* — to be "reckoned" or "accounted" to someone's credit, a central concept in Reformation theology regarding how a believer is seen as righteous before God.</note> just as if they themselves had fully paid the penalty for all their sins and fulfilled every requirement of justice.

Third, let each person search their soul to see whether, from this point forward, they desire to show themselves grateful to God throughout their whole life and to walk sincerely before him; and furthermore, whether they have sincerely renounced all enmity, hatred, and envy, and have firmly resolved to spend the rest of their life in true love and harmony with their neighbor.

Whoever, therefore, possesses this mindset, God without doubt deems them worthy of his grace, and receives them as worthy guests at the banquet of his Son.

On the contrary, as many as do not feel this testimony in their hearts, they eat and drink judgment to themselves. For which reason, according to the command of the Lord Christ and the apostle Paul, we bar from the Lord’s table <note>original: *Domini mensa*. This practice is often called "fencing the table," where the minister warns the unrepentant that the bread and wine provide no benefit to those without faith.</note> all those who are conscious of the following sins, declaring to them that they have no part in the kingdom of Christ: namely, idolaters; all who call upon <note>original: *inuocant* — to pray to or summon.</note> saints, angels, or other creatures; all who worship statues or images; all who practice magic and divination; all who whisper spells over humans, cattle, or any other things, or place their faith in such incantations; <note>This refers to folk magic and "white" witchcraft common in rural areas, which the reformers viewed as a violation of the first commandment.</note> all who hold God, his Word, and the venerable sacraments in contempt; all blasphemers; all who stir up factions in the Church or the State; all perjurers; all who [disobey] parents—

<meta>catchword: bus suis (to parents)</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the final criteria for a worthy participant in the Lord's Supper—faith in Christ's imputed righteousness and a desire for holy living—while listing specific categories of sinners, such as idolaters and practitioners of magic, who are forbidden from participating.</summary>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues the "fencing of the table" from page 170. It transitions from warning unrepentant sinners to comforting those who feel their own unworthiness, emphasizing that the Lord's Supper is a remedy for the weak, not a reward for the perfect. It then begins the section on the purpose of the sacrament: the remembrance of Christ.</meta>

<page-num>171</page-num>

...oaths; all those who rebel against parents or superiors; all murderers, brawlers, and quarrelers; all those who persist in hatred and envy against their neighbor; all adulterers, fornicators, drunkards, thieves, usurers, <note>original: δανεισταῖς — while it literally means "lenders," in this historical context it refers to predatory moneylending or usury, which was considered a grave sin.</note> extortioners, gamblers, and the covetous; and all those who lead a scandalous life. All these people, as long as they persist in such sins, must remember to abstain from this food, which Christ ordained only for the faithful, lest they bring upon themselves a heavier judgment and condemnation.

However, beloved, we do not give this warning for the purpose of completely disheartening the broken hearts of the faithful, <note>A "broken heart" in this context refers to the "contrite heart" of a believer who is deeply sorry for their sins.</note> as if thinking that only those who happen to be entirely sinless are permitted to participate in the Lord’s table. For we do not come to this supper to testify that we are perfect and righteous in ourselves. On the contrary, because we seek our life in Christ, we acknowledge that we lie in the midst of death. Therefore, even though we still feel many weaknesses and miseries within ourselves—such as not having perfect faith, and not being as eager to serve God with as much zeal as we ought, but being forced daily to struggle against the weakness of our faith and the evil desires of our flesh—nevertheless, since by the grace of the Holy Spirit we are grieved by these shortcomings from our very soul and we repent of them, and we are eager to fight against unbelief with our whole heart and long to keep all the commandments of God, we therefore remain firm in this conviction: that no sin, nor any weakness that still remains in us against our will, can shut us out from the grace of God or make us unworthy to be partakers of this heavenly food and drink.

Let us now consider, secondly, for what purpose the Lord ordained this supper: namely, for his own remembrance. <note>original: ἀνάμνησιν (*anamnesis*) — This refers to a "remembrance" that is more than just a mental memory; in a liturgical sense, it is a making-present of Christ’s sacrifice for the believer.</note>

We shall, therefore, perform the remembrance of him in this same ceremony in this way: first, let this be firmly settled in our hearts...

<vocab>oaths, parents, the Lord's table, sinless, supper, weaknesses, remembrance</vocab>

It then introduces the second purpose of the sacrament: the spiritual remembrance of Christ's sacrifice.</summary>

*Furthermore, beloved, we do not give these warnings so that the contrite hearts of the faithful should be utterly cast down, as if the Lord’s table were permitted only to those who are entirely without any sin. For we do not come to this supper to testify that we are perfect and righteous in ourselves; but on the contrary, because we seek life outside of ourselves in Christ, we acknowledge that we lie in the midst of death. Therefore, even if we still feel many weaknesses and miseries in ourselves—such as not having perfect faith, nor striving to worship God with as much zeal as is fitting, but being forced daily to struggle with the weakness of faith and the evil desires of the flesh—nevertheless, because by the grace of the Holy Spirit we are heartily sorry and repent for these vices, and we strive with our whole heart to overcome our lack of trust and desire to live according to all God's commandments; we shall remain firm in this confidence: that no sin, nor any weakness from among those that still cling to us against our will, can exclude us from the grace of God or make us unworthy partakers of this heavenly food and drink.*

*In the second place, let us now also consider for what purpose the Lord instituted His Supper: namely, for the remembrance of Himself.*

*We shall therefore establish the remembrance of Him in this act in this way: for first, this shall settle firmly in our hearts...*

<meta>catchword: as often [as]</meta>

[our] persuasion <note>original: πεποίθησις (pepoithēsis) — also translated as confidence or assurance.</note> is in our Lord Jesus Christ and the promise given to the patriarchs from the beginning. He was sent into the world by the Father, taking upon Himself our flesh and blood. He endured on earth in our place the wrath of God—under which we would have been submerged forever—from the beginning of His incarnation <note>original: ἐνανθρωπήσεως (enanthrōpēseōs) — the act of God becoming human.</note> until the end of His life. He fully fulfilled in our stead the obedience owed to the law and to the righteousness of God; this was especially true when the weight of our sins and of God's wrath squeezed from Him the bloody sweat <note>original: θρομβώδη ἱδρῶτα (thrombōdē hidrōta) — clots or thick drops of sweat like blood, as recorded in the Garden of Gethsemane.</note> in the garden. There He was bound, so that we might be released from our chains. He endured countless insults so that we might be freed from insults forever. Though innocent, He was condemned to death, so that we might be acquitted in the judgment of God. 

He gave up His blessed body to be crucified <note>original: ἀνασκολοπίσαι (anaskolopisai) — to fix to a stake or crucify.</note>, so that He might nail the legal bond of our sins <note>original: χειρόγραφον (cheirographon) — a handwritten record of debt or a certificate of indebtedness.</note> to the cross, and thus transfer the curse from us onto Himself, so that He might fill us with His blessing. He descended into the deepest dishonor and the extreme anguish of both body and soul when He cried out with a loud voice, "My God, my God, why have You forsaken me?" <note>Matthew 27:46; Mark 15:34.</note> This was done so that we might be received by God and never be forsaken by Him. Finally, by His own death and the shedding of His blood, He ratified the new and eternal covenant of grace and reconciliation, which is our sign, when He cried out: "It is finished." <note>original: τετέλεσται (tetelestai) — it is completed, or the debt is paid in full.</note>

So that we may believe without doubting that we participate in this covenant, the Lord, at the last supper with His disciples, took bread and gave thanks; and having broken it, He gave it to the disciples and said: "Take, eat; this is my body, which is given for you; do this in remembrance of me." Likewise also He took the cup after supper, gave thanks, and gave it to them, saying: "Take and drink from it, all of you; this cup is the new covenant in my blood, which is shed for you and for many for the forgiveness of sins; do this as often [as]..."

[our] persuasion <note>original: persuasio — here meaning a deep-seated conviction or firm assurance of faith.</note>, that our Lord Jesus Christ, according to the promise made to the patriarchs from the beginning, was sent by the Father into this world; and having taken on our flesh and blood, He sustained for us on earth the wrath of God—under which we would have been submerged forever—from the beginning of His life among men until the end of His life. He perfectly fulfilled for us the obedience owed to the law and to the righteousness of God, especially when the burden of our sins and of divine wrath squeezed from Him a bloody sweat in the garden. There He offered Himself to be bound, so that we might be released from our bonds; and He endured countless insults, so that we might be freed from insults forever. Though innocent, He was condemned to death, so that we might be acquitted <note>original: indemnes demitteremur — literally, "dismissed unharmed" or "sent away without loss."</note> in the judgment of God. 

He gave up His blessed body to be crucified, so that He might nail the record of our sins' debt <note>original: chirographum — a handwritten bond or legal certificate of indebtedness. This refers to the idea that sin is a debt we cannot pay.</note> to the cross. Thus He transferred the curse from us onto Himself, so that He might fill us with His blessing. He lowered Himself to the deepest infamy and the extreme agonies of both body and soul, when He cried out with a loud voice, <margin>Mark 15</margin> *“My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?”* so that we, having been accepted by God, might never be abandoned by Him. And finally, by His death and the shedding of His blood, He ratified this new and everlasting testament <note>The word "testament" here refers to a will or a solemn covenant that grants an inheritance.</note>, namely the covenant of grace and reconciliation, when He cried out: *“It is finished.”*

...[as often] as you drink, in my remembrance; that is, as often as you eat of this bread and drink of this cup. Through this, as a reliable memorial and a pledge <note>original: enechyron — a legal term for a security or collateral given to guarantee a promise.</note>, you shall be reminded and fully assured of this inward love and faithfulness of mine toward you. For your sake—who otherwise were destined to perish in eternal death—I deliver my body unto death upon the cross and pour out my blood. Your starving and parched souls I nourish and provide drink for, using my crucified body and shed blood, leading to eternal life. This is as true as the fact that this bread is broken before each of your eyes, this cup is shared with you, and you eat and drink these things with your mouth in my remembrance.

**T**hrough this ordinance of the holy supper, the Lord directs our faith and our confidence toward his perfect offering—which was accomplished once and for all upon the cross—as the sole foundation of our salvation. There, indeed, true food and drink for eternal life was established for our starving and parched souls. For by his death, he put away sin (the cause of our eternal hunger and thirst) and called upon the life-giving Spirit for us. This was done so that through this Spirit—dwelling in Christ as the head, and in us as his members—we might have true communion <note>original: koinōnia — a deep, participatory fellowship or sharing.</note> with him, and become partakers of all his goods: his righteousness, eternal life, and glory. Furthermore, it was done so that through his Spirit, as members of one body, we might be joined to one another in true love, as is fitting for brothers. As the blessed Paul says: <margin>1 Corinthians 10:17</margin> "We, though many, are one bread and one body, for we all partake of the one bread." For just as one flour is ground from many grains and baked into a single loaf of bread, and as one wine flows and is mixed together from many grapes pressed at the same time, so also we—who have been incorporated into Christ by true faith—should, through brotherly love and in imitation and zeal for the Savior, [show] so...

<meta>This page continues the liturgy of the Lord's Supper, moving from the prayer of preparation to the physical act of distribution and the concluding thanksgiving. It focuses on the "Sursum Corda" (lifting up of hearts) and the use of Scripture during the administration of the elements.</meta>

In order that we may be nourished by Christ, the truly heavenly bread <note>original: "ἐρανίῳ ἄρτῳ" (a variant of οὐρανίῳ) — referring to Christ as the "Bread of Life" from John 6.</note>, let us not focus only on the bread and wine set before our eyes, but let us lift up our hearts and faith on high to heaven, where Jesus Christ intercedes for us at the right hand of the Father. This is the summary of the Christian faith <note>original: "κεφάλαιον" — the "head" or "chief point" of the creed regarding Christ's ascension.</note> which declares Him to be in the heavens. We do not doubt that our souls are thus truly nourished and given drink by His body and blood through the working of the Holy Spirit, just as we receive the blessed bread and wine in remembrance of Him.

After this, the preacher of the Word <term>preacher of the Word: a common Reformed title for the minister, emphasizing the central role of biblical teaching</term>, breaking the Lord's bread for each person, distributes it, saying: *The bread which we break is the communion <note>original: "κοινωνία" — meaning a deep fellowship or shared participation.</note> of the body of Christ.* And the assistant <note>original: "συμπότης" — literally "fellow-drinker" or "guest," here referring to the person assisting the minister with the cup.</note> offering the cup, declares: *The cup of blessing which we bless is the communion of the blood of Christ.*

While the communion is being carried out—depending on the size of the congregation and the custom of each parish—either holy psalms are sung, or certain chapters appropriate for the remembrance of the death of Christ are read, such as John 14, 15, 16, 18, and Isaiah 53. From these, those passages shall be kept which are most fitting and beneficial for the church.

After the completion of the communion, the preacher of the Word shall add this exhortation:

Beloved in the Lord, since the Lord has now graciously fed and given drink to our souls at His own table, let us all with one mind glorify His venerable name with appropriate blessing, and let each one silently harmonize within themselves with a psalm:

> Bless the Lord, O my soul, and all that is within me, bless His holy name. Bless the Lord, O my soul, and forget not all His benefits; who forgives all your iniquities, who heals all your diseases; who redeems your life from corruption, who crowns you with mercy and com— <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence; the catchword "με" at the bottom suggests the next page begins with "mercies" (Greek: οἰκτιρμοῖς).</note>

*So that we may be truly fed by Jesus Christ, the heavenly bread, let us not focus our attention on the bread and wine set before our eyes, but let us lift our hearts and faith upward into the heavens; where Jesus Christ intercedes for us at the right hand of the heavenly Father; where also the articles of the Christian faith <note>This refers to the Apostles' Creed, specifically the section stating Christ "sitteth at the right hand of God the Father Almighty."</note> declare him to sit. After this, the minister of the word <term>minister of the word: a common Reformed title for a pastor, emphasizing their primary duty to preach and teach the Bible</term> distributes the bread of the Lord to each person, breaking it and adding this commendation: *The bread which we break is the sharing <note>original: "communicatio" — often translated as "communion" or "participation," implying a spiritual union with Christ.</note> in the body of Christ.* And his colleague or the assistant offering the cup adds this same commendation: *The cup of blessing which we bless is the sharing in the blood of Christ.*

Meanwhile, while the communion is being performed—depending on the number of those taking part and the convenience of each parish—either sacred songs are sung, or certain chapters of scripture that aid the memory of Christ’s death are read, such as *John 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, and Isaiah 53.* <note>These chapters focus on Christ’s final discourse to his disciples and the prophecy of the "Suffering Servant."</note> Whichever of these is more suitable or helpful for the church shall be observed.

For if, being enemies, we were reconciled to God through the death of his Son, much more, having been reconciled, shall we be saved by his life. <note>Romans 5:8-10</note> Therefore also my mouth and my heart shall proclaim the praise of the Lord from now and forever.

-> Or in this way: <-

Almighty God, compassionate Father; we thank you from all our heart, that out of unutterable mercy you have provided and granted to us your only-begotten Son as mediator and offering for our sin, and moreover as food and drink for eternal life; and furthermore you supply to us true faith, through which we partake of these your benefits, and for the strengthening of this faith you ordained the holy supper <term>holy supper: the Eucharist or Communion, viewed here as a spiritual nourishment for the believer’s faith</term> of this through your Son. Grant therefore, most faithful Father, that through the working of your Spirit, as we remember our Lord Jesus Christ and proclaim his death, we may progress more every day in true faith and the saving communion of Christ, through the same your Son Jesus Christ, Amen.

# THE RITE CONCERNING THE MARITAL

->union.<-

Since various misfortunes and afflictions meet those joined in marriage because of sins, in order that you—A and B—who are present here seeking to have your union confirmed before the church in the name of God, may receive in your hearts the full assurance of God’s unhesitating assistance in your tribulations, learn from the divine oracles how honorable marriage is, being a divine and God-pleasing state. For this reason, God desires to bless and guard those who are wed, but to punish adulterers and the immoral. <note>original: "πόρνους" (pornous) — generally referring to those practicing sexual immorality or fornication outside of marriage.</note>

First, therefore, know that God our Father, after completing the heavens and the earth and all things in them, also created man according to his image and likeness, to rule over the land animals, the creatures of the water, and the birds of the heaven. And after the creation of the male, because no helper like him was found among the various kinds of animals, the Lord God said: "It is not good for the man to be alone; let us make a helper suitable for him." And the Lord God brought a trance upon Adam, and while he slept, He took one of his ribs and filled up the place with flesh; and the rib which He had taken from Adam, He built into a woman and led her to Adam. And Adam said: "This now is bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh; she shall be called Woman, because she was taken out of her man." For this reason, a man shall leave his father and his mother and shall be joined to his wife; and the two shall become one flesh.

You should not doubt, therefore, whether the marital union is from God; for since God himself created the woman for Adam and, acting as a <term>bridal escort</term> <note>original: "νυμφαγωγὸς" — a person who leads the bride from her parents' house to the groom; here, God is depicted as the one personally leading the first bride to her husband.</note>, brought her to him and joined them, He demonstrates by this <term>mystery</term> <note>original: "μυστηρίῳ" — used here to mean a sacred or symbolic act that reveals a divine truth.</note> that even until now, He brings to each person their own spouse as if by His own hand. Furthermore, the Lord Jesus deigned to honor the marital union in Cana of Galilee <note>Cana is the site of Jesus' first recorded miracle, where he turned water into wine at a wedding feast (John 2:1-11).</note> by His presence and also by His miracle-working, so that marriage may be considered honorable among all people, whether they are joined in marriage [under] His care and help...

<summary>This page introduces the Christian marriage rite, explaining the divine origin of marriage through the Genesis creation story and the wedding at Cana. It emphasizes that marriage is an honorable state established and blessed by God himself.</summary>

<keywords>marriage rite, Adam and Eve, wedding at Cana, creation of woman, marital union, liturgical Greek</keywords>

# THEY INSTITUTE MARRIAGES

<margin>N 2</margin>

->...are accustomed to.<-

*Since many kinds of misfortunes and various afflictions present themselves to those joined in marriage because of sins, so that you also who are present, A and B, who desire your marriage to be confirmed before the Church of Christ in the name of God, may conceive a certain confidence in your hearts regarding God’s undoubted help in tribulations, hear and learn from the Holy Scriptures how honorable marriage is, being a divine condition pleasing to God. For this reason, God commands those who are wed to expect His blessing and protection, but adulterers and fornicators to expect His judgment.*

*First, therefore, know that God our Father, after He had finished the heaven, the earth, and all things that are in them, also created man in His own image and likeness, so that there would be one to rule over the earthly and aquatic creatures and the birds of the air. And when, after the sea was established, no helper like him was found among the various kinds of animals, the Lord God said: "It is not good for man to be alone; let us make for him a helper suitable for him." And the Lord God brought a weariness <note>original: "lassitudinem" — while modern Bibles say "deep sleep," this term suggests a heavy, divine slumber or trance</note> upon Adam: and while he slept, He took one of his ribs, and having filled its place with flesh, He built a woman from the rib which He had taken from Adam. When she was led to Adam, Adam said: "This now is bone of my bones, and flesh of my flesh. She shall be called Woman, because she was taken from her man." For this reason a man shall leave his father and his mother and shall cling to his wife; and those two shall be one flesh. Therefore, you shall by no means doubt that marriage is from God. For since God Himself both created the woman for Adam and, like a matron of honor <term>pronuba: in Roman tradition, this was a married woman who attended the bride and led her to the groom; the text uses this image to show God personally officiating the first wedding</term>, led and joined her to the man in that manner, He signifies by that mystery that even until now He leads each person’s spouse to them as if by His own hand. And the Lord Jesus deigned to honor marriage in Cana of Galilee both by His presence and moreover by a miraculous gift, so that marriage might be held honorable among all: and that those who are joined in marriage [might know they are under] His care and help...*

<meta>The catchword "indubita-" suggests the next page continues by discussing the "undoubted" (indubitatum) grace or support God provides to the couple.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the Rite of Marriage from page 184. The previous page established the biblical origin of marriage through the creation of Adam and Eve. This page details the three theological purposes of marriage and begins the specific instructions for the husband and wife regarding their duties toward one another.</meta>

...they may have unhesitating faith, even if at times not even the slightest grounds for hope should appear.

So that you may be able to lead this way of life with piety, you shall learn the reasons for which God ordained marital cohabitation. The first of these is so that the spouses may assist one another with all readiness and faithfulness in everything that is beneficial for both this temporary life and the eternal life. The second is so that, having received children as heirs, they may raise them in the true knowledge of God for the glory of God. The third is so that everyone may be able to avoid unchastity <note>original: "akolasia" — a lack of restraint or self-control</note> and all wicked desires, and live quietly with a good conscience. For because of sexual immorality, let each man have his own wife, and let each woman have her own husband. Therefore, all those who have passed the prime of youth and do not possess the gift of purity should, according to the ordinance of God and the institution of the Christian Church—with the knowledge and cooperation of their parents, guardians, or other relatives—take up the yoke of marital fellowship, lest the temple of God, which is our body, be defiled. For if anyone destroys the temple of God, God will destroy him.

Next, you shall also learn how you ought to behave toward one another according to the word of God. First, then, you, the husband: consider that you have been established by God as the head of the wife, so that, as far as it is possible for you, you may intelligently and wisely instruct, guide, comfort, and protect her, just as the head governs the body, and as Christ continues to be the head, wisdom, comfort, and helper of the Church. Furthermore, you shall love your wife as your own body, just as Christ loved the Church. You shall not behave toward her with bitterness, but you shall live with her according to knowledge, assigning honor to the wife as to the weaker vessel <note>original: "asthenestero skeuei" — a phrase from 1 Peter 3:7 suggesting the wife should be treated with care and respect due to her status as a co-heir in faith</note>, as being joint-heirs of the grace of life, so that your prayers may not be hindered. And since God commanded the man to eat his bread by the sweat of his face, it is your duty to labor with diligence and care in the calling to which you were called, so that with God's help you may be able to support your household in a proper manner and have something to share with those in need.

Conversely, you also, the wife, shall know in what manner you, according to the word of God, [ought to behave] toward your husband...

*...they should have unhesitating proof, even if at times not even the slightest grounds for expectation should appear.*

*In order that you may be able to lead this way of life with piety, you shall learn the causes for which God instituted the partnership of marriage. The first of these is that the spouses might help each other with all zeal and faithfulness in everything that leads to both this physical life and eternal life. The second is that, having obtained children and heirs, they may instruct them in the true knowledge of God for the glory of the divine name. The third is that each person may be able to avoid unchastity <note>original: "incontinentiam" — a lack of self-restraint or sexual self-control</note> and all wicked desires, and live quietly with a good conscience. For because of sexual immorality, let each*
<margin>1 Corinthians 7</margin>
*man have his own wife, and let each woman have her own husband.*
*Therefore, all who have reached adult age and do not have the gift of purity <note>original: "castitatis" — specifically the spiritual gift of being able to remain celibate without sin</note> are bound, according to God’s command and the ritual of the Christian Church—with the knowledge and help of parents or guardians and relatives—to undergo the yoke of marriage, so that the temple of God, which is our body, may not be defiled. For if anyone destroys the temple of God, God will destroy him.*

*Next, you shall also learn how you ought to behave toward one another according to the word of God. First, then, you, the husband, shall consider that you have been established by God as the head of your wife, so that, as far as it is possible, you may wisely and prudently instruct, guide, comfort, and protect her; just as, clearly, the head governs the body, and Christ, as the head of his Church, always provides wisdom, comfort, and help. Furthermore, you shall love your wife as your own body, just as Christ loved his Church. Do not show yourself harsh toward her, but live with her as is fitting for those with knowledge:*
<margin>1 Peter 3</margin>
*giving honor to the womanly vessel as to the weaker one, since they are also heirs together of the grace of life; lest your prayers be interrupted.*
<margin>Genesis 3</margin>

*In contrast, you, the woman, shall also know what kind of person you should be according to the word of God...*

...you shall provide to your husband. <meta>This page concludes the specific instructions addressed to the bride concerning her domestic and spiritual duties. It then transitions into the formal rite of marriage, where the "minister of the word" (the pastor) questions the couple's intent and makes a public call for any legal or moral impediments to the union before the vows are exchanged.</meta>

...you ought to provide to your husband. You shall love, honor, and fear the husband to whom you are married: and in all things consistent with fairness, you shall comply with him as your lord; as the body is subject to the head and the Church is subject to Christ, you shall not dominate your husband, but you shall be quiet. For Adam was created first, and then Eve was created as a helper to Adam. And after the Fall, God enjoined upon Eve—and in the person of Eve, upon the whole female sex: "You shall be under the power of your husband and he himself shall rule over you." <note>original: "Sub potestate viri eris & ipse tui dominabitur" — This is a Latin translation of Genesis 3:16, which was historically used to define the legal and social status of married women.</note> Therefore, you shall not oppose this divine constitution, but rather you shall obey the command of God and you shall imitate the holy matrons who, placing their hope in God, submitted themselves to their husbands, as Sarah obeyed Abraham, calling him "Lord." <note>A reference to 1 Peter 3:6.</note> Furthermore, in all things honorable and useful, you shall show yourself as a helper to your husband: you shall take diligent care of the children and domestic matters; you shall walk in all modesty and integrity, without worldly display, so that you may offer a praiseworthy example of modesty to others.

Since, therefore, you, **A**, and you, **B**, have learned both how marriage was instituted by God and what commands were given to you by God regarding it: have you resolved to lead such a life in marriage, as you testify here before the Church, and do you desire this your union to be confirmed? They respond, **Yes**.

After this, the <term>minister of the word</term> <note>original: "minister verbi" — A common title in Reformation-era liturgies for a pastor or priest, emphasizing their role in preaching the Gospel.</note> adds:

Upon these matters, I take all of you, as many as are present here, as witnesses. Nevertheless, if any of you knows that either of these persons is bound by another matrimonial promise, or that there is any other impediment to this marriage, let him declare it now in this presence.

At this point, if there is no one who objects, the minister of the word adds: Since therefore no one objects, nor does any impediment intervene, may the Lord God confirm your holy undertaking, which He has inspired in your hearts: and may your beginning be in the name of the Lord, who made heaven and earth.

...the yoke you have taken and do take <note>original: "ζυγὸν εἵλές τε καὶ αἱρεῖς" — the "yoke" refers to the bond of marriage</note> upon yourself, promising never to leave her, but to love and support her, just as a faithful and pious man is duty-bound toward his own wife. After these things, addressing the bride as well, he says: And you, **B**, do you confess within yourself before God and his holy church, that you have chosen and do choose **A** as your husband, promising to be obedient to him, to serve and obey, and to live holily with him, and never to leave him, but to maintain faithfulness toward him in all things, as a faithful, noble, and good wife is duty-bound to act toward her own husband, and according to the word of God and his holy Gospel? She answers, **Yes.**

Upon these things the minister <note>original: "ἐκκλησιαστής" (ekklēsiastēs) — literally "one who speaks in the assembly," here referring to the officiating pastor or priest</note>, having joined their right hands, adds: May the Father of mercies, who by his own good pleasure has called you into the holy order <term>order: "τάξις" (taxis) — referring to a divinely ordained arrangement or station in life</term> of marriage, truly love you and bind you together in faith, and may he continue to bless you with his blessing. **Amen.**

Hear now from the holy Gospel how strong the bond of marriage is. Matthew makes this clear in the nineteenth chapter of his Gospel, writing as follows: > And the Pharisees came to him, testing him and saying to him: "Is it lawful for a man to divorce his wife for any cause?" But he answered and said to them: "Have you not read that he who made them from the beginning made them male and female?" And he said: "For this reason a man shall leave his father and mother and shall be joined to his wife; and the two shall be one flesh. So they are no longer two, but one flesh. Therefore, what God has joined together, let no man separate." They said to him: "Why then did Moses command to give a certificate of divorce and to send her away?" He said to them: "Because Moses, in view of the hardness of your hearts, permitted you to divorce your wives; but from the beginning it was not so. And I say to you: whoever divorces his wife, except for sexual immorality <note>original: "πορνείαι" (porneia) — a term for sexual misconduct outside the marriage bond</note>, and marries another, commits adultery; and he who marries a divorced woman commits adultery."

Hear now from the holy Gospel how firm the bond of marriage is. Matthew declares this in the nineteenth chapter of his Gospel, writing thus: Then the Pharisees approached him, testing him, and saying to him: Is it lawful for a man to divorce his wife for any reason? And said, For this reason a man shall leave father and mother, and shall be joined <note>original: "agglutinabitur" — literally "to be glued or cemented to," emphasizing the permanence of the bond</note> to his wife: and those two shall be one flesh. And I say to you, whoever divorces his wife, except for sexual immorality <note>original: "scortationem" — generally referring to unchastity or fornication</note>, and marries another, commits adultery <term>commits adultery: "moechari" — to break the marriage vow through infidelity</term>: and he who marries her who is divorced commits adultery.

Believe the words of the Lord, doubting not at all that it is God who has joined you together; and because of this, accept with patience and thanksgiving everything that is to happen to you in this union <note>original: "συζυγίᾳ" — literally a "yoking together," referring to the bond of marriage</note>, as things coming from God who joined you. Amen.

*After this, the minister <note>original: "ἐκκλησιαστὴς" — literally "the assemblyman," used here for the presiding clergyman</note>, having commanded the newlyweds to kneel, adds: Let us pray to God.*

Lord Almighty, who demonstrates your goodness and wisdom in all your creations and decrees, and who from the beginning declared <note>original: "ἀποθεσπισάμενος" — to decree or speak as an oracle</note> that it is not good for man to be alone, and for this reason created for him a helper corresponding to him <term>helper: "βοηθὸν αὐτῷ κατ' αὐτὸν" — the Greek Septuagint's phrasing for the "helpmeet" in Genesis 2:18</term>, and decreed that two should be joined into one flesh, punishing all unchastity <note>original: "ἀκολασίαν" — lack of restraint or licentiousness</note> in these matters: since you have called and settled these prospective spouses into a marital household, grant them your Holy Spirit, so that they may continue living holily in true and unshakable faith, resisting every evil and doing your divine will. 

Bless them just as you continuously blessed the fathers of the faithful, your beloved and faithful servants Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob; so that, as fellow-heirs of the covenant established long ago with these patriarchs, they may bear holy children and raise them piously, for the glory of your holy name, the benefit of their neighbor, and the progress of your holy Gospel. Hear our prayer, O Father of mercies, through Jesus Christ, your beloved Son, our Lord. Our Father...

# CONCERNING THE VISITATION
->of the sick.<-

It is the work of a true and faithful <term>minister: "ecclesiastes" — literally a preacher or one who assembles the church</term>, not only to teach the people publicly and to shepherd them as is fitting, but also, as much as possible, to advise each individual privately and to provide benefit through counsel, warnings, and comforts. For never does a stronger need for the God-revealed teaching of salvation and comfort seize men than when they are tested by misfortunes—namely, by diseases or life-altering hardships. Especially under the weight of such necessity, a man is more distressed by his own conscience than at any other time in his life, because he feels himself being called to the judgment seat of God. Furthermore, the devil then especially persists in attacking him with the most violent temptations, so that having completely cast down the wretched soul—worn out by disease and despondency <note>original: "δυθυμίᾳ"</note>—he might hurl it into the abyss of despair. Since, therefore, the ministers of the church are servants of God, and God—having revealed His majesty through His other titles—is especially praised as the refuge of the wretched and the Physician of those with broken hearts; it is fitting that the heralds of God, sympathizing with sick souls, should comfort them with all diligence and zeal, and through the preaching of the Gospel, direct them to the Son of God; for this [Gospel] promises help to them—

# CONCERNING THE VISITATION
## of the Sick.

It is the duty of a faithful <term>minister: "antistitis" — literally an overseer or one who stands before a congregation</term> of the church not only to teach the people publicly, over whom he is placed as a pastor, but also, as far as possible, to privately exhort, rebuke, or comfort individuals. Indeed, a man never stands in greater need of divine teaching and saving comfort than when he is tested by adversity—specifically by illnesses or other such hardships—and above all by the inevitable necessity of death. For then his conscience is more distressed than at any other time in his life, because he feels himself being summoned to God's judgment. 

Furthermore, it is then that the devil especially attacks him with the most violent temptations, in order to utterly cast down the wretched soul—already struggling with disease and sorrow—and hurl it into the <term>abyss: "barathrum" — a deep pit or void</term> of despair. Since, therefore, the ministers of the Church are the servants of God—and God, among the other titles by which His majesty is signified, is especially celebrated as the refuge of the wretched and the healer of broken hearts—it is fitting that the heralds of God, sharing a sense of compassion for sick souls, should comfort them with all zeal and care and direct them to the Son of God through the preaching of the Gospel. For that help...

<meta>catchword: force and</meta>

...promised). "Come to me," (He says), "all you who labor and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest." <note>A quotation of Jesus from Matthew 11:28.</note> Although those who are suffering misfortune or illness do not all have the same afflictions—which makes it impossible to write a single form of consolation that fits every possible suffering and circumstance—nevertheless, the following main points and teachings should generally be set before all who are sick.

First, that all illnesses do not happen by chance, but are sent to us by the hand of God <note>The original uses the Greek abbreviation Θ̅υ̅ for "Theou" (of God).</note> and by His fatherly providence; this is so that we, recognizing our sins as the cause of all our misery, might humble ourselves before God. If, therefore, those who are sick do not yet have a deep sense of their sin, the <term>minister: "ἐκκλησιαστής" — one who leads or speaks to the assembly</term> should make the cause of the disease more broadly and clearly an example to them. 

But if the sufferer happens to be distressed by the weight of their own conscience, the minister should not wound the anxious conscience with a heavier blow; rather, he should much more diligently instill the saving <term>grace: "χάριν" — God's unmerited favor and love</term> of God into the weak person. To this end, the first question of the <term>catechism: "κατήχησις" — a summary of religious doctrine used for instruction</term> will be of no small use. By citing passages from Holy Scripture, it can be more clearly declared and shown to the sufferer that their body—even as it lies prostrate in illness—belongs to the Lord Jesus together with their soul. Having been ransomed by His precious blood, they are freed from all sin, and so forth. 

So that the sufferer may receive fuller comfort, the minister, running through the points of the Christian <term>Creed: "Συμβόλου" — the formal statement of faith, such as the Apostles' Creed</term>, should complete them one by one, teaching what specific comfort the sick person can receive from each article of faith. This will be easy to do by using both the Catechism and the passages of Scripture noted within it.

Sometimes, and especially during the first visit to the sufferer, the following exhortation can be made in these or similar words: "Beloved, since the Lord God has visited you with an infirmity of the body, so that you may submit to this by the good pleasure of God, you should know, first, why bodily infirmities are sent to us by God because of our sins..."

A quotation from Matthew 11:28, a central text in Protestant liturgy and pastoral care.</note> Furthermore, even if those who are tried by sorrow or illness do not all experience the same afflictions—and therefore a single consolation cannot be prescribed that fits every circumstance of every person's affliction in general—nevertheless, the following main points and lessons ought generally to be presented to all sick persons.

*First, that all diseases are sent to us not by chance of fortune, but by the hand of God and His fatherly providence;* <term>providence: the belief in God's ongoing, purposeful involvement in the world and individual lives</term> *so that, having recognized sin as the origin of all our misery, we might humble ourselves before God. If, therefore, the sick are not yet affected by a sufficiently deep sense of sorrow, the <term>minister of the word: a title for a pastor or preacher</term> shall place this cause of the disease more broadly and clearly before their eyes.* <note>In the early modern period, illness was often interpreted as a divine "visitation" intended to lead the soul to repentance.</note> *But if the sick person is pressed by the weight of conscience, the minister of the word shall not wound the anxious conscience with a heavier blow; but rather, with all care and diligence, he shall instill the saving grace of God into the sick person. ...the innate sin <note>original: "τη σύμφυτον ἁμαρτίαν" — referring to the concept of original sin inherited from the first man.</note>, which was passed down to us from Adam as if by an inherited lot. This sin brings with it death and everything that contributes to the dominion of death, such as physical defects, diseases, hardships, and miseries. Indeed, if we had continued to live without his sin, neither death nor any disease would have ever mastered us. 

But since we are subject to these things, the holy Gospel teaches us—lest we grow weary in our sins, in diseases, or other trials, and finally in the very necessities of death—that Jesus Christ, the Son of God, delivers us from our sins and deems us worthy of <term>salvation: the deliverance from sin and its consequences</term>, provided that we believe in His promises.

This happens primarily in two ways. First, through the holy Gospel and the sacred <term>mysteries: a term often used for the sacraments, such as the Lord's Supper</term>, He cleanses our hearts and consciences; as the Apostle says in the Acts of the Apostles, chapter 15: 
> by faith He cleansed their hearts. 

After this, once our consciences have been purified in this way from the filth of sin and have been reconciled to God the Father through faith, it is necessary that sins also be purged and eliminated from our nature and essence. Finally, having been cleansed from all sins, we are to be perfected daily in true righteousness and purity—which God requires of us—so that we may attain eternal life with God. 

Thirdly, so that this may be accomplished in us, the Lord God permits diseases to come upon us, and finally death itself. He does this not out of anger toward us, nor intending to destroy us, but treating us with great grace. His goal is to guide us toward true <term>repentance: a sincere turning away from sin toward God</term> and faith in this life, so that He might finally free us—both physically and spiritually—from the sin in which we are still held and from every calamity. 

This is clearly taught by the testimonies of Holy Scripture, such as the blessed Paul writing in 1 Corinthians, chapter 11: 
> But when we are judged, we are disciplined by the Lord, so that we may not be condemned with the world.

...and original sin, which was propagated to us from *Adam* as if by a certain hereditary succession, brings with it death and whatever pertains to the kingdom of death—such as want, diseases, miseries, and hardships. For if we lived without sin, clearly neither death nor any disease would rule over us. Next, lest we lose heart in our sins, in diseases, or other temptations, and finally in the very necessity of death itself, the holy Gospel teaches us that *Jesus Christ*, the Son of *God*, both frees us from sins and bestows <term>salvation: the gift of eternal life and deliverance from the spiritual consequences of sin</term> upon us, if His promises are embraced with true faith.

This happens primarily in two ways. First, through the holy Gospel and the holy <term>sacraments: the sacred rites of the church, such as Baptism and the Lord's Supper</term>, He cleanses our hearts and consciences: as the apostle says in the 15th chapter of the Acts of the Apostles: 
> <note>original: "Fide purgatis cordibus eorum"</note> Having cleansed their hearts by faith. 

Next, when our consciences have been cleansed in this manner from the filth of sin and reconciled to God the Father through faith, it is necessary for sins to also be purged and rooted out from our nature and substance; and finally, once we are purified from all sins, we are to be perfected more and more every day in true righteousness and purity—which God demands of us—so that we may attain eternal life with God. 

Thirdly, so that this may be accomplished in us, the Lord God sends diseases upon us, and finally death itself: not because He is angry with us, nor intending to destroy us, but pursuing us with great grace; so that by leading us as if by the hand to true repentance and faith in this life, He may finally free us—both physically and spiritually—from the sin to which we are still liable and from every calamity. This is clearly declared by other testimonies of Holy Scripture, and specifically by the blessed *Paul* in the 11th chapter of his first letter to the *Corinthians*, where he writes: 
> <note>original: "Sed cum punimur, à Domino erudimur, ne cum mundo condemnemur"</note> But when we are punished, we are disciplined by the Lord, so that we may not be condemned with the world.

...all things work together for good, and nothing is able to separate them from the love of God, which is in Christ Jesus: neither fire, nor sword, nor famine, nor death, nor life, nor anything else. 

The fourth point is that, since these things are so, you possess a full assurance from the holy Gospel—which was preached by the mouth of the Lord Jesus himself and confirmed by his own death and resurrection—that all your sins have been transferred from you onto Christ. For they have been completely cast away from Christ and wiped out for all eternity; thus, no cause for anger or condemnation remains before God against those who believe. Instead, we are to expect only <term>grace: God’s unmerited favor and love</term>, comfort, life, and <term>salvation: deliverance from sin and its consequences</term>. 

Therefore, you must keep this in mind: you stand in the eyes of God not as a wicked and condemned sinner, a branch planted from *Adam*, but as a perfectly righteous and holy person, a beloved child of God in Christ. For in this righteousness and life—if indeed you believe—you are truly to possess life and salvation, just as Christ himself endured the wrath of God and died, not for his own sins, but for yours. 

Attend, therefore, to this grace and these divine comforts. Know this: you are no longer subject to sin, to God’s judgment, or to death and <term>Hades: the realm of the dead or the power of the grave</term>; rather, Christ, the <term>Lamb of God: a title for Jesus representing his role as a sacrifice for human sin</term>, carries them. As it says in the first chapter of John <note>original: Ἰωάννου τῷ ά κεφαλαίῳ</note>: “For this is the one who takes them away” <note>John 1:29</note>. He did not merely take them up and endure them, but he himself, through his own power, destroyed them and made them vanish forever. 

Therefore, through him and in this your Lord Jesus Christ, you shall expect every kind of grace, comfort, and salvation from God the Father. In this confidence, committing everything to his fatherly will, you shall say: 
> “The Lord is my light and my savior; whom shall I fear? The Lord is the defender of my life; of whom shall I be afraid?” <note>Psalm 27:1</note>
> “Your will, O heavenly Father, be done; into your hands I commit my spirit. Amen.”
