It is also fitting to visit the sick even when uninvited <note>original: ἄκλητον (aklēton)</note>, so that this visit may bear some fruit. If the weight of the illness does not hinder their ability to hear, it will be necessary to read to the sufferer some chapter of the **Holy Scripture** <note>original: ἁγία γραφή (hagia graphē)</note> from among those that contribute to the <term>comfort: παρηγορία (parēgoria), meaning consolation or encouragement for the soul</term> of a suffering soul. Such passages are found in:
*   Chapters 15, 16, and 17 of the Gospel according to **John**
*   Chapter 15 of the Gospel according to **Luke**
*   Chapters 5, 8, and 15 of the Epistle to the **Romans**
*   Chapter 15 of the **First Epistle to the Corinthians**
*   Chapters 4 and 5 of the **Second Epistle to the Corinthians**
*   Chapter 12 of the Epistle to the **Hebrews**
*   The Book of **Isaiah**
*   The **Psalms of David**, specifically 25, 51, and 103 <note>The Greek text lists these as κε' (25), να' (51), and ργ' (103)</note>

Or whatever other such life-giving and soul-tending passages one might find suitable for the comfort of the sick.

Furthermore, the <term>minister: ἐκκλησιαστὴς (ekklēsiastēs), referring to a church leader or preacher</term>, along with those present, shall offer prayers on behalf of the sick person whenever circumstances allow, in the following or some other similar manner.

# PRAYER FOR THE
## sick.

> **E**ternal and most merciful God, Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, you who govern <note>original: οἰκονομῶν (oikonomōn), referring to divine management or "stewardship" of the universe</note> both life and death with your hand, and who provides for our benefit in such a way that neither health, nor sickness, nor any other thing—whether it be considered good or evil—befalls us by chance; indeed, not even a single hair falls from our head apart from your fatherly will. You govern all things that happen to us in this life so that everything is perfected for our <term>salvation: σωτηρία (sōtēria), the healing and deliverance of the person</term>. 
>
> You are the one who said: *Call upon me in the day of your affliction, and I will deliver you, and you shall glorify me.* And: *Because he has hoped in me, I will rescue him; I will protect him because he has known my name. He shall cry out to me, and I will hear him; I am with him in affliction; I will deliver him and glorify him; with length of days I will satisfy him, and I will show him my salvation.* 
>
> We beseech you, O Father: since it now pleases you to exercise us sometimes with illnesses and at other times with other sufferings, grant—

...quently, even when not called, he ought to visit the sick. So that this visitation might bear some fruit, if the severity of the illness does not prevent the patient’s ability to hear, he should read to the sick person a chapter of **Holy Scripture** from among those that offer <term>consolation: the act of providing comfort or solace in distress</term> to a suffering soul. Such passages include: 

*   Chapters 14, 15, 16, and 17 of the Gospel according to **John**. 
*   Chapter 15 of the Gospel according to **Luke**. 
*   Chapters 5 and 8 of the Epistle to the **Romans**. 
*   Chapter 15 of the **First Epistle to the Corinthians**. 
*   Chapters 4 and 5 of the **Second Epistle to the Corinthians**. 
*   In addition to these, **Isaiah 53**, and the **Psalms of David** 26, 51, and 103, along with any other similar passages a diligent <term>curator of souls: animarum curator, a traditional term for a pastor or priest responsible for the spiritual care of a congregation</term> finds suitable for comforting the sick.

Furthermore, the minister of the Church, along with those who are present, shall offer prayers with the sick person whenever it is convenient, using the following form or another similar to it.

# PRAYER FOR THE
## sick.

> Eternal, merciful God, Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, who regulates both life and death with your hands, and who unceasingly provides for us in such a way that neither prosperity, nor ill health, nor any other good or evil thing whatsoever happens to us—indeed, not even a single hair falls from our head—without your fatherly will. You govern all things that happen to us in this life so that they may all work together for our salvation. You have said: **Call upon me in the day of your trouble; I will deliver you, and you shall glorify me.** <note>original: "invoca me in die tribulationis tuæ: & eruam te & glorificabis me" — a quote from Psalm 50:15</note> And again: **Because he hoped in me, I will deliver him; I will protect him, because he has known my name. He shall cry to me, and I will hear him; I am with him in trouble. I will rescue him and glorify him. With length of days I will satisfy him, and show him my salvation.** <note>original: "in me speravit, & liberabo eum..." — a quote from Psalm 91:14-16</note> We ask you, Father, since it pleases you to test us now with illnesses and now with other adversities...

...grant us also the gifts of your Holy Spirit, so that first, through your fatherly discipline <note>original: *σωφρονισμοῦ*, referring to a process of bringing someone to their senses or moderate behavior through correction</note>, we may truly recognize that by our manifold sins we have drawn upon ourselves a just punishment, and are worthy of yet much more severe penalties. 

Next, let us hold fast in our hearts to that life-giving consolation <note>original: *παραμυθίαν*</note>: that this your fatherly visitation is a sign not of your anger, but of your fatherly kindness toward us. By this judgment you surely discipline us, so that we might not be condemned with the world; rather, through this exercise <note>original: *γυμνασίᾳ*, the root of 'gymnastics,' implying spiritual training</note>, let true repentance, faith, obedience, and the calling upon your salvation be increased in us. May we always be drawn more toward you in your beloved Son Jesus Christ, so that as members of him who is the Head, just as we share in his sufferings, we might also be conformed to him in glory. 

Give us therefore, O heavenly Father, a steadfast confidence in your all-merciful compassion; and as a sign of your mercy, grant us a brief and gentle relief from the weight of the cross <note>The 'cross' here refers to the burden of the illness being suffered by the patient</note> which has been laid upon us by your fatherly hand. According to your good pleasure, direct it toward the glory of your holy name and the salvation of our souls, through your beloved Son our Lord Jesus Christ, who decreed this promise to us: 

> “I say to you, that if two of you agree on earth about anything they ask, it will be done for them by my Father in heaven; for where two or three are gathered in my name, there am I in the midst of them.” 

And trusting in this promise, he commanded us to pray to you thus: 

**Our Father.**

In addition to these things, grant us, O Father, steadfastness and progress in the true and undisputed universal <term>catholic: here used in its original sense of 'universal' or 'whole,' referring to the shared Christian faith</term> Christian faith, which we affirm by confessing: 

**I believe in God.**

# PRAYER FOR THE DYING
<note>original Greek: ΠΡΟΣΕΥΧΗ ΠΑΡΑ ΤΟΙΣ θνήσκοσι</note>

Lord Almighty <note>original: "παντοκράτορ" (Pantokratōr), a title emphasizing God's absolute sovereignty over all creation</note>, Father of mercies, who holds both death and life in your hands, and who always provides for us, your children, both in living and in dying; you delivered your only-begotten Son unto death for our sake, so that through him we might obtain eternal life. Through him, you promised us that whoever believes in him has eternal life and does not come into judgment, but has passed from death into life; and that no one can snatch his sheep from your hand or his. Do not allow us, O Father, according to your promise, to be tempted beyond our strength, but with the temptation also provide the way out, so that we may be able to endure it. 

And especially when, according to your fatherly will, you are pleased to transfer us from these valleys of weeping <note>original: "κλαυθμῶν αὐλῶν"; a poetic reference to the "valley of tears," signifying the hardships of earthly life</note> into your eternal kingdom, then graciously lighten and remove from us the pains and the fear of physical death, and save us from every temptation. By the power of your Spirit, strengthen the pleading weakness of our flesh <note>original: "δυσωπίαν," referring to the shrinking or shame of the flesh in the face of death</note> with a steady confidence in the mercies you have granted us in Christ. With a fervent calling upon your name and a tireless stillness of heart, receive our spirit into your hands, so that we may glorify you in eternal life together with all the angels and the chosen, through our Lord Jesus Christ, who taught us to pray thus:

->Our Father.<-

Furthermore, O Father, grant us constancy and progress in the true and undoubted universal <term>catholic<gloss>In this historical context, "catholic" refers to the "universal" or "whole" Christian church across time and space, rather than specifically the Roman Catholic denomination.</gloss></term> Christian faith, in which we confess, saying: I believe in God.

# PRAYER IN THE PRESENCE OF THE DYING.
<note>original Latin: "Precatio apud morientes." This prayer was intended to be read by a minister or loved one at the bedside of a person nearing the moment of death.</note>

*Almighty God, merciful Father, who governs both death and life with your hands, and who perpetually provides for us, your children, in both life and death: you who delivered your only-begotten Son unto death for our sake, so that through him we might behold life; you who through him promised us that he who believes in him has eternal life, and does not come into judgment but has passed from death into life; and that no one shall snatch his sheep from your hand or from his. Do not allow us, O kind Father, according to your promise, to be tempted beyond our strength, but with the temptation grant also a way of escape, so that we may be able to endure it. Especially, however, when it shall please your fatherly will to lead us across from this valley of sorrows <note>original: "aerumnarum valle"; a common liturgical expression for the hardships of the mortal world</note> into your eternal kingdom, then—the pains and the fear of physical death having been mercifully softened and removed—preserve us from every temptation. With the strength of your Spirit having confirmed the timidity of our flesh, in a steady confidence in your mercy, which you have shown to us in Christ, and in a fervent calling upon your name and a peaceful tranquility of heart, receive our spirit into your hands: so that we may celebrate you in eternal life with all your angels and your chosen ones; through our Lord Jesus Christ, who taught us thus to pray to you:*

*Our Father... etc.*

*Grant us also, kind Father, both constancy and growth in the true, undoubting, and universal <note>original: "catholica"; here used in its original sense of "universal" or "whole," referring to the shared Christian faith of the ages</note> Christian faith; which we profess with these words:*

*I believe in God...*

<note>The text concludes with the beginning of the Apostles' Creed, signaling that the dying person and the witnesses would together recite the foundational beliefs of their faith.</note>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of historical liturgical forms. # THE WRITING OF THE OLD ASSEMBLY
### From the second apology <note>While the Greek text attributes this to the "Second Apology," this famous passage is actually found in Chapters 65–66 of Justin Martyr’s First Apology.</note> of Justin for the Christians
### To Antoninus the Pious <note>Titus Aurelius Fulvus Boionius Arrius Antoninus, the Roman Emperor from 138 to 161 AD.</note>

But we, after we have thus washed <note>That is, baptized.</note> the one who has been convinced and has given their assent, lead them to those who are called brothers where they are gathered together, to offer common prayers earnestly—both for ourselves and for the person who has been enlightened, and for all others everywhere; so that we may be deemed worthy, having learned the truth, to be found by our deeds as good citizens and keepers of the commandments, so that we may be saved with eternal salvation. Having ended the prayers, we salute one another with a kiss. Then there is brought to the president <note>original: "proestoti"; the one presiding over the brothers.</note> of the brothers bread and a cup of water and mixed wine <note>original: "kramatos"; wine mixed with water, which was the standard practice in the ancient Mediterranean.</note>. And he, taking them, sends up praise and glory to the Father of all things through the name of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, and offers thanks at length for our being deemed worthy of these things from Him. When he has concluded the prayers and the thanksgiving, all the people present shout their assent, saying "Amen." Now "Amen" in the Hebrew tongue signifies "So be it" <note>original: "Genoito".</note>. And when the president has given thanks and all the people have expressed their assent, those who are called among us Deacons <term>Deacons: From the Greek "diakonoi", meaning servants or ministers who assisted in liturgical and charitable duties.</term> give to each of those present to partake of the bread and wine and water over which thanks has been given <note>original: "eucharistēthentos"; literally "the eucharistized bread."</note>, and they carry it away to those who are not present.

And this food is called among us the Eucharist <term>Eucharist: From the Greek "eucharistia", meaning "thanksgiving."</term>, of which no one else is allowed to partake but the one who believes that the things which we teach are true, and who has been washed with the washing that is for the remission of sins and unto regeneration, and who is so living as Christ has enjoined. For we do not receive these as common bread or common drink; but in the same manner as Jesus Christ our Savior, having been made flesh by the Word of God, had both flesh and blood for our salvation, so likewise have we been taught that the food which is blessed by the prayer of His word, and from which our blood and flesh by transmutation are nourished, is the flesh and blood of that Jesus who was made flesh. For the apostles, in the memoirs composed by them, which are called Gospels, have thus delivered unto us what was enjoined upon them: that Jesus took bread, and when He had given thanks, said, "This..." <meta>catchword: "...is My body." (Greek: ποιεῖ- / poiei-, likely leading into "do this in remembrance of me").</meta>

<summary>A foundational 2nd-century account by Justin Martyr describing the early Christian rites of Baptism and the Eucharist, including the communal prayers and the theology of the Real Presence.</summary>
<keywords>Justin Martyr, Eucharist, Baptism, Liturgy, Deacons, Amen, Early Church Fathers, Sacraments</keywords>

<summary>This page features a Latin translation of Justin Martyr's First Apology, providing one of the most important early historical accounts of the Christian rites of Baptism and the Eucharist. <meta>This page completes the passage from Justin Martyr’s First Apology (Chapters 66–67). It concludes the description of the Eucharist and moves into the most famous early account of Sunday worship in the primitive Church. It connects directly to the previous page's description of the leader presiding over the bread and wine.</meta>

...the prayers and the thanksgiving, all the people present assent, saying Amen. This is done for the remembrance of His own body; and having likewise taken the cup and given thanks, He said, "This is my blood," and gave it to them alone. Which also the wicked demons have handed down to be performed in the mysteries of Mithras <note>Mithraism was a Roman mystery religion popular among soldiers. Justin argues that its rituals were demonic imitations of Christian ones, a concept called "diabolical mimicry."</note>, imitating [the Christian rite]. For you know, or can learn, that bread and a cup of water are placed in the initiation rites of those being initiated, accompanied by certain words.

But we, after these things, ever afterward remind one another of these matters; and those of us who have means assist all who are in want, and we are always together. For all things that we receive, we bless the Maker of all through His Son Jesus Christ and through the Holy Spirit.

And on the day called Sunday <note>original: "the day of the Sun."</note>, an assembly is held in one place of all who live in cities or in the country, and the memoirs of the apostles <note>Justin's term for the Gospels.</note> or the writings of the prophets are read, as long as time permits. Then, when the reader has ceased, the president <note>The leader of the assembly, often translated as "presiding brother" or "bishop."</note> verbally instructs and encourages the imitation of these good things. Then we all rise together and send up prayers, and, as we said before, when we have finished the prayer, bread is brought and wine and water. And the president likewise sends up prayers and thanksgivings, according to his ability, and the people assent, saying Amen.

And there is a distribution to each, and a participation of those things over which thanks have been given, and to those who are not present, a portion is sent by the deacons. And those who are well-to-do and willing give what each thinks fit according to his own choice; and what is collected is deposited with the president, who succors the orphans and widows and those who, through sickness or any other cause, are in want, and those who are in bonds and the strangers sojourning among us, and in a word, he becomes a guardian to all who are in need.

But Sunday is the day on which we all hold our common assembly, because it is the first day on which God, having wrought a change in the darkness and matter, made the world; and Jesus Christ our Savior on the same day rose from the dead. For He was crucified on the day before that of Saturn <note>Saturday.</note>; and on the day after that of Saturn, which is the day of the Sun, having appeared to His apostles and disciples, He taught them these things.

"Do this in remembrance of me: this is my body"; and likewise, having taken the cup and given thanks, He said, "This is my blood," and shared it with them alone. This, indeed, the evil demons have handed down to be imitated in the mysteries of Mithras <note>Mithraism was a Roman mystery religion popular among soldiers. Justin argues that its rituals were demonic imitations of Christian ones, a concept called "diabolical mimicry."</note>. And on the day called the day of the Sun <note>original: "solis die"; Sunday</note>, there is a gathering in one place of all who live in cities or in the countryside; and the memoirs of the apostles <note>Justin's term for the Gospels.</note> or the writings of the prophets are read, as long as time permits. Then, when the reading is finished, the president <note>The leader of the assembly, translated here from the Latin "Antistes."</note> provides a spoken instruction and exhortation to the imitation of these good things. Then we all rise together and send up prayers to God. And, as we said before, when we have finished praying, bread is brought, along with wine and water; and the president likewise sends up prayers and thanksgivings to heaven to the best of his ability, and the people give their assent by saying "Amen." 

And a distribution and sharing of those things over which thanks have been given is made to each person, and a portion is sent to those who are absent through the deacons. Then those who are wealthy and willing give what they wish, each according to his own choice; and what is collected is deposited with the president, and he provides for orphans and widows, and those who are in need due to sickness or any other cause, and those who are in bonds, and the strangers sojourning among us; in short, he makes himself a protector to all who are in need. 

But Sunday is the day on which we all hold our common assembly, because it is the first day on which God, having transformed the darkness and the raw, unformed matter, created the world; and Jesus Christ our Savior rose from the dead on the same day. For He was crucified on the day before the day of Saturn <note>original: "pridie saturnalis diei"; Friday</note>, and on the day after the day of Saturn <note>Saturday</note>, which is the day of the Sun <note>Sunday</note>, He appeared again to His apostles and disciples and taught them these things.

->THE END<-

<summary>This concluding page of Justin Martyr's First Apology provides a seminal historical account of early Christian liturgy, including the Eucharist, the reading of the Gospels, and the community's system of social welfare. It concludes by explaining that Christians worship on Sunday to celebrate both the beginning of Creation and the Resurrection of Jesus.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as an "Errata" or correction list for the printed volume. It follows the description of the early Christian liturgy by Justin Martyr. In early modern printing, it was common to include such a list at the end of a book to fix typographical errors made during the manual typesetting process. This specific list corrects both Latin and Greek terminology used in the preceding chapters.</meta>

## The errors shall be corrected thus.

*In the preface, read* blessing <note>original Greek: εὐμοιρίας (eumoirias)</note>, *and a little after,* may you be instructed <note>original Greek: παιδευθῆτε (paideuthēte)</note>. *Page 9, question 12, after these words,* and of grace <note>original Greek: καὶ χάριτος (kai charitos)</note>, *add* of souls <note>original Greek: ψυχῶν (psychōn)</note>. *In the same place, read* by necessity <note>original Greek: ἀνάγκῃ (anankē)</note>. *Page 15, question 22,* it is necessary to believe <note>original Greek: πιστεύει δεῖ (pisteuei dei)</note>. *Page 17, question 26,* even yet <note>original Greek: εἰσέτι (eiseti)</note>. *Page 21, question 30,* to obtain in it all... of the necessary things <note>original Greek: πάντων ἐν αὐτῷ τυγχάνειν... ἀναγκαίων (pantōn en autō tynchanein... anangkaiōn)</note>. *Page 23, question 31,* he meets with <note>original Greek: ἐντυγχάνει (entynchanei)</note>. *Page 26, question 36,* Which <note>original Latin: Quod</note>. *Page 43, question 66,* of the Gospel <note>original Greek: Εὐαγγελίου (Evangeliou)</note>. *Page 51, question 75,* remembrance <note>original Greek: ἀνάμνησιν (anamnēsin)</note>. *Page 53, question 77,* fellowship <note>original Greek: κοινωνία (koinōnia); often used for Holy Communion</note>. *Page 54, question 77, twice, after the word "take,"* Eat <note>original Latin: Comedite</note> *should be added.* *Page 67, question 92,* They guard <note>original Greek: Φυλάσσουσι (phylassousi)</note>. *Page 87, question 117,* to be present <note>original Greek: προσείναι (proseinai)</note>.

Furthermore, if there are errors anywhere in the improper placement of accents, or if any other faults <term>menda; a common Latin term for typographical errors in early printing</term> occur, we entrust those to your own diligence, kind reader <note>original: candide lector; a traditional polite address to the reader in early modern books</note>.

# **The Picture of Paradise** <note>original: "Paraucho no e." The word "Paraucho" is a localized Japanese rendering of the Latin *Paradisus* (Paradise). These adaptations were common in <term>Kirishitan</term> (early Japanese Christian) literature to express theological concepts that had no direct equivalent in existing Buddhist or Shinto vocabulary.</note>
